         One- and Two-Particle Spectral Gap Identities for the Symmetric Inclusion Process and Related Models

                                One- and Two-Particle Spectral Gap Identities for the Symmetric Inclusion Process and Related Models
                             
                            
                                 
                                    
         
             
                 
                    
                         Download PDF 
                         
                    
                 
             
         
    


                                    
                                 
                            
                         
                     
                

                 
                     
                         Seonwoo Kim 1  &  Federico Sau  
             ORCID:  orcid.org/0000-0003-3500-1401 2   
                        
    


                         
                            
        
         
            
                 
                     
                         
                     327  Accesses 
                 
            
            
            
            
            
                
                     
                         Explore all metrics 
                             
                         
                     
                
            
         
        
    
                         
                        
                        
    
    

    
    

                        
                     
                 

                 

                    
                     Abstract The symmetric inclusion process (SIP) models particles diffusing on a graph with mutual attraction. We recently showed [ KS24 ] that, in the log-concave regime (where diffusivity dominates interaction), the spectral gap of the conservative SIP matches that of a single particle. In this paper, our main result demonstrates that this identity generally fails outside this regime, but always holds for the non-conservative SIP, regardless of the interaction strength. When this one-particle spectral gap identity breaks down, we derive sharp bounds for the gap in terms of diffusivity, and reveal a two-particle spectral gap identity in the vanishing diffusivity limit. Our approach leverages the rigid eigenstructure of SIP, refined comparisons of Dirichlet forms for arbitrary diffusivity and particle numbers, and techniques from slow–fast system analysis. These findings extend to the dual interacting diffusion known as Brownian energy process, and shed some light on the spectral gap behavior for related Dirichlet-reversible systems on general, non-mean-field, geometries.

                                     1  Introduction The  symmetric inclusion process  ( \(\textrm{SIP}\) ) is a system of interacting particles hopping on the sites  \(x,y,z, \ldots \in V\)  of a finite graph  G . In its conservative version, each particle jumps from a site  x  to another one  y  at rate  \(c_{xy}\left( \alpha _y+\eta _y\right) \) , where: 
                 
                   \(c_{xy}=c_{yx}\ge 0\)  is a symmetric weight attached to the edge  xy ; 
                 
                 
                   \(\alpha _y> 0\)  is a weight attached to the arrival site  y ; 
                 
                 
                   \(\eta _y\in \mathbb {N}_0\)  denotes the number of particles sitting on  y . 
                 
               While the rate  \(c_{xy}\,\alpha _y\)  accounts for the free motion (or diffusion) of each particle,  \(c_{xy}\,\eta _y\)  introduces an interaction among them by favoring jumps on top of already occupied sites. Moreover, this dynamics is conservative (i.e., it preserves the total number of particles), reversible with respect to a discrete Dirichlet distribution, and irreducible as soon as the underlying graph is connected. We refer to Sect.  2  below for the detailed definition of the process and its properties. For now, let us just remark that  \(\textrm{SIP}\)  arises within different contexts (e.g., as a discrete model of heat conduction [ GKR07 ], and as a multi-type Moran model from population genetics with non-mean-field interactions), and comes with closely related models from statistical physics. For instance, by setting  \(\alpha _y=1\)  and replacing the plus sign with a minus sign in the rates, one obtains the renowned symmetric exclusion process [ Spi70 ]. Furthermore, SIP admits a continuous-spin counterpart, referred to as the Brownian energy process [ GKRV09 ] (see also Sect.  8.1  below). This continuous-spin model, roughly speaking, describes the energies of an instance of Kac’s walk [ Kac56 ], in which velocities evolve as interacting diffusions, rather than being instantaneously updated at random “collision” times. In all these models, a central question is that of determining precise convergence rates to equilibrium, and, in the context of reversible processes, one of the most investigated quantities for this purpose is the so-called  spectral gap . 1.1  One-particle spectral gap identity For  \(\textrm{SIP}\)  on a graph  \(G=(V,(c_{xy})_{x,y\in V})\)  endowed with site weights  \(\alpha =(\alpha _x)_{x\in V}\) , $$\begin{aligned} \textrm{gap}_\textrm{SIP}(G,\alpha )>0 \end{aligned}$$ denotes the corresponding spectral gap, i.e., the smallest non-zero eigenvalue of (the negative of) the infinitesimal generator (see Sect.  2  for the precise definition). In a previous work [ KS24 ], we established the following bounds on  \(\textrm{gap}_\textrm{SIP}(G,\alpha )\)  in terms of  \(\textrm{gap}_\textrm{RW}(G,\alpha )\) , the spectral gap of the random walk ( \(\textrm{RW}\) ) on  G  which jumps from  x  to  y  with rate  \(c_{xy}\,\alpha _y\) : for all graphs  G  and site weights  \(\alpha \) , $$\begin{aligned} \left( 1\wedge \alpha _\textrm{min}\right) \textrm{gap}_\textrm{RW}(G,\alpha )\le \textrm{gap}_\textrm{SIP}(G,\alpha )\le \textrm{gap}_\textrm{RW}(G,\alpha )\; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (1.1)
                 Here and all throughout,  \(a\wedge b:=\min \{a,b\}\)  and  \(\alpha _\textrm{min}:=\min _{x\in V}\alpha _x\) . Notably, as soon as  \(\alpha _\textrm{min}\ge 1\) , the inequalities in ( 1.1 ) saturate to an identity: $$\begin{aligned} \textrm{gap}_\textrm{SIP}(G,\alpha )=\textrm{gap}_\textrm{RW}(G,\alpha )\; \text {,}\;\;\quad \alpha _\textrm{min}\ge 1\; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (1.2)
                 This one-particle reduction corresponds to a  \(\textrm{SIP}\) -version of the celebrated Aldous’ spectral gap conjecture, originally formulated for interchange and symmetric exclusion processes in the early 90 s, and settled two decades later in [ CLR10 ]. In fact, the identity in ( 1.2 ) is extremely powerful, as it reduces, for every underlying graph  G , the spectral gap of  \(\textrm{SIP}\)  — an arbitrarily large system, as it may consist of arbitrarily many particles — to that of  \(\textrm{RW}\) , a much simpler Markov chain with finite state space  V , for which several techniques to bound the spectral gap in terms of simple features of the underlying graph  G  are known (see, e.g., [ SC97 ,  MT06 ,  LP17 ,  Her23 ]). For completeness, let us emphasize that an identity like ( 1.2 ), while trivial for a system of independent particles, is not at all expected to hold for truly interacting systems. Indeed, apart from the processes treated in [ CLR10 ], only a handful of other models were discovered to satisfy, on any geometry, a spectral gap identity involving the corresponding random walk. These models are: 
                   
                     the symmetric exclusion process in contact with reservoirs in [ Sal23 ,  Sal24 ]; 
                   
                   
                     the Binomial splitting process in [ QS23 ,  BC24 ]. 
                   
                 The identity in ( 1.2 ) adds  \(\textrm{SIP}\)  to the short list above, provided that  \(\alpha _\textrm{min}\ge 1\)  (we shall also refer to this condition as “log-concave regime”, see Remark  2.1  below). One of our main results (Theorem  2.4 ) states that, without this condition on  \(\alpha _\textrm{min}\) , this identity, in general, fails: for some graphs  G  and site weights  \(\alpha \) , $$\begin{aligned} \textrm{gap}_\textrm{SIP}(G,\alpha )<\textrm{gap}_\textrm{RW}(G,\alpha ) \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (1.3)
                 In other words, the factor  \(1\wedge \alpha _\textrm{min}\)  in ( 1.1 ) cannot be generally neglected when $$\begin{aligned} \alpha _\textrm{min}\in (0,1)\; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (1.4)
                 This naturally leads us to further investigate this regime, with the twofold goal of:  
                   
                     (a) 
                    
                       deriving an alternative lower bound for  \(\textrm{gap}_\textrm{SIP}(G,\alpha )\) , better capturing its dependence on  \(\alpha _\textrm{min}\) ; 
                    
                   
                   
                     (b) 
                    
                       obtaining a two-particle reduction of  \(\textrm{gap}_\textrm{SIP}(G,\alpha )\) , in the asymptotic regime of vanishing diffusivity  \(\alpha \rightarrow 0\) . 
                    
                   
                 We discuss these two steps in Sects.  1.2  and  1.3 , respectively. 1.2  Sharp dependence on  \(\alpha _\textrm{min}\) 
                         In order to isolate the role of  \(\alpha _\textrm{min}\) , let us introduce new site weights  \(\hat{\alpha }=\frac{\alpha }{\alpha _\textrm{min}}\) . With this notation, a simple scaling argument implies $$\begin{aligned} \textrm{gap}_\textrm{RW}(G,\alpha )=\alpha _\textrm{min}\,\textrm{gap}_\textrm{RW}(G,\hat{\alpha })\; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ Due to the particle interaction, the same argument does not directly apply to  \(\textrm{SIP}(G,\alpha )\) . Instead, if combined with the inequalities in ( 1.1 ), the above identity yields $$\begin{aligned} \alpha _\textrm{min}^2 \, \textrm{gap}_\textrm{RW}(G,\hat{\alpha })\le \textrm{gap}_\textrm{SIP}(G,\alpha )\le \alpha _\textrm{min}\, \textrm{gap}_\textrm{RW}(G,\hat{\alpha })\; \text {,}\;\;\quad \alpha _\textrm{min}\in (0,1)\; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (1.5)
                 Hence, if we encode the dependence on  \((G,\hat{\alpha })\)  through  \(\textrm{gap}_\textrm{RW}(G,\hat{\alpha })\) , the lower bound in ( 1.5 ) above would predict  \(\textrm{gap}_\textrm{SIP}(G,\alpha )\)  to depend on the square of  \(\alpha _\textrm{min}\) . Our second main result (Theorem  2.2 ) proves this guess to be wrong, showing that, in general,  \(\textrm{gap}_\textrm{SIP}(G,\alpha )\)  depends linearly, rather than quadratically as in ( 1.5 ), on  \(\alpha _\textrm{min}\in (0,1)\) : for all graphs  G  and site weights  \(\alpha \) , $$\begin{aligned} C\,\alpha _\textrm{min}\le \textrm{gap}_\textrm{SIP}(G,\alpha ) \; \text {,}\;\;\quad \alpha _\textrm{min}\in (0,1)\; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (1.6)
                 where  \(C> 0\)  is a constant depending only on  G  and  \(\hat{\alpha }=\frac{\alpha }{\alpha _\textrm{min}}\) . Indeed, in Theorem  2.2 , since  \(\alpha _\textrm{min} \le 1\)  and  \(\hat{\alpha }_\textrm{ratio} = \alpha _\textrm{ratio}\) , we have $$\begin{aligned} \alpha _\textrm{min}\left\{ \frac{1}{54} \, \frac{ \hat{\alpha }_\textrm{ratio}}{ (6e)^{1/\hat{\alpha }_\textrm{ratio}} } \, \frac{ c_\textrm{min}}{|V|^2\, \textrm{diam}(G) }\right\} \le \textrm{gap}_\textrm{SIP}(G,\alpha ) \; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ where the constant inside the bracket in the left-hand side depends only on the graph structure and the constant  \(\hat{\alpha }\) . Because of the second inequality in ( 1.5 ), the order-one dependence on  \(\alpha _\textrm{min}\)  for  \(\textrm{gap}_\textrm{SIP}(G,\alpha )\)  in ( 1.6 ) is sharp. As we will see, this sharpened result crucially requires to express the dependence on  G  not through  \(\textrm{gap}_\textrm{RW}(G,\hat{\alpha })\) , but other features of the underlying geometry. 1.3  Two-particle spectral gap identity All considerations made so far leave the following question unanswered: given that the spectral gap of  \(\textrm{SIP}\)  does not always coincide with that of a single particle (namely,  \(\textrm{RW}\) ), does it instead coincide with the spectral gap of the  k -particle  \(\textrm{SIP}\) , for some integer  \(k\ge 2\) , independent of the underlying graph  G  and site weights  \(\alpha \) ? Asking the same question for  \(\textrm{SIP}\) ’s “ instantaneously thermalized” variant also known as Beta-Binomial splitting process (see, e.g., [ PR26 ] or Sect.  8.2 )—Pietro Caputo conjectured (personal communication) this spectral gap reduction to hold true with  \(k=2\) . In words, the Beta-Binomial splitting dynamics consists of, first selecting an edge  xy  with rate  \(c_{xy}\)  (regardless of the particle configuration), and then letting particles sitting on  x  and  y  redistribute themselves according to the  \(\textrm{SIP}\) -equilibrium, restricted to that edge. Besides being clearly related to  \(\textrm{SIP}\) , the Beta-Binomial splitting process is the particle analogue of a model which shows up in the literature under various names, e.g., the Kipnis-Marchioro-Presutti model [ KMP82 ], a random walk on the simplex [ CLL20 ], or (the energies of) the renowned Kac’s walk [ Kac56 ,  CCL03 ]. Spectral gap estimates for this model are known only on two specific geometric settings: the complete graph [ CCL03 ,  Cap08 ], and the segment with  \(\alpha _\textrm{min}\ge 1\)  [ CLL20 ]. In both cases, a spectral gap identity with  \(k=2\)  is indeed verified. The aforementioned conjecture translates to the context of  \(\textrm{SIP}\)  as follows: for all graphs  G  and site weights  \(\alpha \) , $$\begin{aligned} \textrm{gap}_\textrm{SIP}(G,\alpha )=\textrm{gap}_2(G,\alpha )\; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (1.7)
                 where  \(\textrm{gap}_k(G,\alpha )\) ,  \(k\ge 1\) , stands for the spectral gap of the  k -particle  \(\textrm{SIP}\) . Clearly, we have  \(\textrm{gap}_1(G,\alpha )=\textrm{gap}_\textrm{RW}(G,\alpha )\)  and ( 1.7 ) equivalently reads as  \(\textrm{gap}_k(G,\alpha )=\textrm{gap}_2(G,\alpha )\) , for all  \(k\ge 2\)  (cf. ( 2.4 ) and ( 2.7 )). Moreover, in view of ( 1.2 ) and  \(\textrm{gap}_\textrm{SIP}(G,\alpha )\le \textrm{gap}_2(G,\alpha )\le \textrm{gap}_1(G,\alpha )\)  in ( 2.7 ), the two-particle spectral gap identity in ( 1.7 ) remains to be verified only off the log-concave regime, i.e., when ( 1.4 ) holds. We provide a partial answer in the affirmative to this conjecture, by determining spectral gap asymptotics as  \(\alpha \rightarrow 0\)  along directional limits (i.e., for  \(\varepsilon \rightarrow 0\)  and some  \(\hat{\alpha } \in (0,\infty )^V\) ,  \(\alpha =\varepsilon \hat{\alpha }\) ; see ( 2.8 )). The precise form of the asymptotic validity of ( 1.7 ) is the content of Theorem  2.3  below. We refer to Sect.  2.3  for more details, but let us briefly remark that our asymptotic analysis highlights the key role that the two-particle dynamics plays for both results in Theorems  2.3  and  2.4 . We believe this point of view to be fruitful also for other related interacting systems with Gamma-like reversible measures. For more details, see Sect.  8.2 . 1.4  Non-conservative case So far, we discussed only closed (or conservative) systems in which inclusion particles neither get created nor annihilated. Open (or non-conservative) systems allow for this possibility, usually modeled as systems in contact with reservoirs, see, e.g., [ Spo83 ,  DEHP93 ,  CGGR13 ]. For  \(\textrm{SIP}\) , the standard choice consists of creating a particle in  x  with rate  \(\omega _x\,\theta _x\left( \alpha _x+\eta _x\right) \) , and annihilating each particle therein (if any) with rate  \(\omega _x\left( 1+\theta _x\right) \) , where  \(\omega _x\)  and  \(\theta _x\)  form a set of non-negative site parameters added to the model. For precise definitions, see Sect.  6 . In essence, the open  \(\textrm{SIP}\)  presents two main features: first, due to the lack of particle conservation, the configuration space now consists of a unique, countably infinite, irreducible component; second, the unique ergodic measure is reversible if the  \(\theta \) -parameters do not depend on  x , and non-reversible if they do depend. This drastic change from reversibility to non-reversibility goes together with the emergence of a number of remarkable phenomena (e.g., currents, long-range correlations), which distinguish equilibrium from non-equilibrium statistical mechanics, see, e.g., [ Spo91 ,  DMP91 ,  KL99 ,  Sch01 ]. Nevertheless, as already observed in the physics literature around thirty years ago for a large class of exactly solvable models [ ADHR94 ] (see also [ FGK20 ]), the “ spectrum” does not depend on the  \(\theta \) -parameters, and, thus, is the same for both equilibrium and non-equilibrium systems. The quotation marks above are required when dealing with infinite configuration spaces and corresponding generators, because the full spectrum,  a priori , may include also non-eigenvalues and may depend on the underlying functional space on which the matrix/operators act. That said, this  \(\theta \) -independence of the spectrum becomes rigorous for systems having a finite configuration space as, for instance, the symmetric exclusion process in contact with reservoirs (see Remark  6.6  below for more details). In this article, we establish the following results for the non-conservative  \(\textrm{SIP}\) , for any underlying graph  G  and site weights  \(\alpha \) ,  \(\omega \) , and  \(\theta \) :  
                   
                     (i) 
                    
                       Eigenfunctions of the purely absorbing system (corresponding to  \(\theta \equiv 0\) ) “lift” to (generalized) eigenfunctions of  \(\textrm{SIP}\)  (with general  \(\theta \) ) associated to the same eigenvalues. 
                    
                   
                   
                     (ii) 
                    
                       Remarkably, the spectral gap of the particle system with  \(\theta \equiv 0\)  coincides with  $$\begin{aligned}\textrm{gap}_\textrm{RW}(G,\alpha ,\omega )>0\; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$  the spectral gap of the one-particle system, namely, of the random walk evolving on  G , and killed with rate  \(\omega _x\)  when sitting on  \(x\in V\)  (Theorem  6.1 ). 
                    
                   
                   
                     (iii) 
                    
                       In the reversible case (i.e., when  \(\theta \equiv \mathrm{const.}\) ), the eigenfunctions obtained in (i) provide an orthonormal basis in a natural  \(L^2\) -space. 
                    
                   
                 Let us emphasize that step (i) holds for general  \(\theta \)  (i.e., in both reversible and non-reversible settings), while step (ii) is a statement concerning the case  \(\theta \equiv 0\)  only; establishing the claim in (iii) is where we need to restrict to the reversible  \(\textrm{SIP}\) . Putting these steps together, our main result in this non-conservative setting (Corollary  6.4 ) may be summarized as follows: for all graphs  G  and site weights  \(\alpha \)  and  \(\omega \) , $$\begin{aligned} \textrm{gap}_\textrm{SIP}(G,\alpha ,\omega ,\varrho )=\textrm{gap}_\textrm{RW}(G,\alpha ,\omega )\; \text {,}\;\;\quad \text {whenever}\,\, \theta \equiv \varrho >0\; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ where  \(\textrm{gap}_\textrm{SIP}(G,\alpha ,\omega ,\varrho )\)  denotes the first gap in the spectrum of the reversible  \(\textrm{SIP}\)  with  \(\theta \equiv \varrho >0\) , compatibly with the functional setting of step (iii). When comparing the conservative and non-conservative settings, we remark a different behavior of  \(\textrm{SIP}\) ’s spectral gap: while a system of  two particles  sharply captures the spectral gap of the many-particle  closed   \(\textrm{SIP}\) , the spectral gap of the  open   \(\textrm{SIP}\)  is always governed (at least when  \(\theta \equiv \mathrm{const.}\) ) by  one random walk with killing  — with no constraint on the value of  \(\alpha _\textrm{min}>0\) . We believe this dichotomy to hold true not just for  \(\textrm{SIP}\) , but also for a larger class of interacting systems in statistical mechanics having Dirichlet-like steady states (see Sect.  8.2  for further details). 1.5  Organization of the paper The rest of the paper is organized as follows. Sections  2 – 5  concern the closed  \(\textrm{SIP}\) . More specifically, Sect.  2  contains the model definition, the main results (Theorems  2.2 – 2.4 ), and an outline of their proofs. In Sects.  3  and  4 , we present the proof of Theorem  2.2 ; in Sect.  5 , we prove the remaining two theorems from Sect.  2 . Sections  6 – 7  focus on  \(\textrm{SIP}\)  in contact with reservoirs, the first one of these sections detailing the model and main results, the second one presenting the proofs of the main results therein. Finally, in Sect.  8 , we briefly discuss some extensions of our results to discrete and continuous-spin models related to  \(\textrm{SIP}\) . In particular, in view of the isospectrality showed in [ KS24 ] between  \(\textrm{SIP}\)  and Brownian energy process, we derive spectral gap estimates for the latter. We conclude the paper with two appendices, in which we provide the full proofs of two technical ingredients employed in Sect.  5 . For the reader’s convenience, we add, at the end of the paper, a notation guide collecting the main recurrent symbols. 2  Conservative SIP. Setting and Main Results Unless stated otherwise, all throughout the article, our underlying geometry consists of a weighted finite graph  \(G=(V,(c_{xy})_{x,y\in V})\) , with symmetric conductances  \(c_{xy}=c_{yx}\ge 0\)  (conventionally,  \(c_{xx}=0\) ). We always assume the undirected graph  G  to be connected, i.e., for all  \(x, y \in V\) , there exists a sequence  \(x_0=x, x_1,\ldots , x_{\ell -1}, x_\ell =y\)  in  V  such that  \(\prod _{j=1}^\ell c_{x_{j-1}x_j}>0\) . The length  \(\ell \in \mathbb {N}\)  of the shortest sequence connecting two distinct sites  \(x,y\in V\)  corresponds to their graph distance,  \(\textrm{dist}_G(x,y)\) . As usual, we define the diameter,  \(\textrm{diam}(G)\in \{1,\ldots , |V|\}\) , as the largest of all pairwise distances. 2.1  Model For a graph  \(G=(V,(c_{xy})_{x,y\in V})\) , positive site weights  \(\alpha =(\alpha _x)_{x\in V}\) , and  \(k\in \mathbb {N}\) ,  \(\textrm{SIP}_k(G,\alpha )\)  denotes the Markov chain evolving on the configuration space $$\begin{aligned} \Xi _k:=\left\{ \eta \in \mathbb {N}_0^V: |\eta |=k\right\} \; \text {,}\;\;\quad \text {with}\,\, |\eta |:=\sum _{x\in V} \eta _x\; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ with infinitesimal generator given, for all  \(f\in \mathbb {R}^{\Xi _k}\) , as $$\begin{aligned} L_{G,\alpha ,k}f(\eta )=\sum _{x, y \in V} c_{xy}\, \eta _x\left( \alpha _y+\eta _y\right) \left( f(\eta -\delta _x+\delta _y)-f(\eta )\right) \; \text {,}\;\;\quad \eta \in \Xi _k\; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (2.1)
                 In this formula,  \( \eta -\delta _x+\delta _y\in \Xi _k\)  denotes the configuration obtained from  \(\eta \)  by removing a particle from  x  (if any) and placing it on  y . Since all particle configurations in  \(\Xi _k\)  are accessible (as the graph  G  is connected and the weights  \(\alpha =(\alpha _x)_{x\in V}\)  are all positive),  \(\textrm{SIP}_k(G,\alpha )\)  is irreducible. As a simple detailed balance computation shows,  \(\textrm{SIP}_k(G,\alpha )\)  admits a unique reversible measure  \(\mu _{\alpha ,k}\) , given, for all  \(\eta \in \Xi _k\) , by $$\begin{aligned} \mu _{\alpha ,k}(\eta )= \frac{1}{Z_{\alpha ,k}}\prod _{x\in V}\frac{\Gamma (\alpha _x+\eta _x)}{\Gamma (\alpha _x)\,\eta _x!}\; \text {,}\;\;\quad \text {with}\,\, Z_{\alpha ,k}:=\frac{\Gamma (|\alpha |+k)}{\Gamma (|\alpha |)\,k!}\; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (2.2)
                 Here,  \(\Gamma \)  denotes the usual gamma function satisfying, for all  \(a>0\) ,  \(\Gamma (a+1)=a\, \Gamma (a)\) . 
                   Remark 2.1 
                   (Log-concavity) The measure  \(\mu _{\alpha ,k}\)  in ( 2.2 ) is known as Dirichlet-Multinomial distribution of parameters  \(k\in \mathbb {N}\)  and  \(\alpha =(\alpha _x)_{x\in V}\) , and is the discrete analogue of the Dirichlet distribution on the simplex  \(\varSigma \subset \mathbb {R}^V\)  of probability measures, see ( 8.1 ) and ( 8.2 ) below. The Dirichlet distribution is well known to be log-concave (in the sense that its probability density is of the form  \(\exp (-U)\) , for some convex  \(U:\mathbb {R}^V\rightarrow \mathbb {R}\) ) if and only if  \(\alpha _\textrm{min}\ge 1\) . Hence,  \(\mu _{\alpha ,k}\)  is the discrete counterpart of a log-concave measure if and only if  \(\alpha _\textrm{min}\ge 1\) . More directly,  \(\mu _{\alpha ,k}\)  is a “discrete log-concave measure” also in the following sense:  \(\mu _{\alpha ,k}\)  is the canonical measure (i.e., conditional on  \(|\eta |=k\) ) of the product measure  \(\nu _{\alpha ,\varrho }\)  in ( 6.2 ) below, whose Negative-Binomial marginal measures on  \(\mathbb {N}_0\)  are log-concave (i.e.,  \(\nu _{\alpha ,\varrho }(\eta _x=\ell )^2\ge \nu _{\alpha ,\varrho }(\eta _x=\ell -1)\,\nu _{\alpha ,\varrho }(\eta _x=\ell +1)\) , for all  \(\ell \ge 1\)  and  \(x\in V\) ) if and only if  \(\alpha _\textrm{min}\ge 1\) . 
                 2.2  A non-asymptotic lower bound for the spectral gap Because of irreducibility and reversibility, all  \(|\Xi _k|\)  eigenvalues of the (negative) generator  \(-L_{G,\alpha ,k}\)  in ( 2.1 ) are real and non-negative, the smallest one being equal to zero, whereas the second one — referred to as spectral gap of  \(\textrm{SIP}_k(G,\alpha )\)  and shortened as  \(\textrm{gap}_k(G,\alpha )\)  — being strictly positive. Confronting with the notation from Sect.  1 , we have $$\begin{aligned} \textrm{gap}_\textrm{RW}(G,\alpha )=\textrm{gap}_1(G,\alpha )\; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (2.3)
                 because  \(\textrm{SIP}_k(G,\alpha )\)  with  \(k=1\)  corresponds to a single, thus, non-interacting particle, abbreviated as  \(\textrm{RW}(G,\alpha )\) . All cases  \(k\ge 2\)  describe a truly interacting system; hence, we define $$\begin{aligned} \textrm{gap}_\textrm{SIP}(G,\alpha )= \inf _{k\ge 2} \textrm{gap}_k(G,\alpha )\; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (2.4)
                 We are now ready to state the precise statement of the result in ( 1.6 ), concerned with the sharp dependence of  \(\textrm{gap}_\textrm{SIP}(G,\alpha )\)  on  \(\alpha _\textrm{min}=\min _{x\in V}\alpha _x\) , with a special focus on the regime  \(\alpha _\textrm{min}\in (0,1)\) . 
                   Theorem 2.2 
                   For all graphs  \(G=(V,(c_{xy})_{x,y\in V})\)  and site weights  \(\alpha =(\alpha _x)_{x\in V}\) , we have $$\begin{aligned} \alpha _\textrm{min}\left\{ \frac{1}{{54}} \, \frac{ \alpha _\textrm{ratio}}{{{(6e)}}^{\alpha _\textrm{min}/\alpha _\textrm{ratio}} } \, \frac{ c_\textrm{min}}{|V|^2\, \textrm{diam}(G) }\right\} \le \textrm{gap}_\textrm{SIP}(G,\alpha )\; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ where $$\begin{aligned} \alpha _\textrm{ratio}:=\frac{\alpha _\textrm{min}}{\alpha _\textrm{max}}= \frac{\min _{x\in V}\alpha _x}{\max _{y\in V}\alpha _y}\le 1\; \text {,}\;\;\quad c_\textrm{min}:=\min _{\begin{array}{c} x,y\in V\\ c_{xy}> 0 \end{array}}c_{xy}>0\; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (2.5)
                 
                 As already discussed in Sect.  1.2 , this lower bound captures the correct dependence of  \(\textrm{gap}_\textrm{SIP}(G,\alpha )\)  on  \(\alpha _\textrm{min}\)  for every fixed  G , provided that  \(\alpha _\textrm{ratio}\)  remains bounded away from zero. Moreover, the estimate is non-asymptotic, with a rather explicit constant depending only on simple geometric parameters of the graph, such as its size and diameter. We do not claim, however, that this geometric dependence is sharp. This is fundamentally due to the fact that the  \(L^2\)  inequalities (Lemmas  4.2 – 4.6 ) used in the proof of Theorem  2.2  are designed to maintain the sharp linear dependence on  \(\alpha _\textrm{min}\) , at the expense of suboptimality caused by exploiting the geometric structure. To illustrate this point, take  \(\alpha \equiv \varepsilon \in (0,1)\) , so that  \(\alpha _\textrm{min}=\varepsilon \)  and  \(\alpha _\textrm{ratio}=1\) . For  d -dimensional boxes or tori of side length  N , Theorem  2.2  gives $$\begin{aligned} \textrm{gap}_\textrm{SIP}(G,\varepsilon \textbf{1}) \ge C\,\varepsilon \,N^{-(2d+1)}\; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ for some  \(C=C(d,c_\textrm{min})>0\) . This is not expected to be the correct dependence on  N . Indeed, in view of Theorem  2.3  and of the proof of Theorem  2.4 , the relevant small- \(\varepsilon \)  scale should rather be $$\begin{aligned} \textrm{gap}_\textrm{SIP}(G,\varepsilon \textbf{1}) \asymp \varepsilon /s_d(N)\; \text {,}\;\;\quad s_d(N):={\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} N^{2} & \text {if}\quad d=1\\ N^{2}\log N & \text {if}\quad d=2\\ N^{d} & \text {if}\quad d\ge 3\; \text {.}\end{array}\right. } \end{aligned}$$ 
                    (2.6)
                 Thus, while Theorem  2.2  is sharp in its dependence on  \(\alpha _\textrm{min}\) , its dependence on the geometry is generally far from optimal. Rather than optimizing these geometric constants on specific families of graphs, we keep the underlying geometry arbitrary and pass to the regime of vanishing weights  \(\alpha \rightarrow 0\) . In this setting, we identify the relevant asymptotic object not in terms of the spectral gap of  \(\textrm{RW}(G,\alpha )\) , but of the “second simplest system” on the same geometry:  \(\textrm{SIP}_2(G,\alpha )\) , namely the symmetric inclusion process with just two particles. 2.3  Spectral gap’s asymptotics As already proved in [ KS24 ] (see also Sect.  3.1  below), we have, for all graphs  G  and site weights  \(\alpha \) , $$\begin{aligned} { \textrm{gap}_{k+1}(G,\alpha )\le \textrm{gap}_{k}(G,\alpha )\; \text {,}\;\;\quad k \ge 1\; \text {.}} \end{aligned}$$ 
                    (2.7)
                 The next result shows that, along directional limits  \(\alpha \rightarrow 0\) , i.e., $$\begin{aligned} \alpha =\varepsilon \hat{\alpha } \; \text {,}\;\;\quad \text {with}\,\, \varepsilon \rightarrow 0\,\, \text {and}\,\, \hat{\alpha } = (\hat{\alpha }_x)_{x\in V}\in (0,\infty )^V\ \text {fixed}\; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (2.8)
                 equality is achieved in ( 2.7 ) for  \(k\ge 2\) . This result is formulated along fixed positive directions; see Remark  5.15  below for the relation with general limits  \(\alpha \rightarrow 0\) . 
                   Theorem 2.3 
                   For all graphs  \(G=(V,(c_{xy})_{x,y\in V})\)  and site weights  \(\hat{\alpha } = (\hat{\alpha }_x)_{x\in V}\) , we have $$\begin{aligned} \lim _{\varepsilon \rightarrow 0} \frac{\textrm{gap}_k(G,\varepsilon \hat{\alpha })}{\textrm{gap}_2(G,\varepsilon \hat{\alpha })} =1\; \text {,}\;\;\quad {k\ge 2}\; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                 In view of the definition of  \(\textrm{gap}_\textrm{SIP}(G,\alpha )\)  in ( 2.4 ) and of the inequalities in ( 2.7 ), the above limit is an asymptotic version of the two-particle spectral gap identity in ( 1.7 ). Let us stress that this result holds true for any graph  G , positive site weights  \(\hat{\alpha }\) , and integer  \(k\ge 2\)  (trivially, for  \(k=2\) ). However, the case  \(k=1\)  is excluded for a good reason, as shown in the following result. 
                   Theorem 2.4 
                   For some graphs  \(G=(V,(c_{xy})_{x,y\in V})\)  and site weights  \(\hat{\alpha }=(\hat{\alpha }_x)_{x\in V}\) , we have $$\begin{aligned} \lim _{\varepsilon \rightarrow 0}\frac{\textrm{gap}_2(G,\varepsilon \hat{\alpha })}{\textrm{gap}_1(G,\varepsilon \hat{\alpha })}<1\; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (2.9)
                 
                 This inequality provides, if combined with ( 2.7 ) and the definitions in ( 2.3 )–( 2.4 ), an instance of ( 1.3 ), namely, of the failure of the one-particle spectral gap identity in ( 1.2 ). The graphs  G  and site weights  \(\hat{\alpha }\)  which we adopt to prove this result are far from being intricate. In fact, we take  G  to be the standard  d -dimensional discrete torus, with  \(d\ge 2\)  and sufficiently large size, and  \(\hat{\alpha } \equiv 1\) . These are just some of the possible examples that one could exhibit for proving ( 2.9 ). Indeed, as it will become apparent from the proof, essentially any other sparse geometry for which the random walk’s relaxation time (i.e.,  \(\textrm{gap}_\textrm{RW}(G,\alpha )^{-1}\) ) is much smaller than the expected meeting time of two independent random walks initialized at equilibrium, will equally work. We refer to the subsequent section for further explanations. 2.4  Proofs outline Recall that an inclusion particle jumps from  x  to a nearest neighbor  y  at rate proportional to  \(\alpha _y + \eta _y\) . On the one hand, when non-zero, the term  \(\eta _y\ge 1\)  stands for the interaction between particles on adjacent sites, which mutually attract each other, tending to stick together. On the other hand,  \(\alpha _y> 0\)  represents an independent mechanism of particle diffusion. In this sense, the regime  \(\alpha _\textrm{min}\in (0,1)\)  depicts the situation in which, at least in some portions of the graph, particle stickiness dominates over diffusion. This regime becomes particularly significant when considering the limit  \(\alpha \rightarrow 0\) . In this setting,  \(\textrm{SIP}(G,\alpha )\)  exhibits a metastable behavior, as thoroughly studied in the last decade [ GRV13 ,  BDG17 ,  KS21 ,  Kim21 ,  Kim25 ]. These works describe the following qualitative picture, when  \(\alpha =\varepsilon \hat{\alpha }\)  as in ( 2.8 ). Roughly speaking, as long as particles are far from each other (i.e., at graph distance  \(\ge 2\) ), they perform jumps on the timescale  \(\varepsilon ^{-1}\) . As soon as they are at distance one, they stick together in a much shorter time, roughly of order one. These two mechanisms bring particles to meet and pile up together in large stacks (or, condensates) in a time of order  \(\varepsilon ^{-1}\) . Concurrently, some particles sitting on a stack would still attempt to jump to empty nearest neighboring vertices at rate  \(\asymp \varepsilon \) . While some of the attempts fail, that is, the “courageous” particle is instantaneously sucked back into a neighboring stack, some of these will succeed to attract sufficiently many particles, managing to move the whole stack. Due to the rates’ symmetry encoded in the condition  \(c_{xy}=c_{yx}\) , it is then part of the results in [ GRV13 ,  BDG17 ] to show that relevant jumps of entire stacks occur at times of order  \(\varepsilon ^{-1}\) , and that, on this timescale, stacks are effectively approximated by independent random walks on  G , each evolving at rate proportional to  \(\varepsilon \) , and capable of coalescing (possibly in a very complicated way if three or more stacks are involved) when getting at distance one from each other. Hence, the metastable relevant part of the dynamics takes place, according to this qualitative picture, on the timescale  \(\varepsilon ^{-1}\) . Although this metastable picture provides a first correct intuition that  \(\textrm{gap}_\textrm{SIP}(G,\varepsilon \hat{\alpha }) \asymp \varepsilon \)  as  \(\varepsilon \rightarrow 0\) , our quantitative analysis must take into account also features not captured by usual metastability limit theorems. For instance, while metastability describes macroscopic features of the system when initialized from macroscopically relevant configurations, global functional inequalities as those determining spectral gaps require bounds which must be uniform over the initial conditions. Moreover, we seek for lower bounds for  \(\textrm{gap}_\textrm{SIP}(G,\varepsilon \hat{\alpha })\)  that are independent of the system size. However, a crucial ingredient in the metastability picture of  \(\textrm{SIP}(G,\varepsilon \hat{\alpha })\)  is that  \(k\in \mathbb {N}\) , the number of particles in the system is not too large; more precisely, $$\begin{aligned} \log k\ll \varepsilon ^{-1}\; \text {,}\;\;\quad \text {as}\,\, \varepsilon \ll 1\; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (2.10)
                 This condition, always assumed in previous works (see, e.g., [ GRV13 ,  BDG17 ,  Kim21 ,  Kim25 ]) is not just technical, but strictly required for the condensation mechanism to take place: ( 2.10 ) is a sufficient and necessary condition for the steady state of the system to charge configurations consisting of a single stack of particles only. 2.4.1  Outline of the proof of Theorem  2.2 
                            Our proof for determining the order-one dependence on  \(\varepsilon \hat{\alpha }_\textrm{min}\)  of  \(\textrm{gap}_\textrm{SIP}(G,\varepsilon \hat{\alpha })\)  combines three main ingredients: 
                     
                       the nested eigenstructure of  \(\textrm{SIP}\)  (valid for any underlying graph  G ) as already exploited in [ KS24 ]; 
                     
                     
                       the full knowledge of the eigendecomposition in mean-field geometries and, more specifically, the fact that eigenvalues grow quadratically (neglecting multiplicities) with the particle total number; 
                     
                     
                       comparison inequalities of Dirichlet forms associated to the particle system on a graph  G , against that on the complete graph. 
                     
                   All three steps are presented and combined to yield the proof of Theorem  2.2  in Sect.  3 , but the actual proof of the comparison inequality, longer and more technical, is presented in Sect.  4 . In the metastable regime ( 2.10 ), our comparisons build on the classical distinguishing paths method as applied, e.g., in the seminal work [ DSC93 ] to the symmetric exclusion process. Most of the care in our context lies in devising paths in which the total “cost” becomes not larger than  \(\varepsilon ^{-1} \, k^2\) , up to constants depending only on  G  and  \(\hat{\alpha }\) . Here, the cost of each move heavily depends on whether relocating a single particle augments, keeps constant, or lowers the total number of stacks, and whether stacks do have or not neighboring stacks. Hence, following the metastable behavior of the system offers us a guideline to construct efficient distinguishing paths, e.g., taking care of never creating more than one extra occupied site along the path. We refer to Sects.  4.2  and  4.3  for the details. When ( 2.10 ) does not hold, as already mentioned, the metastability picture breaks down, and so does this approach via distinguishing paths. In order to overcome this, we replace paths by a more sophisticated two-dimensional  surface , which is obtained by collecting the trace of all relevant paths. In presence of stacks consisting of a huge number of particles, this two-dimensional surface allows us to rigorously implement the idea of moving only a smaller portion of the stack at the time according to some suitably chosen probability, and leaving the rest untouched. While this strategy of moving only some (and not all) particles of a stack produces an extra factor  \(\varepsilon ^{-1}\) , which we could not afford in the regime ( 2.10 ), this is now not problematic, as we can control this factor with a suitable function of the number of particles of the stack. For more details, see Sect.  4.4 . As we just sketched, these estimates involving Dirichlet forms yield comparison constants which unavoidably degenerate like  \(1/k^2\)  as the total number of particles  \(k\in \mathbb {N}\)  grows. Here is where we crucially exploit the first two ingredients of the proof, in particular, the quadratic growth of eigenvalues for the mean-field system, which removes this degeneracy. 2.4.2  Outline of the proof of Theorems  2.3  and  2.4 
                            In order to establish the strict inequality between  \(\textrm{gap}_\textrm{SIP}(G,\varepsilon \hat{\alpha })\)  and  \(\textrm{gap}_\textrm{RW}(G,\varepsilon \hat{\alpha })\)  as in ( 1.3 ), comparison arguments as those we just described turn out to be too loose (and, actually, too elaborated) to capture the precise pre-factors. Instead, we follow a more basic approach, namely, turning the aforementioned metastability picture into some quantitative spectral information. More specifically, for a fixed graph  G  and a fixed number of particles  \(k\in \mathbb {N}\) , we employ and sharpen classical limit theorems for slow–fast systems from [ Kur73 ] (see also [ GRV13 ] for another application in the context of  \(\textrm{SIP}\) ). These theorems rigorously describe the metastable dynamics for the slow macroscopic variables of  \(\textrm{SIP}(G,\varepsilon \hat{\alpha })\)  in the limit  \(\varepsilon \rightarrow 0\) . The limit of the slow variables after a thermalization of the fast ones identifies the low-lying spectrum. This allows us to capture the behavior of  \(\textrm{gap}_\textrm{SIP}(G,\varepsilon \hat{\alpha })\) , at least for  \(\varepsilon \)  small enough. These intuitive ideas are stated and proved rigorously in Sect.  5.1 . We are then left with identifying the spectral gap of the limiting metastable dynamics. In contrast to  \(\textrm{SIP}\) , which is irreducible, this metastable chain may consist of both recurrent and transient states, as we detail in Sect.  5.2 . Recurrent regions correspond to those configurations in which all particles sit together, and the resulting single stack moves like  \(\textrm{RW}(G,\hat{\alpha })\) . Transient states are those configurations in which there are stacks at graph distance at least two from each other. Hence, the spectral gap of the metastable process is just the smallest among the random walk’s gap and the eigenvalues associated to any part of the (sub-stochastic) transient dynamics. Most of our work is devoted to analyzing the hierarchy of transient states. Indeed, even the decomposition into irreducible components and the jump rates of the transient dynamics are, in general, highly complicated and non-trivially dependent on the underlying geometry and the number of particles. Nevertheless, by exploiting a form of consistency for the metastable limiting dynamics, we are able to deduce that the lowest-lying eigenvalue of the transient dynamics is attained by the system with just  two  particles, as adding more particles—thus, potentially, stacks—does not cause any slowdown in the system. More in detail, in Sect.  5.3 , we verify that one only needs to focus on the number of stacks (and not on the precise allocation of particles in each stack): for each  \(m \ge 2\) , the smallest eigenvalue of all transient sub-systems with  m  piles comes exactly from the sub-system with  m  isolated particles. As a next step, in Sect.  5.4 , we prove that the smallest eigenvalue of all sub-systems with  \(m \ge 2\)  isolated particles is attained at  \(m=2\) , i.e., the sub-system with two isolated particles. Collecting these observations, we prove Theorem  2.3  in Sect.  5.5 . Theorem  2.4  is also proved in this section: by exploiting this rigorous metastable description of eigenvalues’ asymptotics, our proof boils down to estimates of relaxation and mean meeting times of independent particles on a well studied geometry, corresponding, respectively, to (the inverse of) the spectral gap of the recurrent and transient dynamics of  \(\textrm{SIP}\) ’s metastable process. 3  Proof of Theorem  2.2 
                      We follow the outline in Sect.  2.4.1 . 3.1  General eigenstructure of SIP We start by recalling from [ KS24 ] some general facts on the eigenstructure of  \(\textrm{SIP}\)  functional to our analysis. First of all,  \(\textrm{SIP}\)  is consistent, in the sense that the system with  \(k-1\)  particles is recovered (in the sense of finite-dimensional distributions) from the system with  k  particles, provided that one particle is removed  uniformly at random . In formula, this means that $$\begin{aligned} L_{G,\alpha ,k}\,\mathfrak {a}_{k} = \mathfrak {a}_{k}\,L_{G,\alpha ,k-1} \end{aligned}$$ 
                    (3.1)
                 holds true for all graphs  G , site weights  \(\alpha \) , and  \(k\ge 2\) , where  \(\mathfrak {a}_{k}:\mathbb {R}^{\Xi _{k-1}}\rightarrow \mathbb {R}^{\Xi _k}\)  is the  annihilation operator  defined as $$\begin{aligned} \mathfrak {a}_{k} g(\eta ):=\sum _{x\in V}\eta _x\, g(\eta -\delta _x)\; \text {,}\;\;\quad g\in \mathbb {R}^{\Xi _{k-1}}\; \text {,}\;\;\eta \in \Xi _k\; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (3.2)
                 As proved in [ KS24 , Appendix A],  \(\mathfrak {a}_k\)  is one-to-one. This readily implies that the (real) spectrum of  \(L_{G,\alpha ,k-1}\)  is contained (with multiplicities) in that of  \(L_{G,\alpha ,k}\)  (and, in particular, that ( 2.7 ) holds). More precisely, if  \((\lambda ,f)\)  is an eigenvalue-eigenfunction pair for  \(-L_{G,\alpha ,k-1}\) , then  \((\lambda ,\mathfrak {a}_k f)\)  is one for  \(-L_{G,\alpha ,k}\) . As a consequence of this fact and reversibility, the rest of the spectrum of  \(-L_{G,\alpha ,k}\)  that does not come from  \(-L_{G,\alpha ,k-1}\)  must be found in the orthogonal complement of those eigenfunctions “lifted”  from those of  \(-L_{G,\alpha ,k-1}\) . This observation leads us to introduce the  creation operator   \(\mathfrak {a}_{\alpha ,k-1}^\dagger :\mathbb {R}^{\Xi _k}\rightarrow \mathbb {R}^{\Xi _{k-1}}\) : $$\begin{aligned} \mathfrak {a}_{\alpha ,k-1}^\dagger f(\xi ) :=\sum _{x\in V} (\xi _x +\alpha _x ) \, f(\xi + \delta _x ) \; \text {,}\;\;\quad f \in \mathbb {R}^{\Xi _k} \; \text {,}\;\;\xi \in \Xi _{k-1} \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (3.3)
                 As demonstrated in [ KS24 , Proposition 3.1], the two operators  \(\mathfrak {a}_k\)  and  \(\mathfrak {a}_{\alpha ,k-1}^\dagger \)  are adjoint one to each other in the following sense: $$\begin{aligned} \langle \mathfrak {a}_kg\, \vert \, f \rangle _{\alpha ,k} = \frac{k}{|\alpha |+k-1} \, \langle g\, \vert \, \mathfrak {a}_{\alpha ,k-1}^\dagger f \rangle _{\alpha ,k-1} \; \text {,}\;\;\quad f\in \mathbb {R}^{\Xi _k} \; \text {,}\;\;g \in \mathbb {R}^{\Xi _{k-1}} \; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (3.4)
                 where  \(\langle \,\cdot \,\, \vert \, \,\cdot \, \rangle _{\alpha ,k}\)  denotes the inner product in  \(\Xi _k\)  with respect to  \(\mu _{\alpha ,k}\)  given in ( 2.2 ). In turn,  \(\mathfrak {a}_{\alpha ,k-1}^\dagger \)  is surjective and the following orthogonal decomposition holds: $$\begin{aligned} L^2(\mu _{\alpha ,k}) = \textrm{Im}\,\mathfrak {a}_k\oplus _{\perp } \textrm{Ker}\,\mathfrak {a}_{\alpha ,k-1}^\dagger \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (3.5)
                 Next, let us recall a well-known variational characterization of  \(\textrm{gap}_k(G,\alpha )\) : for each  \(k\ge 2\) , $$\begin{aligned} \textrm{gap}_k(G,\alpha ) = \inf _{\begin{array}{c} f \in \mathbb {R}^{\Xi _k}\\ f \ne \text {const.} \end{array}} \frac{\mathcal {E}_{G,\alpha ,k}(f)}{\textrm{Var}_{\alpha ,k}(f)} \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (3.6)
                 Here,  \(\mathcal {E}_{G,\alpha ,k}(f)=\left\langle f\, \Bigg | \vert \, -L_{G,\alpha ,k}f \right\rangle _{\alpha ,k}\)  denotes the Dirichlet form evaluated at  \(f\in \mathbb {R}^{\Xi _k}\) , namely, $$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {E}_{G,\alpha ,k}(f) = \frac{1}{2} \sum _{\eta \in \Xi _k} \sum _{x,y\in V} \mu _{\alpha ,k}(\eta )\,c_{xy}\, \eta _x \left( \alpha _y + \eta _y\right) \left( f(\eta -\delta _x+\delta _y)-f(\eta )\right) ^2 \; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (3.7)
                 whereas  \(\textrm{Var}_{\alpha ,k}(f)\)  stands for the variance of  \(f \in \mathbb {R}^{\Xi _k}\)  with respect to  \(\mu _{\alpha ,k}\) . If  \(f\in \textrm{Ker}\,\mathfrak {a}_{\alpha ,k-1}^\dagger \) , then  \(\langle f\rangle _{\alpha ,k}=\langle f\, \vert \, 1 \rangle _{\alpha ,k}=0\)  (remark that  \(1\in \textrm{Im}\,\mathfrak a_k\)  and apply ( 3.4 )) and, thus, $$\begin{aligned} \textrm{Var}_{\alpha ,k}(f) = \left\Vert f\right\Vert _{\alpha ,k}^2:=\sum _{\eta \in \Xi _k} \mu _{\alpha ,k}(\eta )\, f(\eta )^2 \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ Taking advantage of the lifting property of  \(\mathfrak {a}_k\) , the self-adjointness of  \(L_{G,\alpha ,k}\)  on  \(L^2(\mu _{\alpha ,k})\) , and the orthogonal decomposition in ( 3.5 ), ( 3.6 ) simplifies as follows: $$\begin{aligned} \textrm{gap}_k(G,\alpha ) = \textrm{gap}_{k-1}(G,\alpha ) \wedge \bigg ( \inf _{\begin{array}{c} f\in \textrm{Ker}\,\mathfrak {a}_{\alpha ,k-1}^\dagger \\ f\ne 0 \end{array}} \frac{\mathcal {E}_{G,\alpha ,k}(f)}{\left\Vert f\right\Vert _{\alpha ,k}^2} \bigg ) \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (3.8)
                 Hence, by an iterative argument on  \(k\ge 2\) , we may focus on comparing  \(\mathcal {E}_{G,\alpha ,k}(f)\)  and  \(\left\Vert f\right\Vert _{\alpha ,k}^2\) , only for functions  \(f \in \textrm{Ker}\,\mathfrak {a}_{\alpha ,k-1}^\dagger \) . 3.2  Dirichlet forms and comparisons The Dirichlet form  \(\mathcal {E}_{G,\alpha ,k}(f)\)  depends on the underlying graph  G , whereas the reversible measure  \(\mu _{\alpha ,k}\) , and, thus, the orthogonal decomposition in ( 3.5 ) and  \(\left\Vert f\right\Vert _{\alpha ,k}^2\) , do not. This simple observation motivates us to compare  \(\mathcal {E}_{G,\alpha ,k}(f)\)  with its complete graph analogue, i.e.,  \(\mathcal {E}_{K,\alpha ,k}(f)\) , where  \(K=K_V\)  denotes the complete graph on the sites of  V  with unitary conductances  \(c_{xy}\equiv 1\) . It turns out that, in this complete graph case, we can obtain the full eigendecomposition of  \(\textrm{SIP}\) . Remark that, although the spectrum and a set of eigenfunctions of  \(\textrm{SIP}_k(K,\alpha )\)  are known [ Shi77 ] (see also [ Cor23 , Theorem 1.4] or [ WZ19 ] and references therein), the next proposition, together with the identity in ( 3.8 ), provides a simple and complete description of eigenvalues and eigenspaces when  \(G=K\) , which we shall exploit later. 
                   Proposition 3.1 
                   For every  \(k \ge 1\)  and  \(f\in \textrm{Ker}\,\mathfrak {a}_{\alpha ,k-1}^\dagger \) , we have $$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {E}_{K,\alpha ,k}(f) = k \left( |\alpha |+k-1\right) \left\Vert f\right\Vert _{\alpha ,k}^2 \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (3.9)
                 
                
                   Proof 
                   The result is well known for  \(k=1\) , the random walk case. Indeed,  \(\textrm{Ker}\,\mathfrak a_{\alpha ,0}^\dagger \)  coincides with the subspace of functions  \(f\in \mathbb {R}^{\Xi _1}\)  having mean zero with respect to  \(\mu _{\alpha ,1}\) , which equals  \(\mu _{\alpha ,1}(\delta _x)=\frac{\alpha _x}{|\alpha |}\) ,  \(x\in V\) . 
                   Now, fix  \(k\ge 2\)  and  \(f\in \textrm{Ker}\,\mathfrak {a}_{\alpha ,k-1}^\dagger \) . We have $$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {E}_{K,\alpha ,k}(f)= k\sum _{\xi \in \Xi _{{k-1}}}\mu _{\alpha ,k-1}(\xi )\, \mathcal {E}_{K,\alpha +\xi ,1}(f_\xi )\; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (3.10)
                 with  \(f_\xi \in \mathbb {R}^{\Xi _1}\)  being defined as  \(f_\xi (\delta _x):=f(\xi +\delta _x)\) . Remark that ( 3.10 ) corresponds to [ KS24 , Eq. (3.10)], which is deduced from the following identity [ KS24 , Eq. (3.9)]: for all  \(x\in V\)  and  \(\xi \in \Xi _{k-1}\) , $$\begin{aligned} \mu _{\alpha ,k}(\xi +\delta _x)\left( \xi _x+1\right) = \frac{Z_{\alpha ,k-1}}{Z_{\alpha ,k}}\,\mu _{\alpha ,k-1}(\xi )\left( \alpha _x+\xi _x\right) = k\,\mu _{\alpha ,k-1}(\xi )\,\frac{\alpha _x+\xi _x}{|\alpha |+k-1}\; \text {,}\;\;\nonumber \\ \end{aligned}$$ 
                    (3.11)
                 where the second step used (cf. ( 2.2 )) $$\begin{aligned} \frac{Z_{\alpha ,k-1}}{Z_{\alpha ,k}} =\frac{k}{|\alpha |+k-1}\; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ Analogously to ( 3.10 ), we get $$\begin{aligned} \left\Vert f\right\Vert _{\alpha ,k}^2 = \sum _{\xi \in \Xi _{k-1}}\mu _{\alpha ,k-1}(\xi )\left\Vert f_\xi \right\Vert _{\alpha +\xi ,1}^2\; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (3.12)
                 By ( 3.10 ) and the identity in ( 3.9 ) for  \(k=1\)  (note that  \(f\in \textrm{Ker}\,\mathfrak {a}_{\alpha ,k-1}^\dagger \)  ensures that  \(f_\xi \in \textrm{Ker}\,\mathfrak a_{\alpha ,0}^\dagger \) ) with  \(\alpha +\xi \)  in place of  \(\alpha \)  therein (note that  \(|\alpha +\xi |=|\alpha |+k-1\) ), we obtain $$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {E}_{K,\alpha ,k}(f) = k\left( |\alpha |+k-1\right) \sum _{\xi \in \Xi _{k-1}}\mu _{\alpha ,k-1}(\xi ) \left\Vert f_\xi \right\Vert _{\alpha +\xi ,1}^2 = k\left( |\alpha |+k-1\right) \left\Vert f\right\Vert _{\alpha ,k}^2\; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ where the last step used ( 3.12 ). This proves the desired result for  \(k\ge 2\) .  \(\square \) 
                 In view of ( 3.8 ) and Proposition  3.1 , it remains to compare the two Dirichlet forms  \(\mathcal {E}_{G,\alpha ,k}(f)\)  and  \(\mathcal {E}_{K,\alpha ,k}(f)\) , for any  \(f\in \mathbb {R}^{\Xi _k}\) . This is the content of the following theorem, and certainly represents the hardest step of the proof of Theorem  2.2 . 
                   Theorem 3.2 
                   For all  \(k \ge 2\)  and  \(f\in \mathbb {R}^{\Xi _k}\) , we have $$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {E}_{K,\alpha ,k}(f)\le \frac{{54} \, k \left( \alpha _\textrm{max}+k-1\right) |V|^2 \, \textrm{diam}(G) \, {(6e)}^{\alpha _\textrm{max} }}{\alpha _\textrm{min} \, \alpha _\textrm{ratio} \, c_\textrm{min}} \, \mathcal {E}_{G,\alpha ,k}(f) \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                 We postpone the proof of this estimate to Sect.  4  and, in Remark  4.7 , we discuss possible improvements. 
                   Remark 3.3 
                   While all previous steps relied on the fact that we chose the test function  f  in  \(\textrm{Ker}\,\mathfrak {a}_{\alpha ,k-1}^\dagger \subset \mathbb {R}^{\Xi _k}\) , the last theorem is true for all  \(f\in \mathbb {R}^{\Xi _k}\) . 
                 3.3  Proof of Theorem  2.2 
                         Assuming the validity of Theorem  3.2 , we now conclude the proof of Theorem  2.2 . 
                   Proof of Theorem 2.2 
                   By ( 2.4 ), ( 3.8 ) and an iterative argument, it suffices to prove $$\begin{aligned} \inf _{\begin{array}{c} f\in \textrm{Ker}\,\mathfrak {a}_{\alpha ,k-1}^\dagger \\ f\ne 0 \end{array} } \frac{\mathcal {E}_{G,\alpha ,k}(f)}{\left\Vert f\right\Vert _{\alpha ,k}^2} \ge \alpha _\textrm{min}\, \frac{1}{21} \frac{ \alpha _\textrm{ratio}\, c_\textrm{min}}{ 6^{\alpha _\textrm{max}} \, |V|^2\, \textrm{diam}(G)} \; \text {,}\;\;\quad \text {{for all}}\,\, k\ge 2\; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ By Theorem  3.2  and Proposition  3.1 , we have, for all  \(f\in \textrm{Ker}\,\mathfrak {a}_{\alpha ,k-1}^\dagger \)  and  \(k \ge 2\) , $$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {E}_{G,\alpha ,k}(f)&\ge \frac{ \alpha _\textrm{min} \, \alpha _\textrm{ratio} \, c_\textrm{min} }{ {54} \, k \left( \alpha _\textrm{max}+k-1\right) |V|^2 \, \textrm{diam}(G) \, {(6e)}^{\alpha _\textrm{max} } } \, \mathcal {E}_{K,\alpha ,k}(f) \\&= \frac{\alpha _\textrm{min}\, c_\textrm{min}\, \alpha _\textrm{ratio}\left( |\alpha |+k-1\right) }{{54} \left( \alpha _\textrm{max}+k-1\right) |V|^2\, \textrm{diam} (G) \, {(6e)}^{\alpha _\textrm{max}} }\left\Vert f\right\Vert _{\alpha ,k}^2 \\&\ge \frac{1}{{54}}\frac{\alpha _\textrm{min}\, c_\textrm{min} \, \alpha _\textrm{ratio} }{|V|^2 \, \textrm{diam}(G) \, {(6e)}^{\alpha _\textrm{max}}} \left\Vert f\right\Vert _{\alpha ,k}^2 \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ This concludes the proof of the theorem.  \(\square \) 
                 4  Proof of Theorem  3.2 
                      Since  \(G, \alpha , k\)  and  \(f\in \mathbb {R}^{\Xi _k}\)  are fixed all throughout this section, we abbreviate $$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {E}= \mathcal {E}_{G,\alpha ,k}(f) \quad \text {and} \quad \mathcal {E}_K = \mathcal {E}_{K,\alpha ,k}(f) \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (4.1)
                 Let us assume that an ordering is given on  V , so that $$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {E}_K= \sum _{x<y} \sum _{\eta \in \Xi _{k}} \mu _{\alpha ,k}(\eta )\,\eta _x\left( \alpha _y+\eta _y\right) \left( f(\eta -\delta _x+\delta _y)-f(\eta )\right) ^{2} =:\sum _{x<y} \mathcal {E}_K^{x,y} \; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (4.2)
                 where, for any pair  \(x<y\) ,  \(\mathcal {E}_K^{x,y}\)  is defined as the summation in  \(\eta \in \Xi _k\)  above. The following lemma gives an upper bound for the last term  \(\mathcal {E}_K^{x,y}\) . 
                 Lemma 4.1 
                 For every pair  \(x<y\) , $$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {E}_K^{x,y} \le \frac{{108 \, k \left( \alpha _\textrm{max}+k-1\right) \textrm{diam}(G) \, (6e)^{\alpha _\textrm{max} }}}{\alpha _\textrm{min} \, \alpha _\textrm{ratio} \, c_\textrm{min}} \, \mathcal {E}\; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (4.3)
                 
               We first prove Theorem  3.2  provided that Lemma  4.1  holds true. 
                 Proof of Theorem 3.2 
                 Recall the notation from ( 4.1 ). By ( 4.2 ) and ( 4.3 ), we obtain This is exactly the claim in Theorem  3.2 .  \(\square \) 
               In the remainder of this section, we fix  \(x<y\)  and prove Lemma  4.1 . For  \(A\subseteq V\)  and  \(\ell \in \mathbb {N}_0\) , define $$\begin{aligned} \Xi _\ell ^A :=\left\{ \sigma \in \Xi _\ell : \sum _{{z} \in A}\sigma _{z} = \ell \right\} \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ Note that  \(\Xi _{k}^{V}=\Xi _{k}\) . Moreover, for  \(\sigma \in \Xi _{\ell }^{A}\) ,  \(y_{1},\ldots ,y_{s}\in V\) , and  \(m_{1},\ldots ,m_{s}\in \mathbb {N}_0\) , define $$\begin{aligned} \sigma _{m_1,\ldots ,m_{s}}^{y_{1}\cdots y_s} :=\sigma +\sum _{i=1}^{s} m_{i}\, \delta _{y_{i}} \in \Xi _{\ell +m_1 +\cdots +m_s}\; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (4.4)
                 where the right-hand side should be understood as a summation of functions. Thus, the number of particles in the new configuration  \(\sigma _{m_{1},\ldots ,m_{s}}^{y_{1}\cdots y_{s}}\)  equals  \(\ell +m_{1}+\cdots +m_s\) . According to ( 4.4 ), we may decompose the Dirichlet summation  \(\mathcal {E}_K^{x,y}\)  in ( 4.2 ) as $$\begin{aligned} \sum _{\ell =1}^{k} \sum _{m=1}^{\ell } \sum _{\sigma \in \Xi _{k-\ell }^{V\setminus \{x,y\}}} \mu _{\alpha ,k}(\sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy})\,m\left( \alpha _{y}+\ell -m\right) (f(\sigma _{m-1,\ell -m+1}^{xy})-f(\sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy}))^{2} \; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ where  \(\ell \in \llbracket 1,k \rrbracket :=[1,k]\cap \mathbb {Z}\)  denotes the number of particles in  \(\{x,y\}\) , and  \(m\in \llbracket 1,\ell \rrbracket \)  denotes the number of particles at  x . For each  \(\eta \in \Xi _k\)  and  \(x,y \in V\) , define $$\begin{aligned} &  {\mathfrak {c}_K(\eta ;x,y)} :=\mu _{\alpha ,k}(\eta ) \, \eta _x \left( \alpha _y + \eta _y\right) \; \text {,}\;\;\nonumber \\ &  \quad \nabla _K^2 f(\eta ;x,y) :=\mathfrak {c}_K (\eta ;x,y)\, (f(\eta -\delta _x+\delta _y)-f(\eta ))^2 \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (4.5)
                 According to this notation, $$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {E}_K^{x,y} = \sum _{\ell =1}^k \sum _{m=1}^\ell \sum _{\sigma \in \Xi _{k-\ell }^{V\setminus \{x,y\}}} \nabla _K^2 f ( \sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy}; x,y) \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (4.6)
                 Indeed, the two summations in  \(\ell \in \llbracket 1,k \rrbracket \)  and  \(m \in \llbracket 1,\ell \rrbracket \)  count all possible types of jumps from  x  to  y , and the summation in  \(\sigma \in \Xi _{k-\ell }^{V \setminus \{x,y\}}\)  counts the possible particle configuration on the remaining vertices in  \(V \setminus \{x,y\}\) . Fix a shortest sequence $$\begin{aligned} x=x_{0},x_{1},\ldots ,x_{t}=y \end{aligned}$$ 
                    (4.7)
                 in  G  such that  \(c_{x_{s-1}x_s}>0\)  for all  \(s\in \llbracket 1,t \rrbracket \) , where  \(t\in \llbracket 1,\textrm{diam}(G) \rrbracket \) . For  \(\eta ,\zeta \in \Xi _{k}\) , write $$\begin{aligned} \mathfrak {c}(\eta ,\zeta ):=\mu _{\alpha ,k}(\eta )\, r_{\alpha ,k}(\eta ,\zeta )\quad \text {and} \quad \nabla ^{2}f(\eta ,\zeta ) :=\mathfrak {c}(\eta ,\zeta ) \left( f(\zeta )-f(\eta )\right) ^{2}\; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (4.8)
                 where  \(r_{\alpha ,k}(\cdot ,\cdot )\)  is the transition rate function of  \(\textrm{SIP}_{k}(G,\alpha )\) . Note that by the reversibility of  \(\mu _{\alpha ,k}\) ,  \(\nabla ^2f(\eta ,\zeta ) = \nabla ^2f(\zeta ,\eta )\) . Then, we have $$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {E}= \sum _{ \eta \in \Xi _k } \sum _{\begin{array}{c} z<w \\ c_{zw}>0 \end{array} } \nabla ^2f(\eta ,\eta -\delta _z+\delta _w ) \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (4.9)
                 A sequence of configurations  \(\eta _0,\eta _1,\ldots , \eta _M\)  in  \(\Xi _k\)  is a  path  if the transition rates  \(r_{\alpha ,k}(\cdot ,\cdot )\)  are positive along the sequence, i.e.,  \(r_{\alpha ,k}(\eta _{s-1},\eta _{s})>0\)  for all  \(s\in \llbracket 1,M \rrbracket \) . The idea to prove Lemma  4.1  is as follows. First, we decompose  \(\mathcal {E}_K^{x,y}\)  according to ( 4.6 ). Then, for each triple  \((\ell ,m,\sigma )\) , we upper bound  \({\nabla _K^2 f ( \sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy}; x,y) }\)  with a certain collection of terms  \(\nabla ^2f(\eta ,\eta -\delta _z+\delta _w)\)  that appear in the right-hand side of ( 4.9 ). Finally, we count the number of overlaps for each term  \(\nabla ^2f(\eta ,\eta -\delta _z+\delta _w)\)  for all such triple  \((\ell ,m,\sigma )\) , which gives an upper bound of  \(\mathcal {E}_K^{x.y}\)  in terms of  \(\mathcal {E}\) . The procedure of upper bounding each  \({\nabla _K^2 f ( \sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy}; x,y) }\)  depends on the detailed distribution of particles along the sequence ( 4.7 ). In the following four subsections, Sects.  4.1 ,  4.2 ,  4.3  and  4.4 , we demonstrate each simple cases, and finally in Sect.  4.5  we deal with the general case and conclude the proof of Lemma  4.1 . 4.1  Connected case First, suppose that (cf. ( 4.7 )) $$\begin{aligned} c_{xy}>0 \; \text {,}\;\;\quad {\text {which implies that}}\,\, t=1 \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (4.10)
                 Define $$\begin{aligned} {\Omega _{x,y}^{\ell ,m,\sigma }} :=\Big \{ \{ \sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy}, \sigma _{m-1,\ell -m+1}^{xy} \} \Big \} \, \; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ which is the singleton set that contains the edge  \(\{\sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy}, \sigma _{m-1,\ell -m+1}^{xy} \}\) . When there is no room for confusion, we simply write  \(\Omega = \Omega _{x,y}^{\ell ,m,\sigma }\) . 
                   Lemma 4.2 
                   Suppose that ( 4.10 ) holds. Then, we have $$\begin{aligned} {\nabla _K^2 f ( \sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy}; x,y) } \le {\frac{m \left( \alpha _y+\ell -m\right) }{c_\textrm{min} \, \alpha _\textrm{min}}} \sum _{\{\zeta ,\zeta '\} {\in } \Omega } \nabla ^2f(\zeta ,\zeta ') \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                
                   Proof 
                   It is clear that  \(\sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy},\sigma _{m-1,\ell -m+1}^{xy}\)  is itself a path, and $$\begin{aligned} {\nabla _K^2 f ( \sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy}; x,y) } = \frac{1}{c_{xy}} \, \nabla ^2f(\sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy},\sigma _{m-1,\ell -m+1}^{xy}) = \frac{1}{c_{xy}} \, \sum _{\{\zeta ,\zeta '\} \in \Omega } \nabla ^2 f(\zeta ,\zeta ') \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ Thus, the lemma easily follows since  \(c_{xy} \ge c_\textrm{min}\)  and  \(m\,(\alpha _y+\ell -m) \ge \alpha _y \ge \alpha _\textrm{min}\) .  \(\square \) 
                 4.2  Decomposition of gradients: occupied sites Now, assume that (cf. ( 4.7 ) and ( 4.10 )) $$\begin{aligned} c_{xy}=0 \; \text {,}\;\;\quad {\text {which implies that}} \,\, t \ge 2 \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (4.11)
                 Additionally, in this subsection, assume that all sites along the sequence ( 4.7 ) are occupied by particles of  \(\sigma \) , i.e., $$\begin{aligned} \sigma _{x_s} \ge 1\quad \text {for all} \,\, s\in \llbracket 1,t-1 \rrbracket \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (4.12)
                 Then, starting from  \(\sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy}\) , we send a single particle from  \(x_{s-1}\)  to  \(x_s\)  for each  \(s \in \llbracket 1,t \rrbracket \) , to arrive at  \(\sigma _{m-1,\ell -m+1}^{xy}\) . The corresponding path becomes $$\begin{aligned} \sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy},\sigma _{m-1,1,\ell -m}^{xx_1y},\ldots ,\sigma _{m-1,1,\ell -m}^{xx_{t-1}y},\sigma _{m-1,\ell -m+1}^{xy} \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (4.13)
                 See Fig.  1  for a visual representation. Fig. 1 Full size image Path of configurations from  \(\sigma _{,\ell -m}^{xy}\)  to  \(\sigma _{m-1,\ell -m+1}^{xy}\)  in Sect.  4.2 . In this occupied case, the red particle simply moves from  x  to  y  along the sequence  \(x=x_0,x_1,\dots ,x_t=y\)  consecutively Let  \(\Omega ={\Omega _{x,y}^{\ell ,m,\sigma }}\)  be the collection of all edges  \(\{\sigma _{m-1,1,\ell -m}^{xx_{s-1}y},\sigma _{m-1,1,\ell -m}^{xx_sy}\}\)  for all  \(s \in \llbracket 1,t \rrbracket \) , i.e., $$\begin{aligned} \Omega _{x,y}^{\ell ,m,\sigma }:= \left\{ \{\sigma _{m-1,1,\ell -m}^{xx_{s-1}y},\sigma _{m-1,1,\ell -m}^{xx_sy}\}: s \in \llbracket 1,t \rrbracket \right\} \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$  With this notation, we prove in this subsection the following lemma. 
                   Lemma 4.3 
                   Assume that the conditions in ( 4.11 ) and ( 4.12 ) hold. Then, we have $$\begin{aligned} {\nabla _K^2 f ( \sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy}; x,y) } \le {\frac{ t \, m \left( \alpha _y + \ell -m\right) (1+\alpha _\textrm{max})}{c_\textrm{min} \, \alpha _\textrm{min}} } \sum _{\{\zeta ,\zeta '\} {\in } \Omega } \nabla ^2f(\zeta ,\zeta ') \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                
                   Proof 
                   By Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, for any path  \(\eta =\eta _0,\eta _1,\ldots ,\eta _M=\zeta \) , we have (cf. ( 4.8 )) $$\begin{aligned} (f(\zeta )-f(\eta ))^2 \le \left( \sum _{i=1}^M \nabla ^2f(\eta _{i-1},\eta _i) \right) \left( \sum _{i=1}^M \frac{1}{\mathfrak {c}(\eta _{i-1},\eta _i)} \right) \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (4.14)
                 Thus, applying ( 4.14 ) along the path ( 4.13 ), we get (cf. ( 4.5 )) $$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} {\nabla _K^2 f ( \sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy}; x,y) } \le \, \Bigg ( \sum _{s=1}^t \nabla ^2f(&\sigma _{m-1,1,\ell -m}^{xx_{s-1}y},\sigma _{m-1,1,\ell -m}^{xx_sy}) \Bigg ) \, \\&\times \left( \sum _{s=1}^t \frac{ \mu _{\alpha ,k}(\sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy})\, m\left( \alpha _y + \ell -m \right) }{ \mathfrak {c}(\sigma _{m-1,1,\ell -m}^{xx_{s-1}y},\sigma _{m-1,1,\ell -m}^{xx_sy}) } \right) \; \text {.}\end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$ 
                    (4.15)
                 For  \(x\in V\)  and  \(m \in \mathbb {N}_0\) , define $$\begin{aligned} \pi _{x}(m) :=\frac{\Gamma (\alpha _{x}+m)}{\Gamma (\alpha _{x})\,m!}\; \text {,}\;\;\quad \text {such that} \,\, \mu _{\alpha ,k}(\eta )=\frac{1}{Z_{\alpha ,k}}\prod _{x\in V}\pi _{x}(\eta _{x})\; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (4.16)
                 Here,  \(\Gamma (\cdot )\)  denotes the usual gamma function. We now collect some basic properties.  \(\square \) 
                
                   Lemma 4.4 
                   We have, for each  \(m \in \mathbb {N}_0\) , $$\begin{aligned} \alpha _x \le \pi _x(m)\left( \alpha _x+m\right) = \pi _x(m+1) \left( m+1\right) \le \alpha _x\,e^{\alpha _x\sum _{j=1}^m \frac{1}{j}} \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (4.17)
                 Moreover, for  \(m \ge m' \ge 1\) , $$\begin{aligned} 1 \le \frac{ \pi _x(m)\,m }{ \pi _x(m')\,m' } \le e^{\alpha _x \sum _{j=m'}^{m-1}\frac{1}{j}} \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (4.18)
                 
                
                   Proof 
                   Since  \(\Gamma (a+1)=a\,\Gamma (a)\)  for all  \(a> 0\) , $$\begin{aligned} \pi _x(m) \left( \alpha _x+m\right) = \frac{ \Gamma (\alpha _x+m+1) }{\Gamma (\alpha _x) \, m!} = \pi _x(m+1) \left( m+1\right) \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ Clearly, since  \(\alpha _x >0\) , $$\begin{aligned} \frac{ \Gamma (\alpha _x+m+1) }{\Gamma (\alpha _x) \, m!} = \alpha _x \, \prod _{j=1}^m \frac{j+\alpha _x}{j} \ge \alpha _x \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ On the other hand, since  \(1+a \le e^a\)  for any  \(a \in \mathbb {R}\) , $$\begin{aligned} \frac{ \Gamma (\alpha _x+m+1) }{\Gamma (\alpha _x) \, m!} = \alpha _x \, \prod _{j=1}^m \left( 1+\frac{\alpha _x}{j} \right) \le \alpha _x \, \exp \left( \sum _{j=1}^m \frac{\alpha _x}{j} \right) \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ Combining the three displayed identities proves ( 4.17 ). In addition, for  \(m \ge m' \ge 1\) , $$\begin{aligned} \frac{\pi _x(m) \, m}{\pi _x(m') \, m'} = \frac{ \Gamma (\alpha _x+m) / (m-1)!}{ \Gamma (\alpha _x+m') / (m'-1)!} = \prod _{j=m'}^{m-1} \frac{j+\alpha _x}{j} \; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ which lies inside the interval  \([1,\exp \, (\sum _{j=m'}^{m-1}\frac{\alpha _x}{j})]\)  due to the same reasoning as above. This proves ( 4.18 ).  \(\square \) 
                 Returning to the proof of Lemma  4.3 , let us look at the term inside the second parenthesis in ( 4.15 ). The term for  \(s=1\)  equals $$\begin{aligned} \frac{ \mu _{\alpha ,k}(\sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy})\, m\left( \alpha _y + \ell -m \right) }{ \mathfrak {c}(\sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy},\sigma _{m-1,1,\ell -m}^{xx_1y}) } = \frac{ m\left( \alpha _y + \ell -m \right) }{ r_{\alpha ,k} (\sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy},\sigma _{m-1,1,\ell -m}^{xx_1y}) } = \frac{ \alpha _{y}+\ell -m }{ \left( \alpha _{x_1}+\sigma _{x_1}\right) c_{xx_1} } \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ Similarly, by ( 4.16 ), the term for  \(s=t\)  becomes $$\begin{aligned} \frac{ \pi _{x}(m)\,\pi _{x_{t-1}}(\sigma _{x_{t-1}})\, m }{ \pi _{x}(m-1)\, \pi _{x_{t-1}} (\sigma _{x_{t-1}}+1) \, (\sigma _{x_{t-1}}+1) \, c_{x_{t-1}y} } \; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ and the term for  \(s \in \llbracket 2,t-1 \rrbracket \)  becomes $$\begin{aligned} \frac{ \pi _{x}(m)\,\pi _{x_{s-1}}(\sigma _{x_{s-1}})\, m\left( \alpha _{y}+\ell -m\right) }{ \pi _{x}(m-1)\, \pi _{x_{s-1}}(\sigma _{x_{s-1}}+1) \, (\sigma _{x_{s-1}}+1) \left( \alpha _{x_{s}}+\sigma _{x_{s}}\right) c_{x_{s-1}x_{s}} } \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ Combining the last three displayed identities, the summation inside the second parenthesis in ( 4.15 ) equals $$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned}&\frac{ \alpha _{y}+\ell -m }{ \left( \alpha _{x_1}+\sigma _{x_1}\right) c_{xx_1} } + \frac{ \pi _{x}(m)\,\pi _{x_{t-1}}(\sigma _{x_{t-1}})\, m }{ \pi _{x}(m-1)\, \pi _{x_{t-1}} (\sigma _{x_{t-1}}+1) \, (\sigma _{x_{t-1}}+1) \, c_{x_{t-1}y} } \\&+ \sum _{s=2}^{t-1} \frac{ \pi _{x}(m)\,\pi _{x_{s-1}}(\sigma _{x_{s-1}})\, m\left( \alpha _{y}+\ell -m\right) }{ \pi _{x}(m-1)\, \pi _{x_{s-1}}(\sigma _{x_{s-1}}+1) \, (\sigma _{x_{s-1}}+1) \left( \alpha _{x_{s}}+\sigma _{x_{s}}\right) c_{x_{s-1}x_{s}} } \; \text {.}\end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$ 
                    (4.19)
                 The first term in ( 4.19 ) is easily bounded by (cf. ( 2.5 )) $$\begin{aligned} \frac{\alpha _y + \ell -m }{ \left( \alpha _{x_1}+\sigma _{x_1}\right) c_{xx_1} } \le \frac{m \left( \alpha _y+\ell -m\right) }{c_\textrm{min}} \; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ where we used that  \(\sigma _{x_1} \ge 1\) . By ( 4.16 ), the second term in ( 4.19 ) reads as $$\begin{aligned} \frac{ \alpha _x + m-1 }{ (\alpha _{x_{t-1}} + \sigma _{x_{t-1}}) \, c_{x_{t-1} {y}} } \le {\frac{m \left( \alpha _y + \ell -m\right) }{c_\textrm{min} \, \alpha _\textrm{min}} \, \frac{\alpha _x + m-1}{m} \le \frac{m \left( \alpha _y + \ell -m\right) }{c_\textrm{min} \, \alpha _\textrm{min}} \, (1+\alpha _\textrm{max}) } \; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ where we used  \(\sigma _{x_{t-1}} \ge 1\)  and  \(\alpha _y + \ell -m \ge \alpha _\textrm{min}\)  at the first inequality and  \(\alpha _x + m-1 \le m(1+\alpha _x)\)  at the second inequality. Finally, the third term in ( 4.19 ) is similarly dealt with: $$\begin{aligned} \sum _{s=2}^{t-1} \frac{ \left( \alpha _x + m-1\right) \left( \alpha _{y}+\ell -m\right) }{ (\alpha _{x_{s-1}} + \sigma _{x_{s-1}})\left( \alpha _{x_{s}}+\sigma _{x_{s}}\right) c_{x_{s-1}x_{s}} } \le {\frac{(t-2) \, m \left( \alpha _y + \ell -m\right) (1 + \alpha _\textrm{max})}{c_\textrm{min} \, \alpha _\textrm{min} } } \; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ where we used that  \(\sigma _{x_{s-1}}\ge 1\)  and  \({\alpha _{x_s} + \sigma _{x_s} \ge \alpha _\textrm{min}}\)  for all  \(s\in \llbracket 2,t-1 \rrbracket \) . Therefore, inserting these three bounds and ( 4.19 ) into ( 4.15 ), we conclude that $$\begin{aligned} {\nabla _K^2 f ( \sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy}; x,y) } \le {\frac{ t \, m \left( \alpha _y + \ell -m\right) (1+\alpha _\textrm{max})}{c_\textrm{min} \, \alpha _\textrm{min}} } \left( \sum _{s=1}^t \nabla ^2f(\sigma _{m-1,1,\ell -m}^{xx_{s-1}y},\sigma _{m-1,1,\ell -m}^{xx_sy}) \right) \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ This proves Lemma  4.3 .  \(\square \) 4.3  Decomposition of gradients: empty sites and few particles Next, assume ( 4.11 ) and that sites  \(x_1,\dots ,x_{t-1}\)  are empty with respect to  \(\sigma \) , i.e., $$\begin{aligned} \sigma _{x_s}=0 \quad \text {for all} \,\, s \in \llbracket 1,t-1 \rrbracket \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (4.20)
                 In this case, we divide the analysis into two parts. In Sect.  4.3 , assume that $$\begin{aligned} \alpha _x \sum _{j=1}^{m-1}\frac{1}{j} \le 1 \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (4.21)
                 We construct a path from  \(\sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy}\)  to  \(\sigma _{m-1,\ell -m+1}^{xy}\)  as follows (see also Fig.  2 ):  (F) : 
                       Move  m  particles from  \(x_{s-1}\)  to  \(x_{s}\)  consecutively, for each  \(s\in \llbracket 1,t-1 \rrbracket \) . This subpath starts from  \(\sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy}\) , visits  \(\sigma _{m-i,i,\ell -m}^{x_{s-1}x_sy}\)  for each  \(i\in \llbracket 0,m \rrbracket \)  and  \(s\in \llbracket 1,t-1 \rrbracket \) , and finally arrives at  \(\sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{x_{t-1}y}\) . 
                     (S) : 
                       Move a particle from  \(x_{t-1}\)  to  y . The resulting configuration is  \(\sigma _{m-1,\ell -m+1}^{x_{t-1}y}\) . If  \(m=1\) , the path is complete. 
                     (B) : 
                       If  \(m\ge 2\) , move the remaining  \(m-1\)  particles from  \(x_s\)  to  \(x_{s-1}\)  consecutively, for each  \(s\in \llbracket 1,t-1 \rrbracket \)  (backwards). This subpath starts from  \(\sigma _{m-1,\ell -m+1}^{x_{t-1}y}\) , visits  \(\sigma _{m-i,i-1,\ell -m+1}^{x_{s-1}x_sy}\)  for each  \(i\in \llbracket 1,m \rrbracket \)  and  \(s\in \llbracket 1,t-1 \rrbracket \) , thereby arrives at  \(\sigma _{m-1,\ell -m+1}^{xy}\)  as desired. 
                     Fig. 2 Full size image Path of configurations from  \(\sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy}\)  to  \(\sigma _{m-1,\ell -m+1}^{xy}\)  in Sect.  4.3 . Double arrows indicate series of consecutive jumps. Here, first the stack of  m  particles at site  x  moves to  \(x_{t-1}\)  (type  (F) ), then the red particle jumps from site  \(x_{t-1}\)  to  y  (type  (S) ), and then the remaining stack of  \(m-1\)  particles at site  \(x_{t-1}\)  moves back to  x  (type  (B) ) Above, letters  (F) , ( S)  and  (B)  stand for  forward ,  single  and  backward , respectively. Let  \(\Omega ={\Omega _{x,y}^{\ell ,m,\sigma }}\)  denote the collection of all edges that appear in the aforementioned path from  \(\sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy}\)  to  \(\sigma _{m-1,\ell -m+1}^{xy}\) . Recall ( 4.5 ). In this subsection, we prove the following lemma: 
                   Lemma 4.5 
                   Assume that ( 4.11 ) holds, and suppose that  \(\ell \in \llbracket 1,k \rrbracket \) ,  \(m\in \llbracket 1,\ell \rrbracket \)  and  \(\sigma \in \Xi _{k-\ell }^{V\setminus \{x,y\}}\)  satisfy ( 4.20 ) and ( 4.21 ). Then, $$\begin{aligned} {\nabla _K^2 f ( \sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy}; x,y) } \le \frac{{6t \, m \left( \alpha _y + \ell -m\right) }}{ c_\textrm{min}\, \alpha _\textrm{min}\, \alpha _\textrm{ratio} } \left( \sum _{\{\zeta ,\zeta '\} {\in } \Omega } \nabla ^2f(\zeta ,\zeta ') \right) \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                
                   Proof 
                   Applying ( 4.14 ) along the path constructed above, we have the following upper bound for  \({\nabla _K^2 f ( \sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy}; x,y) }\) : $$\begin{aligned} \left( \sum _{\{\eta ,\zeta \} {\in } \Omega } \nabla ^2f(\eta ,\zeta )\right) \left( \sum _{\{\eta ,\zeta \} {\in } \Omega } \frac{ \mu _{\alpha ,k}(\sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy})\, m\left( \alpha _y + \ell -m\right) }{ \mathfrak {c}(\eta ,\zeta ) } \right) \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (4.22)
                 Here, we expand the term in the second parenthesis as done exactly in ( 4.19 ). Namely, referring to the definition of  \(\Omega \)  and ( 4.16 ), the term inside the second parenthesis in ( 4.22 ) expands as $$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned}&\sum _{s=1}^{t-1} \sum _{i=0}^{m-1} \frac{ \pi _x(m)\, m\left( \alpha _{y}+\ell -m\right) }{ \pi _{x_{s-1}}(m-i) \, \pi _{x_s} (i ) \, (m-i ) \left( \alpha _{x_s}+i \right) c_{x_{s-1} x_s}} + \frac{ \pi _x(m) }{ \pi _{x_{t-1}}(m )\, c_{x_{t-1}y} } \\&+ \sum _{s=1}^{t-1} \sum _{i=2}^{m} \frac{ \pi _x(m) \, m \left( \alpha _y + \ell -m\right) }{ \pi _{x_{s-1}}(m-i ) \, \pi _{x_{s}}(i-1 ) \left( i-1 \right) (\alpha _{x_{s-1}} + m-i ) \, c_{x_s x_{s-1}} } \; \text {.}\end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$ 
                    (4.23)
                 Here, the first double summation indicates the forward jumps in  (F) , the second single term indicates the jump in  (S) , and the last double summation indicates the backward jumps in  (B) . First, consider the first (double) summation in ( 4.23 ). According to ( 4.17 ) in Lemma  4.4 , $$\begin{aligned} \pi _x(m) \, m \le \alpha _x \, e^{\alpha _x \sum _{j=1}^{m-1} \frac{1}{j}} \; \text {,}\;\;\quad \pi _{x_{s-1}}(m-i) \, (m-i) \ge \alpha _{x_{s-1}} \; \text {,}\;\;\quad \pi _{x_s}(i) \, (\alpha _{x_s}+i) \ge \alpha _{x_s} \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ Since  \(c_{x_{s-1}x_s} \ge c_\textrm{min}\)  from ( 2.5 ), we may bound the first double summation in ( 4.23 ) from above by $$\begin{aligned} \frac{1}{c_\textrm{min}} \sum _{s=1}^{t-1} \sum _{i=0}^{m-1} \frac{ \alpha _x \, e^{\alpha _x \sum _{j=1}^{m-1} \frac{1}{j} } \left( \alpha _y + \ell -m \right) }{ \alpha _{x_{s-1}} \, \alpha _{x_s} } \le \frac{e \, {m \left( \alpha _y + \ell -m\right) (t-1) } }{ c_\textrm{min}\, \alpha _\textrm{min}\, \alpha _\textrm{ratio} } \; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (4.24)
