                 where we used that  \(\alpha _x \sum _{j=1}^{m-1} \frac{1}{j} \le 1\)  from ( 4.21 ),  \(\alpha _x / \alpha _{x_s} \le \alpha _\textrm{ratio}^{-1}\)  from ( 2.5 ), and  \(\alpha _{x_{s-1}} \ge \alpha _\textrm{min}\) . Next, the second term in ( 4.23 ) can be similarly bounded as $$\begin{aligned} \frac{ \pi _x(m) }{ \pi _{x_{t-1}}(m )\, c_{x_{t-1}y} } \le \frac{ \alpha _x \, e^{\alpha _x \sum _{j=1}^{m-1} \frac{1}{j} } }{ \alpha _{x_{t-1}} \, c_\textrm{min}} \le \frac{ e }{ c_\textrm{min} \,\alpha _\textrm{ratio} } {\le \frac{e \, m \left( \alpha _y + \ell -m\right) }{c_\textrm{min} \, \alpha _\textrm{ratio} \, \alpha _\textrm{min}} } \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (4.25)
                 Similarly, the third (double) summation in ( 4.23 ) is bounded by $$\begin{aligned} \frac{1}{c_\textrm{min}} \sum _{s=1}^{t-1} \sum _{i=2}^{m} \frac{ \alpha _x \, e^{\alpha _x \sum _{j=1}^{m-1} \frac{1}{j} } \left( \alpha _y + \ell -m \right) }{ \alpha _{x_{s-1}} \, \alpha _{x_s} } \le \frac{e \, {m \left( \alpha _y + \ell -m\right) (t-1) } }{ c_\textrm{min}\, \alpha _\textrm{min}\, \alpha _\textrm{ratio} } \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (4.26)
                 Collecting ( 4.23 ), ( 4.24 ), ( 4.25 ) and ( 4.26 ), and applying that  \(e \le 3\) , the term inside the second parenthesis in ( 4.22 ) is bounded by $$\begin{aligned} \frac{{9t \, m \left( \alpha _y + \ell -m\right) }}{ c_\textrm{min} \,\alpha _\textrm{min}\, \alpha _\textrm{ratio} } \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ This concludes the proof of Lemma  4.5 .  \(\square \) 
                 4.4  Decomposition of gradients: empty sites and many particles In this subsection, we assume that ( 4.11 ), ( 4.20 ) hold and that (cf. ( 4.21 )) $$\begin{aligned} \alpha _x \sum _{j=1}^{m-1} \frac{1}{j} >1 \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (4.27)
                 In this case, constructing a single one-dimensional path is insufficient to bound the term  \({\nabla _K^2 f ( \sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy}; x,y) }\)  properly. The main reason is that in the inequality in ( 4.24 ), we are not able to bound the term  \(e^{\alpha _x \sum _{j=1}^{m-1}\frac{1}{j} }\)  with a constant in this new regime ( 4.27 ). Having in mind that this term essentially comes from the mechanism that all  m  particles at  x  move together, we now avoid this obstacle by sending only a limited amount of particles along the path. This replacement costs us an additional  \(\alpha _x^{-1}\)  term in the upper bound, which is now manageable by the new condition  \(\alpha _x \sum _{j=1}^{m-1}\frac{1}{j} >1\) ; we refer to ( 4.39 ) for the exact place where this point is effectively exploited. Define a new integer  \(\widetilde{m}\)  as $$\begin{aligned} \widetilde{m}:=\left\lfloor \frac{m}{2} \right\rfloor \; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (4.28)
                 where  \(\lfloor a \rfloor \)  is the greatest integer less than or equal to  \(a \in \mathbb {R}\) . Then, we consider a two-dimensional system of paths from  \(\sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy}\)  to  \(\sigma _{m-1,\ell -m+1}^{xy}\)  as depicted in Fig.  3 . Fig. 3 Full size image Two-dimensional system of paths from  \(\sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy}\)  (top left) to  \(\sigma _{m-1,\ell -m+1}^{xy}\)  (top right) explained in Sect.  4.4 . Double arrows indicate series of consecutive paths. Moreover, the blue (resp. red) numbers indicate the corresponding value of the flow  \(\varphi \)  along the path in the vertical (resp. horizontal) direction, as defined in ( 4.31 ) and ( 4.32 ). The values are chosen so as to ensure that  \(\varphi \)  is a unit flow from  \(\sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy}\)  to  \(\sigma _{m-1,\ell -m+1}^{xy}\) In Fig.  3 , the downward vertical arrows starting from  \(\sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy}\)  in the top left corner denote the  \(\widetilde{m}\)  consecutive particle jumps from  x  to  \(x_1\) . Then, for  \(i \in \llbracket 1,\widetilde{m} \rrbracket \) , the  i -th bold arrow on the left, from  \(\sigma _{m-i,i,\ell -m}^{xx_1y}\)  to  \(\sigma _{m-i,i,\ell -m}^{xx_{t-1}y}\) , denote the movements of  i  particles from  \(x_{s-1}\)  to  \(x_s\)  consecutively for each  \(s\in \llbracket 2,t-1 \rrbracket \) , as in mechanism  (F)  in Sect.  4.3 . Next, the middle horizontal arrows denote the single particle jump from  \(x_{t-1}\)  to  y , corresponding to mechanism  (S)  in Sect.  4.3 . The bold arrows in the right part denote the movements of  \(i-1\)  particles from  \(x_{t-1}\)  back to  \(x_1\)  for each  \(i \in \llbracket 2,\widetilde{m} \rrbracket \)  (backwards), as in mechanism  (B)  in Sect.  4.3 . Finally, the upward vertical arrows below  \(\sigma _{m-1,\ell -m+1}^{xy}\)  in the top right corner denote the  \(\widetilde{m}-1\)  consecutive particle jumps from  \(x_1\)  to  x . The values attached to the arrows, roughly speaking, play the role of weights (encoded as the value of the flow, see below) for each move. As before, let  \(\Omega = \Omega _{x,y}^{\ell ,m,\sigma }\)  be the collection of all edges that appear in Fig.  3 . The objective of this subsection is to prove the following lemma: 
                   Lemma 4.6 
                   Assume that ( 4.11 ) holds, and suppose that  \(\ell \in \llbracket 1,k \rrbracket \) ,  \(m\in \llbracket 1,\ell \rrbracket \)  and  \(\sigma \in \Xi _{k-\ell }^{V\setminus \{x,y\}}\)  satisfy ( 4.20 ) and ( 4.27 ). Then, we have $$\begin{aligned} {\nabla _K^2 f ( \sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy}; x,y) } \le \frac{ {4t \, m \left( \alpha _y + \ell -m\right) } \, 6^{\alpha _\textrm{max} }}{\alpha _\textrm{min} \, \alpha _\textrm{ratio} \, c_\textrm{min}} \left( \sum _{\{\zeta ,\zeta '\} {\in } \Omega } \nabla ^2f(\zeta ,\zeta ') \right) \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                 To derive an estimate similar to ( 4.22 ) that works also in this two-dimensional setting, we make use of some potential-theoretic arguments. We present the statements as concisely as possible and refer the readers to, e.g., [ BH15 , Chapter 7] or [ Kim25 , Appendix A] for more details about the objects used in this subsection. Only in this subsection, we restrict the original system  \(\textrm{SIP}_k(G,\alpha )\)  to the subset of the configurations that appear in Fig.  3 , which we denote by  \(\hat{\Xi }\) . Moreover, let  \(\mathcal {D}_{\Omega }(g)\)  denote the (normalized) Dirichlet form in  \({\hat{\Xi }_k}\)  given by (cf. ( 4.8 )) $$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {D}_\Omega (g)= \frac{1}{2} \sum _{\eta ,\zeta \in {\hat{\Xi }_k}} \mu _{\alpha ,k}(\eta )\, r_{\alpha ,k}(\eta ,\zeta ) \left( g(\zeta )-g(\eta )\right) ^2 = \sum _{\{\eta ,\zeta \} {\in } \Omega } \nabla ^2g(\eta ,\zeta )\; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (4.29)
                 For non-empty disjoint subsets  A ,  B  of  \(\Omega \) , introduce (e.g., [ BH15 , Theorem 7.33] or [ Kim25 , Proposition A.1]) $$\begin{aligned} \textrm{cap}(A,B):=\inf \left\{ \mathcal {D}_\Omega (g): g\in \mathbb {R}^{{\hat{\Xi }_k}}\ \text {such that}\ g = 1\ \text {on}\ A\ \text {and}\ g = 0\ \text {on}\ B\right\} \; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ the capacity between  A  and  B . In particular, for  \(A=\{\sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy}\}\)  and  \(B=\{\sigma _{m-1,\ell -m+1}^{xy}\}\) , we have, after shifting and renormalizing, $$\begin{aligned} \textrm{cap}(\sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy},\sigma _{m-1,\ell -m+1}^{xy})\, (f(\sigma _{m-1,\ell -m+1}^{xy})-f(\sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy}))^{2} \le \mathcal {D}_\Omega (f) \; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (4.30)
                 where  \(f\in \mathbb {R}^{\Xi _k}\)  is the function which we fixed at the beginning of Sect.  4 . 
                   Proof of Lemma 4.6 
                   Let us describe the flow  \(\varphi \)  on  \({\hat{\Xi }_k}\)  represented in Fig.  3 , where we recall that “flow on  \(\Omega \) ” refers to any antisymmetric real-valued function on  \({\hat{\Xi }_k} \times {\hat{\Xi }_k}\) . Due to this antisymmetry, we shall omit to specify the value of  \(\varphi \)  when exchanging the arguments. Hence, vertically, define $$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \varphi (\sigma _{m-i,i,\ell -m}^{xx_1y},\sigma _{m-i-1,i+1,\ell -m}^{xx_1y})&:=\frac{ \sum _{j=i+1}^{\widetilde{m}} \frac{1}{j} }{ \sum _{j=1}^{\widetilde{m}} \frac{1}{j} }\; \text {,}\;\; &  \quad \text {for}\,\, i\in \llbracket 0,\widetilde{m}-1 \rrbracket \; \text {,}\;\;\\ \varphi (\sigma _{m-i-1,i,\ell -m+1}^{x{x_1}y},\sigma _{m-i,i-1,\ell -m+1}^{x{x_1}y})&:=\frac{ \sum _{j=i+1}^{\widetilde{m}} \frac{1}{j} }{ \sum _{j=1}^{\widetilde{m}} \frac{1}{j} }\; \text {,}\;\; &  \quad \text {for}\,\, i\in \llbracket 1,\widetilde{m}-1 \rrbracket \; \text {.}\end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$ 
                    (4.31)
                 Horizontally, define  \(\varphi \)  as a constant flow at each  i -th vertical level from  \(\sigma _{m-i,i,\ell -m}^{xx_1y}\)  to  \(\sigma _{m-i,i-1,\ell -m+1}^{xx_1y}\)  with value $$\begin{aligned} \frac{\frac{1}{i}}{ \sum _{j=1}^{\widetilde{m}} \frac{1}{j} }\; \text {,}\;\;\quad \text {for each}\,\, i \in \llbracket 1,\widetilde{m} \rrbracket \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (4.32)
                 The function  \(\varphi \)  is a unit flow from  \(\sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy}\)  to  \(\sigma _{m-1,\ell -m+1}^{xy}\) . Indeed, letting  \((\textrm{div}\,\varphi )(\eta )\)  denote the net divergence at  \(\eta \) , i.e.,  \((\textrm{div}\,\varphi )(\eta ) :=\sum _{\zeta \in \Omega } \varphi (\eta ,\zeta )\) , we have $$\begin{aligned} (\textrm{div}\,\varphi )(\sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy}) = 1 \; \text {,}\;\;\quad (\textrm{div}\,\varphi )(\sigma _{m-1,\ell -m+1}^{xy}) = -1 \quad \text {and} \quad \textrm{div}\,\varphi = 0 \quad \text {otherwise} \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ Thus, the standard Thomson principle (e.g., [ BH15 , Theorem 7.37] or [ Kim25 , Proposition A.2]) indicates that $$\begin{aligned} \textrm{cap}(\sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy},\sigma _{m-1,\ell -m+1}^{xy})\ge \frac{1}{\Vert \varphi \Vert ^{2}} \; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (4.33)
                 where  \(\Vert \varphi \Vert ^2\)  is the (square) flow norm given by $$\begin{aligned} \Vert \varphi \Vert ^2 :=\sum _{ {\{ \eta ,\zeta \} } \in \Omega } \frac{\varphi (\eta ,\zeta )^2}{\mathfrak {c}(\eta ,\zeta )} \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ Hence, by ( 4.5 ), ( 4.30 ) and ( 4.33 ), we obtain $$\begin{aligned} {\nabla _K^2 f ( \sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy}; x,y) } \le \mathcal {D}_\Omega (f)\, \mu _{\alpha ,k}(\sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy})\,m\left( \alpha _y+\ell -m\right) \Vert \varphi \Vert ^2 \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (4.34)
                 Next, we upper bound the right-hand side of ( 4.34 ), except  \(\mathcal {D}_\Omega (f)\) . For this purpose, we decompose  \(\varphi = \varphi _1 + \varphi _2\) , where  \(\varphi _1\)  (resp.  \(\varphi _2\) ) indicates the vertical (resp. horizontal) part of  \(\varphi \) . First, the (square) flow norm of the vertical part  \(\varphi _1\)  reads as $$\begin{aligned} \Vert \varphi _1\Vert ^2 = \sum _{i=0}^{\widetilde{m}-1} \frac{( \sum _{j=i+1}^{\widetilde{m}}\frac{1}{j} )^{2}/( \sum _{j=1}^{\widetilde{m}}\frac{1}{j} )^{2}}{ \mathfrak {c}(\sigma _{m-i,i,\ell -m}^{xx_{1}y},\sigma _{m-i-1,i+1,\ell -m}^{xx_{1}y}) } +\sum _{i=1}^{\widetilde{m}-1} \frac{( \sum _{j=i+1}^{\widetilde{m}} \frac{1}{j} )^{2}/( \sum _{j=1}^{\widetilde{m}}\frac{1}{j} )^{2}}{ \mathfrak {c}(\sigma _{m-i-1,i,\ell -m+1}^{x{x_1}y},\sigma _{m-i,i-1,\ell -m+1}^{x{x_1}y}) }\; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ Since the two numerators are clearly bounded by 1, we further get $$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned}&\mu _{\alpha ,k}(\sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy})\,m\left( \alpha _y+\ell -m\right) \Vert \varphi _1\Vert ^2 \\&\le \sum _{i=0}^{\widetilde{m}-1} \frac{ \mu _{\alpha ,k}(\sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy})\,m\left( \alpha _y+\ell -m\right) }{ \mathfrak {c}(\sigma _{m-i,i,\ell -m}^{xx_{1}y},\sigma _{m-i-1,i+1,\ell -m}^{xx_{1}y}) } +\sum _{i=1}^{\widetilde{m}-1} \frac{ \mu _{\alpha ,k}(\sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy})\,m\left( \alpha _y+\ell -m\right) }{ \mathfrak {c}(\sigma _{m-i-1,i,\ell -m+1}^{x{x_1}y},\sigma _{m-i,i-1,\ell -m+1}^{x{x_1}y}) }\; \text {.}\end{aligned}\nonumber \\ \end{aligned}$$ 
                    (4.35)
                 By the definitions in ( 4.8 ) and ( 4.16 ), the summand in the first summation in ( 4.35 ) becomes $$\begin{aligned} \frac{ \mu _{\alpha ,k}(\sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy})\,m\left( \alpha _y+\ell -m\right) }{ \mu _{\alpha ,k} (\sigma _{m-i,i,\ell -m}^{xx_{1}y}) \, r_{\alpha ,k} (\sigma _{m-i,i,\ell -m}^{xx_{1}y},\sigma _{m-i-1,i+1,\ell -m}^{xx_{1}y}) } = \frac{ \pi _x(m)\, m \left( \alpha _y + \ell - m\right) }{ \pi _x(m-i)\, \pi _{x_1}(i) \left( m-i\right) \left( \alpha _{x_1} + i \right) c_{xx_1} } \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ Thus, by applying ( 4.17 ) and ( 4.18 ), the first summation in ( 4.35 ) can be bounded from above by $$\begin{aligned}&\sum _{i=0}^{\widetilde{m}-1} \frac{ \pi _x(m)\, m \left( \alpha _y + \ell - m\right) }{ \pi _x(m-i)\, \pi _{x_1}(i) \left( m-i\right) \left( \alpha _{x_1} + i \right) c_{xx_1} } \\&\le \sum _{i=0}^{\widetilde{m}-1} e^{\alpha _x \sum _{j=m-i}^{m-1}\frac{1}{j} } \, \frac{\alpha _y + \ell -m}{ \alpha _{x_1} \, c_\textrm{min} } \le \frac{{\tilde{m} \left( \alpha _y + \ell -m\right) }}{\alpha _\textrm{min} \, c_\textrm{min} } \, e^{\alpha _x \sum _{j=m- \widetilde{m}+1}^{m-1}\frac{1}{j} } \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ Noting that  \(\sum _{j=m-\widetilde{m}+1}^{m-1}\frac{1}{j} \le \log \frac{m-1}{m-\widetilde{m}} \le \log 2\)  (cf. ( 4.28 )), we deduce that $$\begin{aligned} \sum _{i=0}^{\widetilde{m}-1} \frac{ \mu _{\alpha ,k}(\sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy})\,m\left( \alpha _y+\ell -m\right) }{ \mathfrak {c}(\sigma _{m-i,i,\ell -m}^{xx_{1}y},\sigma _{m-i-1,i+1,\ell -m}^{xx_{1}y}) } \le \frac{ {\tilde{m} \left( \alpha _y + \ell -m\right) } \, 2^{\alpha _\textrm{max}}}{\alpha _\textrm{min} \, c_\textrm{min} } \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (4.36)
                 Similarly, the second summation in ( 4.35 ) can be bounded by the same value in the right-hand side of ( 4.36 ). Thus, collecting ( 4.35 ) and ( 4.36 ) along with  \(2 \tilde{m} \le m\) , we deduce $$\begin{aligned} \mu _{\alpha ,k}(\sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy}) \, m \left( \alpha _y+\ell -m\right) \Vert \varphi _1 \Vert ^2 \le \frac{{m \left( \alpha _y + \ell -m\right) 2^{\alpha _\textrm{max}}}}{\alpha _\textrm{min} \, c_\textrm{min} } \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (4.37)
                 Next, the horizontal part  \(\mu _{\alpha ,k}(\sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy}) \, m \left( \alpha _y+\ell -m\right) \Vert \varphi _2\Vert ^2\)  becomes $$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned}&\sum _{i=1}^{\widetilde{m}} \sum _{s=2}^{t-1} \sum _{i'=0}^{i-1} \frac{ \mu _{\alpha ,k}(\sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy}) \,m \left( \alpha _y+\ell -m\right) \frac{1}{i^{2}}/( \sum _{j=1}^{\widetilde{m}}\frac{1}{j} )^{2} }{ \mathfrak {c}(\sigma _{m-i,i-i',i',\ell -m}^{xx_{s-1}x_{s}y},\sigma _{m-i,i-i'-1,i'+1,\ell -m}^{xx_{s-1}x_{s}y}) } \\&+ \sum _{i=1}^{\widetilde{m}}\frac{ \mu _{\alpha ,k}(\sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy}) \,m \left( \alpha _y+\ell -m\right) \frac{1}{i^{2}}/( \sum _{j=1}^{\widetilde{m}}\frac{1}{j} )^{2}}{\mathfrak {c}(\sigma _{m-i,i,\ell -m}^{xx_{t-1}y},\sigma _{m-i,i-1,\ell -m+1}^{xx_{t-1}y})} \\&+ \sum _{i=2}^{\widetilde{m}} \sum _{s=2}^{t-1} \sum _{i'=1}^{i-1}\frac{ \mu _{\alpha ,k}(\sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy}) \,m \left( \alpha _y+\ell -m\right) \frac{1}{i^{2}}/( \sum _{j=1}^{\widetilde{m}}\frac{1}{j} )^{2}}{\mathfrak {c}(\sigma _{m-i,i-i'-1,i',\ell -m+1}^{xx_{s-1}x_sy},\sigma _{m-i,i-i',i'-1,\ell -m+1}^{xx_{s-1}x_sy})} \; \text {.}\end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$ 
                    (4.38)
                 According to ( 4.8 ) and ( 4.16 ), the first (triple) summation in ( 4.38 ) equals $$\begin{aligned} \sum _{i=1}^{\widetilde{m}} \sum _{s=2}^{t-1} \sum _{i'=0}^{i-1} \frac{ \pi _{x}(m) \, m \left( \alpha _{y}+\ell -m\right) \frac{1}{i^{2}}/( \sum _{j=1}^{\widetilde{m}}\frac{1}{j} )^{2} }{ \pi _{x}(m-i) \, \pi _{x_{s-1}}(i-i' ) \, \pi _{x_{s}}(i' ) \left( i-i' \right) \left( \alpha _{x_{s}}+i' \right) \, c_{x_{s-1}x_{s}} } \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ By Lemma  4.4 ,  \(\pi _{x_{s-1}}(i-i') \, (i-i') \ge \alpha _{x_{s-1}} \ge \alpha _\textrm{min}\) ,  \(\pi _{x_s} (i') \, (\alpha _{x_s} + i') \ge \alpha _\textrm{min}\) , and $$\begin{aligned} \frac{\pi _x(m)\,m}{\pi _x(m-i)} \le (m-i) \, e^{\alpha _x \sum _{j=m-i}^{m-1} \frac{1}{j}} \le m \, e^{\alpha _x \sum _{j=m-i}^{m-1} \frac{1}{j}} \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ Substituting these to the penultimate display, along with  \(c_{x_{s-1}x_s} \ge c_\textrm{min}\) , the first (triple) summation in ( 4.38 ) is upper bounded by $$\begin{aligned} \frac{{m \left( \alpha _y + \ell -m\right) }}{\alpha _\textrm{min}^2 \, c_\textrm{min} } \sum _{i=1}^{\widetilde{m}} \sum _{s=2}^{t-1} \sum _{i'=0}^{i-1} \frac{e^{\alpha _x \sum _{j=m-i}^{m-1}\frac{1}{j} }}{i^{2} \, ( \sum _{j=1}^{\widetilde{m}}\frac{1}{j} )^{2}} \le \frac{ {m \left( \alpha _y + \ell -m\right) (t-2)} \, e^{\alpha _x \sum _{j=m-\widetilde{m}}^{m-1}\frac{1}{j} }}{ {\alpha _\textrm{min}^2 \, c_\textrm{min} } \sum _{j=1}^{\widetilde{m}}\frac{1}{j} } \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ Since  \(\sum _{j=m-\widetilde{m}}^{m-1}\frac{1}{j} \le 1+\log 2 < \log 6\)  and  \(\sum _{j=1}^{\widetilde{m}}\frac{1}{j} \ge \frac{1}{2} \sum _{j=1}^{m-1}\frac{1}{j}\)  (cf. ( 4.28 )), we may further bound the right-hand side with $$\begin{aligned} \frac{2 \, {m \left( \alpha _y + \ell -m\right) (t-2) } \, e^{\alpha _x \log 6 }}{\alpha _\textrm{min} \, \alpha _\textrm{ratio} \, c_\textrm{min} \, \alpha _x \sum _{j=1}^{m-1}\frac{1}{j} } \le \frac{2 \, {m \left( \alpha _y + \ell -m\right) (t-2) } \, 6^{\alpha _\textrm{max} }}{\alpha _\textrm{min} \, \alpha _\textrm{ratio} \, c_\textrm{min}} \; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (4.39)
                 where in the inequality we used condition ( 4.27 ). Similarly, the third (triple) summation in ( 4.38 ) is bounded by the same value in the right-hand side of ( 4.39 ). Finally, by the same ideas, the second summation in ( 4.38 ) is bounded from above by $$\begin{aligned} { \frac{m}{\alpha _\textrm{min} \, c_\textrm{min}} \sum _{i=1}^{\tilde{m}} \frac{6^{\alpha _\textrm{max}}}{i^2 \left( \sum _{j=1}^{\tilde{m}}\frac{1}{j}\right) ^2} \le \frac{ m \left( \alpha _y + \ell -m\right) 6^{\alpha _\textrm{max}}}{\alpha _\textrm{min}^2 \, c_\textrm{min} \sum _{j=1}^{\tilde{m}}\frac{1}{j} } \le \frac{ 2 \, m \left( \alpha _y + \ell -m\right) \, 6^{\alpha _\textrm{max}}}{\alpha _\textrm{min} \, c_\textrm{min} \, \alpha _\textrm{ratio} } \; \text {.}}\nonumber \\ \end{aligned}$$ 
                    (4.40)
                 At the first inequality we used  \(i^2 \ge i\)  and  \(\alpha _y + \ell -m \ge \alpha _\textrm{min}\) , and at the second inequality we used  \(\alpha _\textrm{min} \ge \alpha _\textrm{ratio} \, \alpha _x\)  and ( 4.27 ). Collecting ( 4.38 ), ( 4.39 ) and ( 4.40 ), we obtain $$\begin{aligned} \mu _{\alpha ,k}(\sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy}) \,m \left( \alpha _y+\ell -m \right) \Vert \varphi _2\Vert ^2 \le \frac{{(4t-6) \, m \left( \alpha _y + \ell -m\right) 6^{\alpha _\textrm{max} }}}{\alpha _\textrm{min} \, \alpha _\textrm{ratio} \, c_\textrm{min}} \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (4.41)
                 The constant 4 t  appears because the term in ( 4.39 ) should be counted twice to deal with the first and third lines in ( 4.38 ), and the term in ( 4.40 ) corresponds to the second line in ( 4.38 ), thus  \( 2 \times 2(t-2) + 2 = 4t-6\) . Since  \(\varphi _1\)  and  \(\varphi _2\)  have disjoint supports, we have  \(\Vert \varphi \Vert ^2 = \Vert \varphi _1\Vert ^2 + \Vert \varphi _2\Vert ^2\) . Therefore, by ( 4.37 ) and ( 4.41 ), since  \(\alpha _\textrm{ratio} \le 1\) , we conclude that $$\begin{aligned} \mu _{\alpha ,k}(\sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy}) \, m \left( \alpha _y+\ell -m \right) \Vert \varphi \Vert ^2 \le \frac{{4t \, m \left( \alpha _y + \ell -m\right) 6^{\alpha _\textrm{max}} }}{\alpha _\textrm{min} \, \alpha _\textrm{ratio} \, c_\textrm{min}} \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (4.42)
                 Collecting ( 4.29 ), ( 4.34 ) and ( 4.42 ), we arrive at conclusion of Lemma  4.6 .  \(\square \) 
                 4.5  General case and proof of Theorem  3.2 
                         Now, we handle the general case without the restrictions imposed in the previous four subsections. The idea is to send a particle from  x  to  y  along the sequence  \(x=x_{0},x_{1},\ldots ,x_{t}=y\)  by obeying the following two criteria: 
                   
                     If consecutive sites are occupied by particles in  \(\sigma \) , send a single particle along these occupied sites as explained in Sect.  4.2 . 
                   
                   
                     If consecutive sites are empty for the configuration  \(\sigma \) , follow the mechanism explained in Sects.  4.3  or  4.4  depending on the size of the stack of particles just before these empty sites. 
                   
                 To state this procedure in a rigorous manner, we decompose  \({\llbracket 0,t \rrbracket }\)  as $$\begin{aligned} &  \llbracket {0},b_{1}-1 \rrbracket \cup \llbracket b_{1},a_{2}-1 \rrbracket \cup \cdots \cup {\llbracket b_{r-1},a_{r}-1 \rrbracket \cup \llbracket a_{r},t \rrbracket } =:O_{1} \cup E_{1} \cup \cdots \nonumber \\ &  \quad \cup {E_{r-1} \cup O_{r}} \; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (4.43)
                 where  \(1\le b_{1}<a_{2}<b_{2}<\cdots< b_{r-1}<a_{r} \le t\)  and $$\begin{aligned} {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} \sigma _{x_{s}} \ge 1 & \text {if}\quad s\in O_{j} \ \text {for some} \ j\in \llbracket 1,r \rrbracket { {, \ \text {except for} \ s=t}} \\ \sigma _{x_{s}}=0 & \text {if}\quad s\in E_{j} \ \text {for some} \ j\in \llbracket 1,{r-1} \rrbracket \; \text {.}\end{array}\right. } \end{aligned}$$ Here, letter  O  (resp.  E ) stands for  occupied  (resp.  empty ). Also, let  \(a_{1} :=0\)  and  \({b_r} :=t+1\) . Now, the path from  \(\sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy}\)  to  \(\sigma _{m-1,\ell -m+1}^{xy}\)  is constructed as follows. Refer to Fig.  4  for a visual presentation of this recursive procedure.  [I] : 
                       Along  \(x_{a_j}, \ldots , x_{b_j-1}\)  for  \(j\in \llbracket 1,r \rrbracket \) : send a single particle from  \(x_{a_{j}}\)  to  \(x_{b_{j}-1}\)  consecutively, as explained in Sect.  4.2 . This would be a path from  \(\sigma _{m-1,1,\ell -m}^{xx_{a_j}y}\)  to  \(\sigma _{m-1,1,\ell -m}^{xx_{b_j-1}y}\) , where the first configuration for  \(j=1\)  is  \(\sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy}\)  and the second configuration for  \(j=r\)  is  \(\sigma _{m-1,\ell -m+1}^{xy}\) . Notice that this step is omitted if  \(b_j = a_j+1\) . 
                     [II] : 
                       Along  \(x_{b_j-1}, \ldots , x_{a_{j+1}}\)  for  \(j\in \llbracket 1,{r-1} \rrbracket \)  if  $$\begin{aligned}\alpha _{x_{b_j-1}} \sum _{j=1}^{\sigma _{x_{b_j-1}}}\frac{1}{j} \le 1 \;:\end{aligned}$$  we proceed as in Sect.  4.3 ; move all  \(\sigma _{x_{b_{j}-1}}+1\)  particles from  \(x_{b_{j}-1}\)  to  \(x_{a_{j+1}-1}\)  consecutively, move a single particle from  \(x_{a_{j+1}-1}\)  to  \(x_{a_{j+1}}\) , and then move back the remaining  \(\sigma _{x_{b_{j}-1}}\)  particles at  \(x_{a_{j+1}-1}\)  to  \(x_{b_{j}-1}\)  backwards. This is a path from  \(\sigma _{m-1,1,\ell -m}^{xx_{b_j-1}y}\)  to  \(\sigma _{m-1,1,\ell -m}^{xx_{a_{j+1}}y}\) . 
                     [III] : 
                       Along  \(x_{b_j-1}, \ldots , x_{a_{j+1}}\)  for  \(j\in \llbracket 1,{r-1} \rrbracket \)  if  $$\begin{aligned}\alpha _{x_{b_j-1}} \sum _{j=1}^{\sigma _{x_{b_j-1}}}\frac{1}{j} > 1 \;:\end{aligned}$$  we proceed as in Sect.  4.4 ; move each  \(i \in \llbracket 1, \lfloor (\sigma _{x_{b_{j}-1}}+1)/2\rfloor \rrbracket \)  particles from  \(x_{b_{j}-1}\)  to  \(x_{a_{j+1}-1}\)  consecutively, move a single particle from  \(x_{a_{j+1}-1}\)  to  \(x_{a_{j+1}}\) , and then move back the remaining  \(i-1\)  particles at  \(x_{a_{j+1}-1}\)  to  \(x_{b_{j}-1}\)  backwards. As in  [II] , this gives a path from  \(\sigma _{m-1,1,\ell -m}^{xx_{b_j-1}y}\)  to  \(\sigma _{m-1,1,\ell -m}^{xx_{a_{j+1}}y}\) . 
                     Fig. 4 Full size image Path from  \(\sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy}\)  to  \(\sigma _{m-1,\ell -m+1}^{xy}\)  in the general case explained in Sect.  4.5 . The figure explains how the red particle moves from  \(x_0=x\)  to  \(x_t=y\)  along the path. First, in  \(O_1\)  where the sites are occupied, the red particle simply jumps consecutively to the right as explained in Sect.  4.2 . Next, in  \(E_1\)  where the sites are empty and the initial stack is small, we follow the mechanism presented in Sect.  4.3 . In  \(O_2\)  where the sites are again occupied, we proceed as in Sect.  4.2 . In  \(E_2\)  where the sites are empty and the initial stack is big, we follow the two-dimensional collection of paths explained in Sect.  4.4 . We iterate these procedures until the red particle arrives at  y Again, denote by  \({\Omega _{x,y}^{\ell ,m,\sigma }}\)  the collection of all edges that appear along the paths. Proof of ( 4.3 ) First, we bound  \({\nabla _K^2 f ( \sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy}; x,y) }\)  from above by intermediate quadratic forms, on which we can apply the special results in Sects.  4.1 – 4.4 . Recall the decomposition ( 4.43 ). The path  \(x \rightarrow y\)  can be decomposed into (at most)  \(2r-1\)  steps $$\begin{aligned} x = x_0 \rightarrow x_{b_1-1} \rightarrow x_{a_2} \rightarrow x_{b_2-1} \rightarrow \cdots \rightarrow x_{a_r} \rightarrow x_t = y \; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ where step  \(x_{a_j} \rightarrow x_{b_j-1}\)  must be omitted if  \(b_j = a_j +1\) . In this regard, we may decompose as $$\begin{aligned} \Omega _{x,y}^{\ell ,m,\sigma } = \bigcup _{j=1}^r \Omega _{x_{a_j},x_{b_j-1}}^{\ell ,m,\sigma } \cup \bigcup _{j=1}^{r-1} \Omega _{x_{b_j-1},x_{a_{j+1}}}^{\ell ,m,\sigma } \end{aligned}$$ 
                    (4.44)
                 where, with a slight abuse of notation, each  \(\Omega _{x_{a_j},x_{b_j-1}}^{\ell ,m,\sigma }\)  (resp.  \(\Omega _{x_{b_j-1},x_{a_{j+1}}}^{\ell ,m,\sigma }\) ) collects the edges that are obtained within the step  \(x_{a_j} \rightarrow x_{b_j-1}\)  (resp.  \(x_{b_j-1} \rightarrow x_{a_{j+1}}\) ). We will upper bound the term  \({\nabla _K^2 f ( \sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy}; x,y) }\)  with $$\begin{aligned} {\nabla _K^2 f ( \sigma _{m-1,1,\ell -m}^{xx_{a_1}y}; x_{a_1},x_{b_1-1}) }, {\nabla _K^2 f ( \sigma _{m-1,1,\ell -m}^{xx_{b_1-1}y}; x_{b_1-1},x_{a_2}) }, \dots , {\nabla _K^2 f ( \sigma _{m-1,1,\ell -m}^{xx_{a_r}y}; x_{a_r},x_{b_r-1}) } \end{aligned}$$ where  \(x_{a_1}=x_0=x\)  and  \(x_{b_r-1}=x_t=y\) . The Cauchy–Schwarz inequality applied to the difference of  f  along these steps implies that $$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned}&(f(\sigma _{m-1,\ell -m+1}^{xy})-f(\sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy}))^{2} \le \\&t \left( \sum _{j=1}^r \frac{(f(\sigma _{m-1,1,\ell -m}^{xx_{b_j-1}y})-f(\sigma _{m-1,1,\ell -m}^{xx_{a_j}y}))^{2}}{b_j - a_j - 1} + \sum _{j=1}^{r-1} \frac{(f(\sigma _{m-1,1,\ell -m}^{xx_{a_{j+1}}y})-f(\sigma _{m-1,1,\ell -m}^{xx_{b_j-1}y}))^{2}}{a_{j+1} - b_j +1} \right) \; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$ where it was used that  \(\sum _{j=1}^r (b_j-a_j-1) + \sum _{j=1}^{r-1} (a_{j+1}-b_j+1) = b_r - a_1 - 1 = t\) . Here, the first summation in  \(j \in \llbracket 1,r \rrbracket \)  should be run over those  j  such that  \(b_j > a_j+1\) . Substituting ( 4.5 ) to this inequality, we obtain that $$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \nabla _K^2 f ( \sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy}; x,y) \le t \, \Bigg ( \sum _{j=1}^r&\frac{ \mathfrak {c}_K (\sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy}; x,y) \, \nabla _K^2 f ( \sigma _{m-1,1,\ell -m}^{xx_{a_j}y}; x_{a_j},x_{b_j-1}) }{\mathfrak {c}_K(\sigma _{m-1,1,\ell -m}^{xx_{a_j}y};x_{a_j},x_{b_j-1}) (b_j-a_j-1)} \\&+ \sum _{j=1}^{r-1} \frac{ \mathfrak {c}_K (\sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy}; x,y) \, \nabla _K^2 f ( \sigma _{m-1,1,\ell -m}^{xx_{b_j-1}y}; x_{b_j-1},x_{a_{j+1}}) }{\mathfrak {c}_K(\sigma _{m-1,1,\ell -m}^{xx_{b_j-1}y};x_{b_j-1},x_{a_{j+1}}) (a_{j+1} - b_j + 1)} \Bigg ) \, \; \text {.}\end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$ 
                    (4.45)
                 According to the results in Lemmas  4.2 ,  4.3 ,  4.5 , and  4.6 , for each  \(j \in \llbracket 1,r \rrbracket \)  we have $$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned}&\frac{\mathfrak {c}_K (\sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy}; x,y) \, \nabla _K^2 f ( \sigma _{m-1,1,\ell -m}^{xx_{a_j}y}; x_{a_j},x_{b_j-1}) }{\mathfrak {c}_K(\sigma _{m-1,1,\ell -m}^{xx_{a_j}y};x_{a_j},x_{b_j-1}) (b_j-a_j-1)} \le \\&\frac{ \mu _{\alpha ,k}(\sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy}) \, 9 \, m \left( \alpha _y + \ell -m\right) 6^{\alpha _\textrm{max} }}{ \mu _{\alpha ,k} (\sigma _{m-1,1,\ell -m}^{xx_{a_j}y}) \, \alpha _\textrm{min} \, \alpha _\textrm{ratio} \, c_\textrm{min}} \left( \sum _{\{\zeta ,\zeta '\} {\in } \Omega _{x_{a_j},x_{b_j-1}}^{\ell ,m,\sigma }} \nabla ^2f(\zeta ,\zeta ') \right) \; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$ and for each  \(j \in \llbracket 1,j-1 \rrbracket \)  we have $$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned}&\frac{\mathfrak {c}_K (\sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy}; x,y) \, \nabla _K^2 f ( \sigma _{m-1,1,\ell -m}^{xx_{b_j-1}y}; x_{b_j-1},x_{a_{j+1}}) }{\mathfrak {c}_K(\sigma _{m-1,1,\ell -m}^{xx_{b_j-1}y};x_{b_j-1},x_{a_{j+1}}) (a_{j+1}-b_j+1)} \le \\&\frac{ \mu _{\alpha ,k}(\sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy}) \, 9 \, m \left( \alpha _y + \ell -m\right) 6^{\alpha _\textrm{max} }}{ \mu _{\alpha ,k} (\sigma _{m-1,1,\ell -m}^{xx_{b_j-1}y}) \, \alpha _\textrm{min} \, \alpha _\textrm{ratio} \, c_\textrm{min}} \left( \sum _{\{\zeta ,\zeta '\} {\in } \Omega _{x_{b_j-1},x_{a_{j+1}}}^{\ell ,m,\sigma }} \nabla ^2f(\zeta ,\zeta ') \right) \; \text {.}\end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$ Indeed, the term  t  in the right-hand sides of the lemmas cancels out with  \(b_j-a_j-1\)  or  \(a_{j+1}-b_j+1\) , and the term  \(m \, (\alpha _y + \ell -m)\)  cancels out with the  \(\mathfrak {c}_K\) -term except the  \(\mu _{\alpha ,k}\)  part, thus we obtain the right-hand sides in the last two displays. Next, we estimate the ratios of  \(\mu _{\alpha ,k}\)  that appear in the right-hand sides of the last two displays. If  \(a_j \ne x,y\) , then $$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned}&\frac{ \mu _{\alpha ,k}(\sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy}) \, m \left( \alpha _y + \ell -m\right) }{ \mu _{\alpha ,k} (\sigma _{m-1,1,\ell -m}^{xx_{a_j}y}) } = \frac{\pi _x(m) \, m \, \pi _{x_{a_j}} (\sigma _{x_{a_j}}) \, \left( \alpha _y + \ell -m\right) }{\pi _x(m-1) \,\pi _{x_{a_j}}(\sigma _{x_{a_j}}+1) } \\&= (\alpha _x + m-1) \left( \alpha _y + \ell -m\right) \, \frac{\sigma _{x_{a_j}}+1}{\sigma _{x_{a_j}}+\alpha _{x_{a_j}}} \le 2 \, (1+\alpha _\textrm{max}) \, k \, (\alpha _\textrm{max} + k-1) \; \text {.}\end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$ Here, we used  \(\sigma _{x_{a_j}} \ge 1\)  (which implies  \(\frac{\sigma _{x_{a_j}}+1}{\sigma _{x_{a_j}}+\alpha _{x_{a_j}}} \le 2\) ),  \(\alpha _y + \ell -m \le \alpha _\textrm{max} + k-1\) , and  \(\alpha _x + m-1 \le (1+\alpha _\textrm{max}) \, k\) . The same bound trivially holds if  \(a_j = x\)  or  \(a_j = y\) , and similarly, $$\begin{aligned} \frac{ \mu _{\alpha ,k}(\sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy}) \, m \left( \alpha _y + \ell -m\right) }{ \mu _{\alpha ,k} (\sigma _{m-1,1,\ell -m}^{xx_{b_j-1}y}) } \le 2 \, (1+\alpha _\textrm{max}) \, k \, (\alpha _\textrm{max} + k-1) \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ Substituting the last four estimates to ( 4.45 ), we arrive at $$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \nabla _K^2 f ( \sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy}; x,y)&\le \frac{ 18t \, (1+\alpha _\textrm{max}) \, k \, (\alpha _\textrm{max} + k-1) \, 6^{\alpha _\textrm{max} }}{ \alpha _\textrm{min} \, \alpha _\textrm{ratio} \, c_\textrm{min}} \\&\left( \sum _{j=1}^r \sum _{\{\zeta ,\zeta '\} {\in } \Omega _{x_{a_j},x_{b_j-1}}^{\ell ,m,\sigma }} \nabla ^2f(\zeta ,\zeta ') + \sum _{j=1}^{r-1} \sum _{\{\zeta ,\zeta '\} {\in } \Omega _{x_{b_j-1},x_{a_{j+1}}}^{\ell ,m,\sigma }} \nabla ^2f(\zeta ,\zeta ') \right) \, \; \text {.}\end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$ Applying  \(t \le \textrm{diam}(G)\) ,  \(1+\alpha _\textrm{max} \le e^{\alpha _\textrm{max}}\) , and ( 4.44 ), we get, for any  \(\ell \in \llbracket 1,k \rrbracket \) ,  \(m\in \llbracket 1,\ell \rrbracket \)  and  \(\sigma \in \Xi _{k-\ell }^{V\setminus \{x,y\}}\) , $$\begin{aligned} {\nabla _K^2 f ( \sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy}; x,y) } \le \frac{{18 \, k \left( \alpha _\textrm{max}+k-1\right) \textrm{diam}(G) \, (6e)^{\alpha _\textrm{max} }}}{\alpha _\textrm{min} \, \alpha _\textrm{ratio} \, c_\textrm{min}} \sum _{\{\zeta ,\zeta '\} {\in } {\Omega _{x,y}^{\ell ,m,\sigma }}} \nabla ^2f(\zeta ,\zeta ') \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ Along with ( 4.6 ), this implies $$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {E}_K^{x,y} \le \frac{{18 \, k \left( \alpha _\textrm{max}+k-1\right) \textrm{diam}(G) \, (6e)^{\alpha _\textrm{max} }}}{\alpha _\textrm{min} \, \alpha _\textrm{ratio} \, c_\textrm{min}} \sum _{\ell =1}^k \sum _{m=1}^\ell \sum _{\sigma \in \Xi _{k-\ell }^{V\setminus \{x,y\}}} \sum _{\{\zeta ,\zeta '\} {\in } {\Omega _{x,y}^{\ell ,m,\sigma }}} \nabla ^2f(\zeta ,\zeta ') \; \text {.}\nonumber \\ \end{aligned}$$ 
                    (4.46)
                 Thus, to conclude the proof of ( 4.3 ), according to ( 4.9 ) and ( 4.46 ), we only need to count the number of  overlaps , i.e., the number of triples  \((\ell ,m,\sigma )\)  in ( 4.46 ) that produce the same Dirichlet summand  \(\nabla ^2f(\eta ,\eta -\delta _z+\delta _w)\)  in ( 4.9 ). To this end, we fix  \(x<y\) , the sequence  \(x=x_0, x_1, \dots , x_t=y\)  with  \(t\in \llbracket 1,\textrm{diam}(G) \rrbracket \) , and a triple  \((\eta ,z,w)\)  in ( 4.9 ), and then count the number of triples  \((\ell ,m,\sigma )\)  that produce the term  \(\nabla ^2f(\eta ,\eta -\delta _z + \delta _w)\)  in ( 4.46 ) by distinguishing three cases. (Case 1)  There exists no  \(s \in \llbracket 1,t \rrbracket \)  for which  \(\{ x_{s-1},x_s \} = \{ z,w \}\) : Clearly, in this case the overlap is  zero . (Case 2)  There exists  \(s\in \llbracket 1,t \rrbracket \)  for which  \(( x_{s-1},x_s )= (z,w)\) : By the minimal property of the path length  t , there is no other  \(s' \in \llbracket 1,t \rrbracket \)  such that  \(\{x_{s'-1},x_{s'}\}=\{z,w\}\) , thus we may only focus on the particle jump  \(z \rightarrow w\) . First, we observe that if  \(t=1\) , then the overlap is at most  one . Thus, we only focus on the case  \(t \ge 2\) . We further divide into three subcases according to the type— [I] ,  [II]  and  [III] —of the particle jump  \(z \rightarrow w\) . 
                   
                     (Case 2.I)   \(\eta \rightarrow \eta -\delta _z + \delta _w\)  belongs to type  [I] : In this case, recalling that the overall mechanism is to move a single particle from  x  to  y  along the path, we obtain  $$\begin{aligned} \eta = \sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy} + ( \delta _z - \delta _x ) \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$  This observation characterizes  \((\ell ,m,\sigma )\)  uniquely from the configuration  \(\eta \) . Hence, in this subcase, there is exactly  one  overlap. 
                   
                   
                     (Case 2.II)   \(\eta \rightarrow \eta -\delta _z + \delta _w\)  belongs to type  [II] : Recall that the mechanism in Sect.  4.3  is further divided into types  (F) ,  (S)  and  (B) , where type  (B)  is impossible here since the particle jumps forward. Moreover, this  [II] -mechanism is happening in  \(x_{s'}, \dots , x_{s-1},x_s,\dots , x_{s''}\)  with  \(s' \le s-1 < s \le s''\)  such that Footnote  1 $$\begin{aligned} \sigma _{x_{s'}} \ge 1 \; \text {,}\;\;\quad \sigma _{x_{s'+1}} = \cdots = \sigma _{x_{s''-1}} = 0 \quad \text {and} \quad \sigma _{x_{s''}} \ge 1 \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$  First, suppose that  \(x_{s'}\ne x\) . If the jump  \(\eta \rightarrow \eta -\delta _z + \delta _w\)  is of type  (F) , then  $$\begin{aligned}&\eta + ( \eta _w \delta _z - \eta _w \delta _w ) \\&\quad = \sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy} + (\delta _{x_{s'}} - \delta _x) + ( (\sigma _{x_{s'}}+1) \delta _z - (\sigma _{x_{s'}}+1) \delta _{x_{s'}} ) \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$  Note that the jump  \(\eta \rightarrow \eta -\delta _z + \delta _w\)  happens within the  \(\eta _z + \eta _w\)  jumps of the same number of particles from  z  to  w . Thus, the left-hand side configuration  \(\eta + (\eta _w \delta _z - \eta _w \delta _w)\)  detects the moment right before when the particle jumps  \(z \rightarrow w\)  are about to happen. Then, the right-hand side configuration is the corresponding configuration represented in terms of  \((\ell ,m,\sigma )\)  due to the following logic. First, from  \(\sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy}\) , a particle would have traveled from  x  to  \(x_{s'}\) , giving the contribution  \(\delta _{x_{s'}} - \delta _x\) . Then, the whole  \(\sigma _{x_{s'}}+1\)  particles at  \(x_{s'}\)  would have traveled all together from  \(x_{s'}\)  to  z , which gives the contribution  \((\sigma _{x_{s'}}+1)(\delta _z - \delta _{x_{s'}})\) . This instance is exactly right before when the jumps  \(z \rightarrow w\)  are initiated, giving the equality in the display. Thus,  \(\sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy}\)  has a unique representation in terms of  \(x,y,\eta ,z,w\) , thus the triple  \((\ell ,m,\sigma )\)  is determined uniquely. More precisely,  m  is exactly the number of particles at  x ,  \(\ell \)  is the number of particles at both  x  and  y , and  \(\sigma \)  is the remaining configuration on  \(V \setminus \{x,y\}\) . On the other hand, if  \(\eta \rightarrow \eta -\delta _z +\delta _w\)  is of type  (S) , then  \(s=s''\) , and we have  $$\begin{aligned} \eta = \sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy} + (\delta _{x_{s'}} - \delta _x) + ( (\sigma _{x_{s'}}+1) \delta _z - (\sigma _{x_{s'}}+1) \delta _{x_{s'}} ) \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$  Here, both sides detect the instance right before when the single jump  \(z \rightarrow w\)  occurs, which is clearly  \(\eta \)  in the left-hand side and also the configuration in the right-hand side by the same logic as explained above. Hence, the triple  \((\ell ,m,\sigma )\)  is again determined uniquely in this case. Thus, we have two possibilities of triples  \((\ell ,m,\sigma )\) . Now, suppose that  \(x_{s'}=x\) . Then, we may similarly detect two possibilities:  $$\begin{aligned} \eta + ( \eta _w \delta _z - \eta _w \delta _w ) = \sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy} + ( m \delta _z - m \delta _{x} ) \end{aligned}$$  for type  (F) , and  $$\begin{aligned} \eta = \sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy} + ( m \delta _z - m \delta _{x} ) \end{aligned}$$  for type  (S) . They both yield a unique triple  \((\ell ,m,\sigma )\) . Therefore, we conclude that  (Case 2.II)  gives rise to at most  two  overlaps. 
                   
                   
                     (Case 2.III)   \(\eta \rightarrow \eta -\delta _z + \delta _w\)  belongs to type  [III] : As done in  (Case 2.II) , we fix  \(x_{s'}, \dots , x_{s-1}, x_s, \dots , x_{s''}\)  on which this  [III] -mechanism takes place. A forward jump in type  [III]  is of one of the following three types: initial distribution of  \(i \in \llbracket 1,\widetilde{m} \rrbracket \)  particles  \(x_{s'} \rightarrow x_{s'+1}\)  (downward arrows in Fig.  3 ), movement of  i  particles in the bulk (double horizontal arrows in the left half of Fig.  3 ), or the single particle jump  \(x_{s''-1} \rightarrow x_{s''}\)  (single horizontal arrows in the middle part of Fig.  3 ). Each type determines a triple  \((\ell ,m,\sigma )\)  uniquely, as thoroughly explained in  (Case 2.II) . Thus, we omit the details here and conclude that no more than  three  overlaps are possible in  (Case 2.III) . 
                   
                 Collecting these three subcases,  (Case 2)  admits at most  six  overlaps. (Case 3)  There exists  \(s\in \llbracket 1,t \rrbracket \)  for which  \(( x_{s-1},x_s )= (w,z)\) : The analysis in this third case is almost identical to the one done in  (Case 2) . Thus, we choose not to repeat the tedious computations and record here that, also in this case, at most  six  overlaps are possible in  (Case 3) . Finally, given  \(x<y\)  and  \((\eta ,z,w)\) , collecting  (Case 1) ,  (Case 2)  and  (Case 3)  ensure that there are at most  six  possible overlaps of triples  \((\ell ,m,\sigma )\) . Therefore, by ( 4.9 ), we deduce $$\begin{aligned} \sum _{\ell =1}^k \sum _{m=1}^\ell \sum _{\sigma \in \Xi _{k-\ell }^{V \setminus \{x,y\} }} \sum _{\{\zeta ,\zeta '\} {\in } {\Omega _{x,y}^{\ell ,m,\sigma }}} \nabla ^2f(\zeta ,\zeta ') \le 6 \, \mathcal {E}\; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ Combining this with ( 4.46 ), the proof of ( 4.3 ) is now completed.  \(\square \) 
                   Remark 4.7 
                   The full combination of the three types  [I] ,  [II]  and  [III]  is needed to obtain the correct dependence on  \(\alpha _\textrm{min}\)  in Theorem  3.2 . Alternatively, we may combine only two of those types (that is,  [I]  +  [II]  or  [I]  +  [III] ) to obtain different bounds. Here, we briefly record these new results for potential applications in future works. 
                   Recall that we collected ( 4.24 ), ( 4.25 ), and ( 4.26 ) to obtain the exact bound in Lemma  4.5 . If we bound  \({\alpha _x \sum _{j=1}^{m-1} \frac{1}{j}}\)  from above by  \({\alpha _\textrm{max} \left( 1+\log k\right) }\)  rather than by 1, we obtain an alternative bound $$\begin{aligned} {\nabla _K^2 f ( \sigma _{m,\ell -m}^{xy}; x,y) } \le {3 \, e^{\alpha _\textrm{max}(1+\log k)} } \, \frac{{m\left( \alpha _y + \ell -m\right) t }}{ c_\textrm{min}\, \alpha _\textrm{min}\, \alpha _\textrm{ratio} } \left( \sum _{\{\zeta ,\zeta '\} {\in } \Omega } \nabla ^2f(\zeta ,\zeta ') \right) \; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ which does not require ( 4.21 ). Combining this inequality with Lemma  4.3  and applying the same logic explained in that subsection, we get $$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {E}_{K} \le \frac{ C \, e^{\alpha _\textrm{max}(1+\log k)} \, k \left( \alpha _\textrm{max}+k-1\right) |V|^2 \, \textrm{diam}(G) \, 2^{\alpha _\textrm{max} }}{\alpha _\textrm{min} \, c_\textrm{min} \, \alpha _\textrm{ratio} } \, \mathcal {E}\; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ where  \(C>0\)  is a global constant. For simplicity, if we take  \(\alpha =\varepsilon \hat{\alpha }\)  as in ( 2.8 ), take  \(\varepsilon \rightarrow 0\) , and toss the dependence on  G  and  \(\hat{\alpha }\)  to the constants, this reduces to $$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {E}_K \le \frac{ C_{G,\hat{\alpha }} \, e^{C_{G,\hat{\alpha }} \,\varepsilon \log k} \, k^2}{\varepsilon } \, \mathcal {E}\quad \text {as} \ \varepsilon \rightarrow 0 \; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ where  \(C_{G,\hat{\alpha }}>0\)  now depends on  \(G,\hat{\alpha }\)  but not on  \(k,\varepsilon \) . Similarly, if we do not lower bound  \(\alpha _x \sum _{j=1}^{m-1} \frac{1}{j}\)  in ( 4.39 ) by 1 and repeat the same logic as above, we obtain a different bound $$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {E}_{K} \le \frac{ C \, k \left( \alpha _\textrm{max}+k-1\right) |V|^2 \, \textrm{diam}(G) \, 6^{\alpha _\textrm{max} }}{\alpha _\textrm{min}^2 \, c_\textrm{min} \, \alpha _\textrm{ratio} \log k } \, \mathcal {E}\; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ which then implies, in the asymptotic regime ( 2.8 ), $$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {E}_K \le \frac{ C_{G,\hat{\alpha }} \, k^2}{\varepsilon ^2 \log k} \, \mathcal {E}\quad \text {as} \ \varepsilon \rightarrow 0 \; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ with different constants  \(C>0\)  (global) and  \(C_{G,\hat{\alpha }}>0\)  (depends only on  \(G,\hat{\alpha }\) ). 
                 5  Proofs of Theorems  2.3  and  2.4 
                      In this section, we prove Theorems  2.3  and  2.4 , following the outline of Sect.  2.4.2 . The basic starting idea is to split the infinitesimal generator  \(L_{G,\alpha ,k}\)  into two parts: a slow part and a fast part. In view of this decomposition, standard limit theorems for slow–fast systems ensure that, in the small-diffusivity limit,  \(\mathrm SIP\)  is well approximated by the slow dynamics, after a suitable projection/thermalization according to the fast one. Understanding how and when this projection affects  \(\textrm{SIP}\) ’s spectral gap is the main non-trivial task of this section. Recall from ( 2.8 ) that we assume  \(\alpha = \varepsilon \hat{\alpha }\) , where  \(\hat{\alpha }=(\hat{\alpha }_x)_{x\in V}\)  are fixed site weights and  \(\varepsilon \)  tends to 0. Finally, since the graph  G  is fixed all throughout the section, we often drop it from the notation, and simply write, e.g.,  \(\textrm{SIP}_k(\alpha )=\textrm{SIP}_k(G,\alpha )\)  and  \(L_{\alpha ,k}=L_{G,\alpha ,k}\) . 5.1  Slow and fast dynamics Fix  \(k\ge 2\) , and consider  \(\textrm{SIP}_k(\varepsilon \hat{\alpha })\)  with time sped up by a factor  \(\varepsilon ^{-1}\) . Recalling ( 2.1 ), the corresponding generator is then simply given by  \(\varepsilon ^{-1}L_{\alpha ,k}= \varepsilon ^{-1}L_{\varepsilon \hat{\alpha },k}\) . By separating the  \(\varepsilon \) -dependent terms from the rest, we obtain $$\begin{aligned} \varepsilon ^{-1} L_{\varepsilon \hat{\alpha },k}= A_{\hat{\alpha },k} +\varepsilon ^{-1} B_k\; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (5.1)
                 where, for all  \(f \in \mathbb {R}^{\Xi _k}\)  and  \(\eta \in \Xi _k\) , $$\begin{aligned} A_{\hat{\alpha },k} f(\eta )&:=\sum _{x,y\in V}c_{xy}\, \eta _x \hat{\alpha }_y \left( {f(\eta -\delta _x+\delta _y)-f(\eta )} \right) \; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (5.2)
                 $$\begin{aligned} B_k f(\eta )&:=\sum _{x, y \in V}c_{xy}\, \eta _x \eta _y \left( f(\eta -\delta _x+\delta _y)-f(\eta )\right) \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (5.3)
                 Let  \((\mathcal {A}_{\hat{\alpha },k}(t))_{t\ge 0}\)  and  \((\mathcal {B}_k(t))_{t\ge 0}\)  denote the Markov chains in  \(\Xi _k\)  generated by  \(A_{\hat{\alpha },k}\)  and  \(B_k\) , respectively. More in detail,  \((\mathcal {A}_{\hat{\alpha },k}(t))_{t\ge 0}\)  describes  k  independent particles on  G , while  \((\mathcal {B}_k(t))_{t\ge 0}\)  is a particle system with absorbing set $$\begin{aligned} \Omega _k:=\left\{ \eta \in \Xi _k: c_{xy}\, \eta _x\eta _y=0 \ \text {for all}\ x, y \in V\right\} \; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (5.4)
                 and transient set  \(\Delta _k:=\Xi _k\setminus \Omega _k\) , i.e., $$\begin{aligned} \Delta _k:=\left\{ \eta \in \Xi _k: c_{xy}\, \eta _x\eta _y>0\ \text {for some}\ x, y \in V\right\} \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (5.5)
                 Let  \(\Pi _k: \mathbb {R}^{\Xi _k}\rightarrow \mathbb {R}^{\Xi _k}\)  denote the  \(B_k\) -harmonic projection operator, i.e., $$\begin{aligned} \Pi _k f:=\lim _{t\rightarrow \infty } e^{t B_k}f \; \text {,}\;\;\quad f \in \mathbb {R}^{\Xi _k}\; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ Remark that the range of  \(\Pi _k\) , written as  \(\mathcal {R}(\Pi _k)\subseteq \mathbb {R}^{\Xi _k}\) , is a  \(|\Omega _k|\) -dimensional subspace. More precisely, since  \(\Omega _k\)  is the absorbing set of  \(B_k\) , one has, for all  \(f\in \mathbb {R}^{\Xi _k}\) ,  \(\Pi _k f=f\)  on  \(\Omega _k\) , and the values of  \(\Pi _k f\)  on  \(\Delta _k\)  are fully determined by the absorption probabilities on  \(\Omega _k\) . Thus, restriction to  \(\Omega _k\)  gives a canonical linear isomorphism  \(\mathbb {R}^{\Xi _k}\supseteq \mathcal {R}(\Pi _k)\cong \mathbb {R}^{\Omega _k}\) , and  \(\mathcal {R}(\Pi _k)\)  consists of  \(B_k\) -harmonic extensions to  \(\mathbb {R}^{\Xi _k}\)  of functions in  \(\mathbb {R}^{\Omega _k}\) . The following result builds on a powerful theorem [ Kur73 , Theorem 2.1] (see also [ EK86 , §1, Theorem 7.6]) on limit theorems for Markovian slow–fast systems. 
                   Proposition 5.1 
                   The operator  \({\mathscr {G}}_{\hat{\alpha },k}:=\Pi _k A_{\hat{\alpha },k}|_{\mathcal {R}(\Pi _k)}:\mathcal {R}(\Pi _k)\rightarrow \mathcal {R}(\Pi _k)\)  is an infinitesimal generator on  \(\mathcal {R}(\Pi _k) \subseteq \mathbb {R}^{\Xi _k}\) . Moreover, we have the following convergence: $$\begin{aligned} \lim _{\varepsilon \rightarrow 0} \max _{\eta \in \Xi _k}\left| e^{t(A_{\hat{\alpha },k}+\varepsilon ^{-1}B_k)} f(\eta ) - e^{t{{\mathscr {G}}_{\hat{\alpha },k}}} \Pi _k f(\eta ) \right| = 0 \; \text {,}\;\;\quad t>0\; \text {,}\;\;f \in \mathbb {R}^{\Xi _k} \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (5.6)
                 
                
                   Proof 
                   In this proof, we write  \(P_t^\varepsilon = e^{t(A_{\hat{\alpha },k}+\varepsilon ^{-1}B_k)}\) . The first claim and a convergence as in ( 5.6 ) with  \(P_t^\varepsilon f(\eta )\)  replaced by  \(P_t^\varepsilon \Pi _k f(\eta )\)  are the content of [ EK86 , §1, Theorem 7.6]. In order to obtain the desired claim (i.e., remove the projection operator  \(\Pi _k\) ), it suffices to prove that $$\begin{aligned} \lim _{\varepsilon \rightarrow 0} \max _{\eta \in \Xi _k} \Big |P_{t}^\varepsilon \Pi _k f(\eta )-P_{t}^\varepsilon f(\eta )\Big |=0\; \text {,}\;\;\quad t>0\; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ For this purpose, fix  \(f\in \mathbb {R}^{\Xi _k}\) , and observe that $$\begin{aligned} \Pi _k f(\eta )= f(\eta )\; \text {,}\;\;\quad \eta \in \Omega _k\; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ because  \((\mathcal {B}_k(t))_{t\ge 0}\)  has  \(\Omega _k\)  as absorbing states. Hence, since  \(\Pi _k\)  is a contraction, we get $$\begin{aligned} \Big |P_t^\varepsilon \Pi _k f(\eta )-P_t^\varepsilon f(\eta )\Big | \le \sum _{\xi \in \Delta _k } p_t^\varepsilon (\eta ,\xi )\Big |\Pi _k f(\xi )-f(\xi )\Big | \le \big (2\max _{\xi \in \Xi _k}\Big |f(\xi )\Big | \big )\, p_t^\varepsilon (\eta ,\Delta _k ) \; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ where  \(p_t^\varepsilon (\,\cdot \,,\,\cdot \,)\)  denotes the transition kernel of the  \(\varepsilon ^{-1}\) -sped up  \(\textrm{SIP}_k(\varepsilon \hat{\alpha })\)  at time  t . Since $$\begin{aligned} \lim _{\varepsilon \rightarrow 0} p_t^\varepsilon (\eta , \Delta _k)= 0\; \text {,}\;\;\quad \eta \in \Xi _k\; \text {,}\;\;t>0 \; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ (see Proposition  A.1  below for the full statement and proof of this fact), the proof of the proposition is concluded.  \(\square \) 
                 Fix  \(t> 0\) , and recall that  \(e^{t(A_{\hat{\alpha },k}+\varepsilon ^{-1}B_k)}\)  has all real eigenvalues, just one equal to 1, while all the others lying in the interval (0, 1). Further, Proposition  5.1  ensures that all eigenvalues (with multiplicity) of  \(e^{t(A_{\hat{\alpha },k}+\varepsilon ^{-1}B_k)}\)  converge to those of  \(e^{t{{\mathscr {G}}_{\hat{\alpha },k}}}{\Pi _k}\) . Here,  \({\mathscr {G}}_{\hat{\alpha },k}\)  acts on  \(\mathcal {R}(\Pi _k)\) , while  \(e^{t{\mathscr {G}}_{\hat{\alpha },k}}\Pi _k\)  is regarded as an operator on  \(\mathbb {R}^{\Xi _k}\) . On  \(\mathcal {R}(\Pi _k)\) , this operator coincides with  \(e^{t{\mathscr {G}}_{\hat{\alpha },k}}\) , whereas on  \(\textrm{Ker}(\Pi _k)\)  it is identically zero. In particular, the spectrum of  \(e^{t{{\mathscr {G}}_{\hat{\alpha },k}}}{\Pi _k}\)  entirely lies in [0, 1]: one eigenvalue is 1,  \(|\Omega _k|-1\)  eigenvalues lie in (0, 1), while the eigenvalue 0 has multiplicity  \(\textrm{dim}(\textrm{Ker}(\Pi _k))= |\Xi _k|-|\Omega _k|=|\Delta _k|\) . As a consequence, the second-largest eigenvalue of  \(e^{t(A_{\hat{\alpha },k}+\varepsilon ^{-1}B_k)}\)  converges to the second-largest eigenvalue of  \( e^{t {{\mathscr {G}}_{\hat{\alpha },k}}}{\Pi _k}\)  on  \(\mathcal {R}(\Pi _k)\subset \mathbb {R}^{\Xi _k}\) , which is given by  \(e^{-t w_k(\hat{\alpha })}\) , where $$\begin{aligned} w_k(\hat{\alpha }):=\ \text {the second-smallest eigenvalue of}\ {-{\mathscr {G}}_{\hat{\alpha },k}}:\mathcal {R}(\Pi _k)\rightarrow \mathcal {R}(\Pi _k) \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (5.7)
                 Thus, we proved the following claim. (Recall that  \(\textrm{gap}_k(\varepsilon \hat{\alpha })=\textrm{gap}_k(G,\varepsilon \hat{\alpha })\) .) 
                   Corollary 5.2 
                   For all integers  \(k\ge 2\) , we have $$\begin{aligned} \lim _{\varepsilon \rightarrow 0} \frac{\textrm{gap}_k( \varepsilon \hat{\alpha })}{\varepsilon }= w_k(\hat{\alpha })\; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                 In the next subsection, we analyze more in detail the limiting generator  \({\mathscr {G}}_{\hat{\alpha },k}=\Pi _k A_{\hat{\alpha },k}|_{\mathcal {R}(\Pi _k)}\)  and its block-triangular structure. 5.2  Recurrent and transient states As already proved in Proposition  5.1 ,  \({\mathscr {G}}_{\hat{\alpha },k}=\Pi _k A_{\hat{\alpha },k}|_{\mathcal {R}(\Pi _k)}: \mathcal {R}(\Pi _k)\rightarrow \mathcal {R}(\Pi _k)\)  may be interpreted as the infinitesimal generator of a Markov chain on  \(\Omega _k\) , hereafter referred to as  \((\mathcal {M}_{\hat{\alpha },k}(t))_{t\ge 0}\) . For notational simplicity, instead of analyzing directly the operator  \({\mathscr {G}}_{\hat{\alpha },k}\)  on the  \(|\Omega _k|\) -dimensional subspace  \(\mathcal {R}(\Pi _k)\)  of  \(\mathbb {R}^{\Xi _k}\) , we identify  \(\mathcal {R}(\Pi _k)\)  with  \(\mathbb {R}^{\Omega _k}\)  by restriction to  \(\Omega _k\) , and analyze the transformed rate matrix — defined as  \(M_{\hat{\alpha },k}\)  in ( 5.9 ) or ( 5.21 ) below — on  \(\mathbb {R}^{\Omega _k}\) . Let us now describe this rate matrix in detail. Recall the chains  \((\mathcal {A}_{\hat{\alpha },k}(t))_{t\ge 0}\)  and  \((\mathcal {B}_k(t))_{t\ge 0}\)  introduced at the beginning of Sect.  5.1 . Let  \(\textbf{r}^\mathcal {A}_{\hat{\alpha },k}\)  be the transition-rate function of the process  \((\mathcal {A}_{\hat{\alpha },k}(t))_{t\ge 0}\) . Moreover, let  \(\textbf{P}^{\mathcal {B}}_\zeta \)  denote the law of the process  \((\mathcal {B}_k(t))_{t\ge 0}\)  started from  \(\zeta \in \Xi _k\) , and define $$\begin{aligned} \tau _\xi :=\inf \{t\ge 0:\mathcal {B}_k(t)=\xi \} \; \text {,}\;\;\quad \tau _{\Omega _k} :=\min _{\xi \in \Omega _k}\tau _\xi \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ The effective process on  \(\Omega _k\) , denoted by  \((\mathcal {M}_{\hat{\alpha },k}(t))_{t\ge 0}\) , has non-diagonal transition rates $$\begin{aligned} \textbf{r}^\mathcal {M}_{\hat{\alpha },k}(\eta ,\xi ) :=\sum _{\zeta \in \Xi _k} \textbf{r}^\mathcal {A}_{\hat{\alpha },k}(\eta ,\zeta )\, \textbf{P}^{\mathcal {B}}_\zeta \left[ \tau _\xi =\tau _{\Omega _k} \right] \; \text {,}\;\;\quad \eta ,\xi \in \Omega _k\; \text {,}\;\;\eta \ne \xi \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (5.8)
                 The diagonal entries are chosen so that row sums vanish. We write $$\begin{aligned} M_{\hat{\alpha },k}:= \left( \textbf{r}^\mathcal {M}_{\hat{\alpha },k}(\eta ,\xi ) \right) _{\eta ,\xi \in \Omega _k}\in \mathbb {R}^{\Omega _x\times \Omega _k} \end{aligned}$$ 
                    (5.9)
                 for the corresponding square rate matrix of size  \(|\Omega _k|\) . From the form of these rates, we immediately derive a classification of recurrent and transient states for this Markov chain; this will lead to a further characterization of the spectral gap  \(w_k(\hat{\alpha })\)  of  \({\mathscr {G}}_{\hat{\alpha },k}\) . We collect this classification in Proposition  5.4  below; its direct consequence on the spectral gap is the content of the subsequent corollary. Before that, let us introduce, for every integer  \(m\ge 1\) , $$\begin{aligned} \Omega _{k,m} :=\left\{ \eta \in \Omega _k:| \{ x \in V: \eta _x > 0 \} | = m \right\} \; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (5.10)
                 that is, the set of configurations in  \(\Omega _k\)  with  m  separated stacks of particles (cf. ( 5.4 )). Clearly,  \(\Omega _{k,m}=\varnothing \)  implies  \(\Omega _{k,m+\ell }=\varnothing \)  for all  \(\ell \ge 1\) . Further, we have  \(\Omega _{k,1}\ne \varnothing \) ,  \(\Omega _{k,k+1}=\varnothing \) , and, thus,  \( \Omega _k = \sqcup _{m=1}^\infty \Omega _{k,m} = \Omega _{k,1} \sqcup \Omega _{k,2} \sqcup \cdots \sqcup \Omega _{k,k}\) . To make the limiting dynamics more transparent, we first discuss a simple example. 
                   Example 5.3 
                   (Three-site path: block structure) Let  G  be the path  \(\{1,2,3\}\) , with conductances  \(c_{12}, c_{23}>0\)  and  \(c_{13}=0\) , and consider the limiting chain on  \(\Omega _k\)  generated by  \(M_{\hat{\alpha },k}\) . This example illustrates the block structure described above. 
                   For  \(k=2\) , the absorbing set of the fast dynamics is $$\begin{aligned} \Omega _2 = \Omega _{2,1}\sqcup \Omega _{2,2}\; \text {,}\;\;\quad \Omega _{2,1}=\{2\delta _1,2\delta _2,2\delta _3\}\; \text {,}\;\;\quad \Omega _{2,2}=\{\delta _1+\delta _3\}\; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ The block  \(\Omega _{2,1}\)  is recurrent: it describes a single stack of two particles moving effectively as one random walk on  G . The block  \(\Omega _{2,2}\)  is transient: from  \(\delta _1+\delta _3\) , a slow jump of either particle to the middle site creates a configuration in  \(\Delta _2\) , after which the fast dynamics immediately thermalizes into the recurrent block  \(\Omega _{2,1}\) , never returning to  \(\Omega _{2,2}\) . 
                   For  \(k=3\) , one similarly has $$\begin{aligned} \Omega _3 = \Omega _{3,1}\sqcup \Omega _{3,2}\; \text {,}\;\;\quad \Omega _{3,1}=\{3\delta _1,3\delta _2,3\delta _3\}\; \text {,}\;\;\quad \Omega _{3,2} = \{2\delta _1+\delta _3,\ \delta _1+2\delta _3\}\; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ while  \(\Omega _{3,m}=\varnothing \)  for all  \(m\ge 3\) . Again,  \(\Omega _{3,1}\)  is recurrent, while  \(\Omega _{3,2}\)  is transient. In contrast with the case  \(k=2\) , however, a slow jump from a state in  \(\Omega _{3,2}\)  to  \(\Delta _3\)  need not be followed by a fast relaxation directly into  \(\Omega _{3,1}\) : the fast dynamics may also reach the other state of  \(\Omega _{3,2}\) . Indeed, starting, e.g., from  \(2\delta _1+\delta _3\in \Omega _{3,2}\) , a slow jump of one of the two particles sitting at site 1 to site 2 yields  \(\delta _1+\delta _2+\delta _3\in \Delta _3\) . The subsequent fast thermalization may either merge all particles into a single stack, hence reaching  \(\Omega _{3,1}\) , or merge the particle at site 2 with the one at site 3, thereby reaching  \(\delta _1+2\delta _3\in \Omega _{3,2}\) . In conclusion, for  \(k=3\) , the transient block is already a genuine two-state sub-Markov chain. 
                
                   Proposition 5.4 
                   For the chain  \((\mathcal {M}_{\hat{\alpha },k}(t))_{t\ge 0}\) , the following two claims hold true:  
                     
                       (a) 
                      
                         For every  \(m, m'\ge 1\)  with  \(\Omega _{k,m}\ne \varnothing \ne \Omega _{k,m'}\)  and  \(\eta \in \Omega _{k,m}\) , we have  $$\begin{aligned} \textbf{r}_{\hat{\alpha },k}^\mathcal {M}(\eta ,\xi ) = 0\; \text {,}\;\;\quad \text {for all}\ \xi \in \Omega _{k,m'}, \ m'>m\; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$  while, when  \(m\ge 2\) , there exist  \(\eta ^1,\ldots ,\eta ^\ell \in \Omega _{k,m}\)  satisfying  $$\begin{aligned} \textbf{r}_{\hat{\alpha },k}^\mathcal {M}(\eta ,\eta ^1)\left( \prod _{h=1}^{\ell -1} \textbf{r}_{\hat{\alpha },k}^\mathcal {M}(\eta ^h,\eta ^{h+1})\right) \textbf{r}_{\hat{\alpha },k}^\mathcal {M}(\eta ^\ell ,\xi )>0\; \text {,}\;\;\quad \text {for some}\ \xi \in \Omega _{k,m'}, \ m'<m\; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$  As a consequence,  \(\Omega _{k,m}\) ,  \(m\ge 2\) , consists of transient states only. 
                      
                     
                     
                       (b) 
                      
                         The chain  \((\mathcal {M}_{\hat{\alpha },k}(t))_{t\ge 0}\)  is irreducible on  \(\Omega _{k,1}\ne \varnothing \) , and the position of the single stack evolves as  \(\textrm{RW}(\hat{\alpha })\) . 
                      
                     
                   
                
                   Proof 
                   (a) The desired claim follows from the connectedness of the graph  G , and the observation that the rates  \(\textbf{r}_{\hat{\alpha },k}^\mathcal {M}(\,\cdot \,,\,\cdot \,)\)  in ( 5.8 ) describe the following two subsequent mechanisms: a step of the chain  \((\mathcal {A}_{\hat{\alpha },k}(t))_{t\ge 0}\)  increases the number of stacks at most by one, creating, in this case, at least two neighboring stacks; the chain  \((\mathcal {B}_k(t))_{t\ge 0}\)  forces all neighboring particles to eventually merge into isolated (i.e., non-neighboring) stacks. 
                   (b) Observe that, for all  \(x,y,z \in V\)  with  \(c_{xz}>0\) , we have $$\begin{aligned} \textbf{P}^{\mathcal {B}}_{(k-1)\delta _x+\delta _z}\,[\tau _{k\delta _y}=\tau _{\Omega _{k,1}}] = \frac{\mathbb {1}_{y=z}}{k} + \frac{(k-1) \, \mathbb {1}_{y=x}}{k}\; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ because the trajectories of  \((\mathcal {B}_k(t))_{t\ge 0}\)  started from  \((k-1)\delta _x+\delta _z\in \Delta _k\)  coincide with those of the symmetric random walk on  \(\llbracket 0,k \rrbracket \)  started from  \(k-1\) . Plugging this expression into ( 5.8 ), we obtain, for  \(x \ne y\) , $$\begin{aligned} &  \textbf{r}_{\hat{\alpha },k}^\mathcal {M}(k\delta _x,k\delta _y)\\ &  \quad = \sum _{z\in V} \textbf{r}_{\hat{\alpha },k}^\mathcal {A}(k\delta _x,(k-1)\delta _x+\delta _z)\, \textbf{P}_{(k-1)\delta _x+\delta _z}^\mathcal {B}\, [\tau _{k\delta _y}=\tau _{\Omega _{k,1}}] = k\,c_{xy}\,\hat{\alpha }_y\,\frac{1}{k} = c_{xy}\,\hat{\alpha }_y\; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ namely, the transition rates of  \(\textrm{RW}(\hat{\alpha })\) . Irreducibility is then an obvious consequence of that of  \(\textrm{RW}(\hat{\alpha })\) .  \(\square \) 
                
                   Remark 5.5 
                   The claim in Proposition  5.4 (b) is the finite-particle analogue of the metastable picture of  \(\textrm{SIP}\) , fully detailed in [ GRV13 ,  BDG17 ]. 
                
                   Remark 5.6 
                   For  \(k\ge m\ge 2\) , the dynamics on  \(\Omega _{k,m}\ne \varnothing \)  encoded by  \(M_{\hat{\alpha },k}\)  goes as follows. All stacks evolve as independent  \(\textrm{RW}(\hat{\alpha })\)  as long as their mutual distances are at least three. When two or more stacks reach mutual distance two, then there is a positive rate for them to merge. These rates — as well as the new positions of the stacks and their new compositions — depend on  G  and  \(\hat{\alpha }\) , but also non-trivially on  \(k\ge m\)  and, when  \(k>m\) , on the stacks’ composition. For more details, see Sect.  5.3.2  below. 
                 The state-classification in Proposition  5.4  may be equivalently restated as follows: after a suitable conjugation with a permutation matrix,  \(M_{\hat{\alpha },k}\)  is turned into a block lower triangular matrix with  \(M_{\hat{\alpha },k,1},\ldots , M_{\hat{\alpha },k,k}\)  as diagonal blocks, where, for all  \(m\in \llbracket 1,k \rrbracket \) , $$\begin{aligned} M_{\hat{\alpha },k,m} \in \mathbb {R}^{|\Omega _{k,m}|\times |\Omega _{k,m}|} \end{aligned}$$ 
                    (5.11)
                 is the submatrix of  \(M_{\hat{\alpha },k}\)  obtained from the restriction on configurations in  \(\Omega _{k,m}\)  (see Fig.  6 ). Since the eigenvalues of block triangular matrices are the union of those of the diagonal blocks, it suffices to focus on the spectrum of the blocks  \(M_{\hat{\alpha },k,m}\) ,  \(m\in \llbracket 1,k \rrbracket \) . As already showed in the previous section, the eigenvalues of  \(-M_{\hat{\alpha },k,m}\) ,  \(m\in \llbracket 1,k \rrbracket \) , are all real and non-negative. Remark that: 
                   
                     for  \(m=1\) , the spectrum of  \(M_{\hat{\alpha },k,m}\)  coincides with that of the generator of  \(\textrm{RW}(\hat{\alpha })\) ; 
                   
                   
                     for  \(m\ge 2\)  and  \(\Omega _{k,m}\ne \varnothing \) ,  \(M_{\hat{\alpha },k,m}\)  is the rate matrix of a sub-Markovian chain killed upon exiting  \(\Omega _{k,m}\) ; thus, all its eigenvalues are strictly negative. 
                   
                 In view of these simple observations, we register the following useful characterization of the spectral gap  \(w_k(\hat{\alpha })\)  defined in ( 5.7 ). 
                   Corollary 5.7 
                   For all  \(m\ge 2\)  such that  \(\Omega _{k,m}\ne \varnothing \) , define $$\begin{aligned} \lambda _{k,m}(\hat{\alpha }):=\ \text {smallest eigenvalue of the matrix}\ -M_{\hat{\alpha },k,m}\in \mathbb {R}^{|\Omega _{k,m}|\times |\Omega _{k,m}|} \; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (5.12)
                 whereas  \(\lambda _{k,m}(\hat{\alpha }):=+\infty \)  if  \(\Omega _{k,m}=\varnothing \) . Then, we have $$\begin{aligned} w_k(\hat{\alpha }) = \textrm{gap}_\textrm{RW}(\hat{\alpha }) \wedge \min _{m \in \llbracket 2,k \rrbracket } \lambda _{k,m}(\hat{\alpha }) \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (5.13)
                 
                
                   Example 5.8 
                   (Three-site path: eigenvalues) We keep the notation of Example  5.3 . 
                   For  \(k=2\) , the only transient state is  \(\delta _1+\delta _3\) . It exits the transient block as soon as one of the two particles jumps to site 2, which happens at rate $$\begin{aligned} p:=\left( c_{12}+c_{23}\right) \hat{\alpha }_2\; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ Therefore,  \( \lambda _{2,2}(\hat{\alpha })=p\) . 
                   For  \(k=3\) , the transient block is  \( \Omega _{3,2} = \{2\delta _1+\delta _3,\delta _1+2\delta _3\}\) . Starting from  \(2\delta _1+\delta _3\) , a slow jump of one of the two particles at site 1 to site 2 occurs at rate  \(2c_{12}\hat{\alpha }_2\)  and produces  \(\delta _1+\delta _2+\delta _3\) . From this configuration, the fast dynamics reaches  \(\delta _1+2\delta _3\)  with probability  \(c_{23}/(2(c_{12}+c_{23}))\) . Hence, the effective jump rate from  \(2\delta _1+\delta _3\)  to  \(\delta _1+2\delta _3\)  is $$\begin{aligned} q:=2c_{12}\hat{\alpha }_2\frac{c_{23}}{2(c_{12}+c_{23})} = \frac{c_{12}c_{23}}{c_{12}+c_{23}}\hat{\alpha }_2\; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ By symmetry, the effective jump rate in the opposite direction is also  q . The effective killing rate from either transient state to the recurrent block is  p . Indeed, starting again from  \(2\delta _1+\delta _3\) , the slow jump of the particle at site 3 to site 2 contributes  \(c_{23}\hat{\alpha }_2\)  to this killing rate. The slow jump of one of the two particles at site 1 to site 2 contributes instead  \(2c_{12}\hat{\alpha }_2\cdot \frac{1}{2}=c_{12}\hat{\alpha }_2\) , since from  \(\delta _1+\delta _2+\delta _3\)  the fast dynamics reaches  \(\Omega _{3,1}\)  with probability 1/2. Thus, the total killing rate is  \( c_{23}\hat{\alpha }_2+c_{12}\hat{\alpha }_2=p\) . Therefore, in the ordered basis  \(\left( 2\delta _1+\delta _3,\delta _1+2\delta _3\right) \) , $$\begin{aligned} -M_{\hat{\alpha },3,2} = \begin{pmatrix} p+q & -q \\ -q & p+q \end{pmatrix}\; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ Its smallest eigenvalue is  \(\lambda _{3,2}(\hat{\alpha })=p\) , the other eigenvalue being  \(p+2q>p\) . Thus, in this example, the same quantity  p  gives the transient contribution to the limiting gap for both  \(k=2\)  and  \(k=3\) . 
                 The natural next step is to estimate the eigenvalue  \(\lambda _{k,m} (\hat{\alpha })\)  for each  \(k\ge 2\)  and  \(m \in \llbracket 2,k \rrbracket \) , by bounding it from below by  \(\lambda _{2,2}(\hat{\alpha })\) . This task is carried out in the following two subsections. In the first one, we prove  \(\lambda _{k,m}(\hat{\alpha })= \lambda _{m,m}(\hat{\alpha })\) ; in the second one, we show  \(\lambda _{m,m}(\hat{\alpha })\ge \lambda _{2,2}(\hat{\alpha })\) . We conclude this subsection by observing that each submatrix  \(M_{\hat{\alpha },k,m}\)  in  \(\Omega _{k,m}\)  is self-adjoint with respect to a positive measure  \(\varsigma _{\hat{\alpha },k,m}\)  given in ( 5.14 ) below. On the one hand, this property is interesting and may become handy in future use, e.g., for building an  \(L^2\) -theory for the corresponding continuum model. On the other hand, in our storyline, neither this property nor the precise form of the symmetrizing measure are of primary importance. Thus, we only state the full statement here, and postpone its proof to Appendix B. 
                   Lemma 5.9 
                   For all  \(m\le k\) ,  \(M_{\hat{\alpha },k,m}\)  is self-adjoint as an operator on  \(L^2(\varsigma _{\hat{\alpha },k,m})\) , where $$\begin{aligned} \varsigma _{\hat{\alpha },k,m}(\eta ):=\prod _{\begin{array}{c} x\in V\\ \eta _x>0 \end{array}}\frac{\hat{\alpha }_x}{\eta _x}\; \text {,}\;\;\quad \eta \in \Omega _{k,m}\; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (5.14)
                 
                 5.3  Comparing  \(\lambda _{k,m}(\hat{\alpha })\)  with  \(\lambda _{m,m}(\hat{\alpha })\) 
                         The main goal of this subsection is to prove the following result. 
                   Proposition 5.10 
                   For all integers  \(m\ge 2\)  with  \(\Omega _{m,m}\ne \varnothing \) , we have $$\begin{aligned} \lambda _{k,m}(\hat{\alpha })=\lambda _{m,m}(\hat{\alpha })\; \text {,}\;\;\quad k\ge m\; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (5.15)
                 
                 We divide the proof of this identity into a few steps, and fix  \(m\ge 2\)  so as to satisfy  \(\Omega _{m,m}\ne \varnothing \)  all throughout. 5.3.1  Consistency Recall the consistency property ( 3.1 ) satisfied by  \(\textrm{SIP}\) , as well as the corresponding annihilation operator  \(\mathfrak {a}_k: \mathbb {R}^{\Xi _{k-1}} \rightarrow \mathbb {R}^{\Xi _k}\)  in ( 3.2 ). Since  \(M_{\hat{\alpha },k}\)  arises as a small-diffusivity limit of  \(\textrm{SIP}\) , it is natural to expect that an analogous property should hold also for  \(M_{\hat{\alpha },k}\) . This is the content of the following proposition. 
                     Proposition 5.11 
                     Define, for all integers  \(k\ge 2\) , the following “restricted” annihilation operator  \(\hat{\mathfrak a}_k: \mathbb {R}^{\Omega _{k-1}}\rightarrow \mathbb {R}^{\Omega _k}\)  as $$\begin{aligned} \hat{\mathfrak a}_k g(\eta ):=\sum _{x\in V} \eta _x\,g(\eta -\delta _x)\; \text {,}\;\;\quad g\in \mathbb {R}^{\Omega _{k-1}}\; \text {,}\;\;\eta \in \Omega _k\; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (5.16)
                 Then, we have $$\begin{aligned} M_{\hat{\alpha },k}\,\hat{\mathfrak a}_k = \hat{\mathfrak a}_k\, M_{\hat{\alpha },k-1}\; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (5.17)
                 
                  
                     Proof 
                     It is well known that a system of  k  independent random walks is consistent, i.e.,  \(A_{\hat{\alpha },k} \, \mathfrak {a}_k = \mathfrak {a}_k \, A_{\hat{\alpha },k-1}\) . Moreover, by rewriting the consistency property of  \(\textrm{SIP}(\varepsilon \hat{\alpha })\)  in ( 3.1 ) with the notation in ( 5.1 ), we have  \((A_{\hat{\alpha },k} + \varepsilon ^{-1} B_k) \, \mathfrak {a}_k = \mathfrak {a}_k \, (A_{\hat{\alpha },k-1} + \varepsilon ^{-1} B_{k-1})\) . Thus, by linearity, we must also have  \(B_k\,\mathfrak {a}_k=\mathfrak a_k\,B_{k-1}\) , and a similar relation for the harmonic projection  \(\Pi _k\)  of  \(B_k\) . By combining these observations, we obtain $$\begin{aligned} \Pi _k\,\mathfrak a_k = \mathfrak a_k\, \Pi _{k-1}\quad \text {as maps from}\ \mathbb {R}^{\Xi _{k-1}}\ \text {to}\ \mathbb {R}^{\Xi _k}\; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (5.18)
                 and $$\begin{aligned} \Pi _k A_{\hat{\alpha },k}\,\mathfrak a_k = \mathfrak a_k\,\Pi _{k-1}A_{\hat{\alpha },k-1}\quad \text {as maps from}\ \mathbb {R}^{\Xi _{k-1}}\ \text {to}\ \mathbb {R}^{\Xi _k}\; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (5.19)
                 In particular, ( 5.18 ) ensures that  \(\mathfrak a_k\)  maps  \(\mathcal {R}(\Pi _{k-1})\)  into  \(\mathcal {R}(\Pi _k)\) . 
                     For all  \(j\ge 1\) , let  \( \rho _j:\mathcal {R}(\Pi _j)\rightarrow \mathbb {R}^{\Omega _j} \)  be the restriction map to  \(\Omega _j\) , and let  \( \iota _j:\mathbb {R}^{\Omega _j}\rightarrow \mathcal {R}(\Pi _j) \)  be its inverse given by  \(B_j\) -harmonic extension. By definition of the rate matrix in ( 5.9 ), $$\begin{aligned} M_{\hat{\alpha },j} = \rho _j\Pi _j A_{\hat{\alpha },j}\iota _j \quad \text {on }\mathbb {R}^{\Omega _j} \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (5.20)
                 Moreover, since  \(\mathfrak a_k\)  maps  \(\mathcal {R}(\Pi _{k-1})\)  into  \(\mathcal {R}(\Pi _k)\) , we have $$\begin{aligned} \iota _k\hat{\mathfrak a}_k = \mathfrak a_k\iota _{k-1}\quad \text {as maps from}\ \mathbb {R}^{\Omega _{k-1}}\ \text {to}\ \mathcal {R}(\Pi _k) \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (5.21)
                 Indeed, the right-hand side belongs to  \(\mathcal {R}(\Pi _k)\) . Moreover, for all  \(g\in \mathbb {R}^{\Omega _{k-1}}\)  and  \(\eta \in \Omega _k\) , $$\begin{aligned} \mathfrak a_k\iota _{k-1}g(\eta )&= \sum _{x\in V}\eta _x\,\iota _{k-1}g(\eta -\delta _x) = \sum _{x\in V}\eta _x\,g(\eta -\delta _x) = \hat{\mathfrak a}_k g(\eta ) \; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ where we used that  \(\eta -\delta _x\in \Omega _{k-1}\)  whenever  \(\eta \in \Omega _k\)  and  \(\eta _x\ge 1\) . Hence the two sides of ( 5.21 ) are the same  \(B_k\) -harmonic extension. 
                     Finally, by ( 5.20 ), ( 5.21 ), and ( 5.19 ), we get $$\begin{aligned} M_{\hat{\alpha },k}\hat{\mathfrak a}_k&= \rho _k\Pi _k A_{\hat{\alpha },k}\iota _k \hat{\mathfrak a}_k \nonumber \\&= \rho _k\Pi _k A_{\hat{\alpha },k}\mathfrak a_k \iota _{k-1} \nonumber \\&= \rho _k\mathfrak a_k\Pi _{k-1}A_{\hat{\alpha },k-1} \iota _{k-1} \nonumber \\&= \rho _k\mathfrak a_k\iota _{k-1} \rho _{k-1}\Pi _{k-1}A_{\hat{\alpha },k-1} \iota _{k-1} \nonumber \\&= \hat{\mathfrak a}_k M_{\hat{\alpha },k-1} \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ In the fourth line, we used that  \(\Pi _{k-1}A_{\hat{\alpha },k-1}\iota _{k-1}\)  has  \(\mathcal {R}(\Pi _{k-1})\)  as an image, so that  \(\iota _{k-1}\rho _{k-1}\)  acts as the identity on it. This concludes the proof of the proposition  \(\square \) 
                   5.3.2  Irreducible decompositions By an  irreducible component of   \((\mathcal {M}_{\hat{\alpha },k}(t))_{t \ge 0}\)   on   \(\Omega _{k,m}\)  we mean a communicating class for the restriction of the transition graph to  \(\Omega _{k,m}\) : two configurations  \(\eta ,\xi \in \Omega _{k,m}\)  belong to the same component if there exist paths  \(\eta =\eta _0,\eta _1,\ldots ,\eta _\ell =\xi \)  and  \(\xi =\xi _0,\xi _1,\ldots ,\xi _r=\eta \) , all contained in  \(\Omega _{k,m}\) , such that $$\begin{aligned} \textbf{r}^\mathcal {M}_{\hat{\alpha },k}(\eta _{h-1},\eta _h)>0\; \text {,}\;\;\quad \textbf{r}^\mathcal {M}_{\hat{\alpha },k}(\xi _{h-1},\xi _h)>0\; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ for all admissible indices. An irreducible decomposition is the partition of  \(\Omega _{k,m}\)  into such irreducible components. The chain  \((\mathcal {M}_{\hat{\alpha },k}(t))_{t\ge 0}\)  is not necessarily irreducible on  \(\Omega _{k,m}\) . For instance, when  \(G=\mathbb {T}_{2m}\)  is the one-dimensional discrete torus of size 2 m , $$\begin{aligned} {\Omega _{m,m}=\{\delta _1+\delta _3+\ldots +\delta _{2m-1},\delta _0+\delta _2+\ldots +\delta _{2m-2}\}=:\{\eta _{m,\mathrm odd},\eta _{m,\mathrm even}\}}\end{aligned}$$ consists of exactly two configurations (one with particles/stacks on the odd numbers, the other one on the even numbers), and  \(\textbf{r}_{\hat{\alpha },k}^\mathcal {M}(\eta _{m,\mathrm odd},\eta _{m,\mathrm even})=\textbf{r}_{\hat{\alpha },k}^\mathcal {M}(\eta _{m,\mathrm even},\eta _{m,\mathrm odd})=0\) . Moreover, the structure of the irreducible components of the chain  \((\mathcal {M}_{\hat{\alpha },k}(t))_{t\ge 0}\)  on  \(\Omega _{k,m}\)  is always  finer  than the corresponding structure on  \(\Omega _{m,m}\) , in the sense of the following proposition. 
                     Proposition 5.12 
                     Decompose  \(\Omega _{m,m}\)  into irreducible components for the chain  \((\mathcal {M}_{\hat{\alpha },m}(t))_{t\ge 0}\) : for some integer  \(\mathfrak n_m\ge 1\)  (depending only on  \(m\ge 2\)  and  G ), $$\begin{aligned} \Omega _{m,m}=\Omega _{m,m}^1\sqcup \cdots \sqcup \Omega _{m,m}^{\mathfrak n_m}\; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (5.22)
                 For all  \(k> m\)  and  \(i\in \llbracket 1,\mathfrak n_m \rrbracket \) , define, iteratively, $$\begin{aligned} \Omega _{k,m}^i = \{ \eta + \delta _x \in \Omega _{k,m}: \eta \in \Omega _{k-1,m}^i, \ x \in V \ \text {such that} \ \eta _x \ge 1 \} \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (5.23)
                 Then, there exist partitions  \(\Omega _{k,m}^i=\Omega _{k,m}^{i,1} \sqcup \cdots \sqcup \Omega _{k,m}^{i,\mathfrak {N}^k_i}\)  for all  \(i \in \llbracket 1,\mathfrak n_m \rrbracket \)  such that $$\begin{aligned} \Omega _{k,m}= \bigsqcup _{i=1}^{\mathfrak n_m} \bigsqcup _{j=1}^{\mathfrak N_i^k} \Omega _{k,m}^{i,j} \end{aligned}$$ 
                    (5.24)
                 is an  irreducible  decomposition of  \(\Omega _{k,m}\)  for the chain  \((\mathcal {M}_{\hat{\alpha },k}(t))_{t\ge 0}\) . 
                  
                     Proof 
                     First, the fact that  \(\Omega _{k,m}\) , for each  \(k \ge m \ge 2\) , can be decomposed into irreducible components follows at once from Lemma  5.9 . Thus, to prove the validity of Proposition  5.12 , it suffices to check the following two properties:  
                       
                         (a) 
                        
                           \(\Omega _{k,m} = \Omega _{k,m}^1 \sqcup \cdots \sqcup \Omega _{k,m}^{\mathfrak n_m}\) ; 
                        
                       
                       
                         (b) 
                        
                           if  \(i \ne i'\) ,  \(\eta \in \Omega _{k,m}^i\) , and  \(\xi \in \Omega _{k,m}^{i'}\) , then  \(\eta \)  and  \(\eta '\)  belong to different irreducible components of  \(\Omega _{k,m}\)  for  \((\mathcal {M}_{\hat{\alpha },k}(t))_{t\ge 0}\) . 
                        
                       
                     To verify item (a), take  \(\eta \in \Omega _{k,m}\)  and define a new configuration  \(\eta ^*\in \Omega _{m,m}\)  as $$\begin{aligned} \eta ^* :=\sum _{\begin{array}{c} x \in V \\ \eta _x \ge 1 \end{array}} \delta _x \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (5.25)
                 Since  \(\eta \)  has  m  separated stacks of particles,  \(\eta ^*\)  belongs to  \(\Omega _{m,m}\) . Then, according to ( 5.22 ),  \(\eta ^* \in \Omega _{m,m}^i\)  for some  i , thus  \(\eta \in \Omega _{k,m}^i\)  by ( 5.23 ). This proves that  \(\Omega _{k,m}=\Omega _{k,m}^1 \sqcup \cdots \sqcup \Omega _{k,m}^{\mathfrak n_m}\) . 
                     We move on to item (b). It suffices to prove that, for any  \(\eta ,\xi \in \Omega _{k,m}\)  with  \(\textbf{r}_{\hat{\alpha },k}^\mathcal {M}(\eta ,\xi )>0\) , the corresponding configurations  \(\eta ^*,\xi ^* \in \Omega _{m,m}\)  (cf. ( 5.25 )) satisfy either  \(\eta ^* = \xi ^*\)  or  \(\textbf{r}_{\hat{\alpha },m}^\mathcal {M}(\eta ^*,\xi ^*)>0\) . 
                     In view of ( 5.8 ), there exists  \(\zeta \in \Xi _k\)  such that  \(\textbf{r}_{\hat{\alpha },k}^\mathcal {A}( \eta , \zeta ) > 0\)  and  \(\textbf{P}_\zeta ^\mathcal {B}\, [\tau _\xi = \tau _{\Omega _k}] > 0\) . In turn, there exist  \(x,y \in V\)  with  \(c_{xy}>0\)  such that  \(\zeta = \eta - \delta _x + \delta _y\) . First, suppose that  \(\zeta \in \Omega _k\) , i.e.,  \(\zeta = \xi \) .
