Then, it is straightforward that  \(\zeta ^*=\xi ^* \in \Omega _m\)  and  \(\textbf{r}_{\hat{\alpha },m}^\mathcal {A}(\eta ^*,\zeta ^*)>0\) , thus we have  \(\textbf{r}_{\hat{\alpha },m}^\mathcal {M}(\eta ^*,\xi ^*)>0\)  by ( 5.8 ). On the contrary, suppose that  \(\zeta \in \Delta _k\) . This means that  y  has at least one neighboring site (possibly  x ) on which  \(\zeta \)  has a particle. We divide into several cases.  1. : 
                           If  \(\zeta _x \ge 1\)  and  \(\zeta _z = 0\)  for all  \(z \ne x\)  with  \(c_{yz}>0\) , then the dynamics  \((\mathcal {B}_k(t))_{t\ge 0}\)  can only move particles between sites  x  and  y . Thus, by  \(\textbf{P}_\zeta ^\mathcal {B}\, [\tau _\xi = \tau _{\Omega _k} ] >0\)  it necessarily holds that  \(\xi = \eta - \eta _x \delta _x + \eta _x \delta _y \in \Omega _k\) . This implies that  \(\xi ^* = \eta ^* - \delta _x + \delta _y \in \Omega _m\) , thus  \(\textbf{r}_{\hat{\alpha },m}^\mathcal {M}(\eta ^*,\xi ^*) > 0\) . 
                         2. : 
                           Suppose that  \(\zeta _x \ge 1\)  and there exists  \(z \ne x\)  with  \(c_{yz}>0\)  such that  \(\zeta _z \ge 1\) . Then, the dynamics  \((\mathcal {B}_k(t))_{t\ge 0}\)  gets absorbed exactly when  y  becomes empty. For  \(\xi \)  to have the same number of stacks,  m , as  \(\eta \) , it follows that  \(\xi ^*=\eta ^*\) . 
                         3. : 
                           Suppose that  \(\zeta _x = 0\) . Then, since the absorption of  \((\mathcal {B}_k(t))_{t\ge 0}\)  triggers at least one additional loss of a stack of particles, the absorbed configuration  \(\xi \)  has strictly less number of stacks than  \(\eta \) , which contradicts the assumption that  \(\eta ,\xi \in \Omega _{k,m}\) . 
                         
                     The above three cases conclude the proof of Proposition  5.12 .  \(\square \) 
                  
                     Remark 5.13 
                     The irreducible structure in ( 5.24 ) may indeed get  strictly  finer than the one in ( 5.22 ). For example, consider the following  H-shape  graph  G  with  \(V=\llbracket 1,6 \rrbracket \) ,  \(c_{12}=c_{23}=c_{25}=c_{45}=c_{56}=1\) , and  \(c_{xy}=0\)  otherwise (see Fig.  5 ). Then,  \(\Omega _{4,4}\)  is a singleton set with  \(\Omega _{4,4}^1 = \{ \delta _1 + \delta _3 + \delta _4 + \delta _6 \}\) , whereas  \(\Omega _{5,4}\)  has two irreducible components  \(\Omega _{5,4}^{1,1}\)  and  \(\Omega _{5,4}^{1,2}\) , given as $$\begin{aligned} \Omega _{5,4}^{1,1}&= \{ 2\delta _1 + \delta _3 + \delta _4 + \delta _6 , \delta _1 + 2\delta _3 + \delta _4 + \delta _6 \} \\ \Omega _{5,4}^{1,2}&= \{ \delta _1 + \delta _3 + 2\delta _4 + \delta _6 , \delta _1 + \delta _3 + \delta _4 + 2\delta _6 \} \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                   Fig. 5 Full size image The H-shape graph  G  and the corresponding absorbing spaces  \(\Omega _{4,4}=\Omega _{4,4}^1\)  and  \(\Omega _{5,4}=\Omega _{5,4}^{1,1} \sqcup \Omega _{5,4}^{1,2}\)  explained in Remark  5.13 5.3.3  Conclusion of the proof of Proposition  5.10 
                            We are now ready to prove ( 5.15 ). 
                     Proof of Proposition 5.10 
                     Recall the definition of the matrix  \(M_{\hat{\alpha },k}\)  and its submatrices  \(M_{\hat{\alpha },k,m}\)  from ( 5.9 ) and ( 5.11 ), respectively. 
                     First, we deal with the case  \(k=m\) . We split  \(M_{\hat{\alpha },m,m}\)  into  \(\mathfrak n_m\)  diagonal blocks $$\begin{aligned}M_{\hat{\alpha },m,m}^1,\ldots ,M_{\hat{\alpha },m,m}^{\mathfrak n_m}\; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ each corresponding to an irreducible sub-Markovian dynamics. Adopting an analogous notation, we further write the eigenvalue  \(\lambda _{m,m}(\hat{\alpha })\)  in ( 5.12 ) as $$\begin{aligned} \lambda _{m,m}(\hat{\alpha }) = \lambda _{m,m}^1(\hat{\alpha }) \wedge \cdots \wedge \lambda _{m,m}^{\mathfrak n_m}(\hat{\alpha }) \; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ where  \(\lambda _{m,m}^i(\hat{\alpha })\)  stands for the smallest eigenvalue of  \(-M_{\hat{\alpha },m,m}^i\) . By the Perron-Frobenius theorem, in view of sub-Markovianity, this eigenvalue is strictly positive. Furthermore, because of irreducibility, this is the only eigenvalue admitting a non-negative (actually, strictly positive) eigenfunction, which we refer to as $$\begin{aligned} \psi _{\hat{\alpha },m,m}^i \in \mathbb {R}^{\Omega _{m,m}^i} \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ Let, for all  \(i\in \llbracket 1,\mathfrak n_m \rrbracket \) , $$\begin{aligned} \Psi _{\hat{\alpha },m,m}^i (\eta ) :={\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} \psi _{\hat{\alpha },m,m}^i (\eta ) & \text {if}\quad \eta \in \Omega _{m,m}^i \\ 0 & \text {if}\quad \eta \in \Omega _m \setminus \Omega _{m,m}^i \; \text {,}\;\;\end{array}\right. } \end{aligned}$$ 
                    (5.26)
                 be the extension of the eigenfunction  \(\psi _{\hat{\alpha },m,m}^i\in \mathbb {R}^{\Omega _{m,m}^i}\)  to  \(\mathbb {R}^{\Omega _m}\) . In particular,  \(\Psi _{\hat{\alpha },m,m}^i\)  is nonnegative on  \(\Omega _m\)  and strictly positive on  \(\Omega _{m,m}^i\) . Then, due to the block lower triangular structure of  \(M_{\hat{\alpha },m}\) , we have $$\begin{aligned} M_{\hat{\alpha },m}\Psi _{\hat{\alpha },m,m}^i = -\lambda _{m,m}^i(\hat{\alpha })\, \Psi _{\hat{\alpha },m,m}^i\; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (5.27)
                 Since  \( \mathbb {R}^{\Omega _m}\ni \Psi _{\hat{\alpha },m,m}^i\ne 0\) , this shows that a straightforward extension of  \(\psi _{\hat{\alpha },m,m}^i\)  produces an eigenfunction for  \(M_{\hat{\alpha },m}\) . 
                     This canonical extension works well only for the case  \(k=m\) , while it does not for  \(k>m\)  in view of the block lower triangular structure of  \(M_{\hat{\alpha },k}\) . 
                     For  \(k>m\) , we follow a different route. In view of Proposition  5.12 , as done above for  \(k=m\) , we split  \(M_{\hat{\alpha },k,m}\)  into  \(\mathfrak N^k_1 + \cdots + \mathfrak N^k_{\mathfrak n_m}\)  diagonal blocks, say,  \(M_{\hat{\alpha },k,m}^{i,j}\)  for  \(i \in \llbracket 1,\mathfrak n_m \rrbracket \)  and  \(j \in \llbracket 1,\mathfrak N_i^k \rrbracket \) , each corresponding to the irreducible sub-Markovian dynamics on  \(\Omega _{k,m}^{i,j}\) . We refer to Fig.  6  for the overall structure of the matrix  \(M_{\hat{\alpha },k}\) . Then, we write the eigenvalue  \(\lambda _{k,m}(\hat{\alpha })\)  in ( 5.12 ) as $$\begin{aligned} \lambda _{k,m}(\hat{\alpha }) = \min _{i \in \llbracket 1,\mathfrak n_m \rrbracket } \min _{j \in \llbracket 1,\mathfrak N_i^k \rrbracket } \lambda _{k,m}^{i,j}(\hat{\alpha }) \; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (5.28)
                 where  \(\lambda _{k,m}^{i,j}(\hat{\alpha })\)  is the smallest eigenvalue of  \(-M_{\hat{\alpha },k,m}^{i,j}\) , which admits a strictly positive eigenfunction  \(\psi _{\hat{\alpha },k,m}^{i,j}\in \mathbb {R}^{\Omega _{k,m}^{i,j}}\) , again by the Perron-Frobenius theorem. 
                     Now, instead of the canonical extension as in ( 5.26 ), we define  \(\Psi _{\hat{\alpha },k,m}^i \in \mathbb {R}^{\Omega _k}\)  as the “lifting” of  \(\Psi _{\hat{\alpha },m,m}^i\)  through the annihilation operators in ( 5.16 ): $$\begin{aligned} \Psi _{\hat{\alpha },k,m}^i :=( \hat{\mathfrak {a}}_k \circ \cdots \circ \hat{\mathfrak {a}}_{m+1} ) \, \Psi _{\hat{\alpha },m,m}^i \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (5.29)
                 In view of the definition of the annihilation operators in ( 5.16 ) and  \(\Psi _{\hat{\alpha },m,m}^i\ge 0\)  (see ( 5.26 )), one proves, by induction on  \(k>m\) , $$\begin{aligned} \Psi _{\hat{\alpha },k,m}^i(\eta +\xi )\ge \Psi _{\hat{\alpha },m,m}^i(\eta )\; \text {,}\;\;\quad \eta \in \Omega _m\; \text {,}\;\;\eta +\xi \in \Omega _k\; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (5.30)
                 Further,  \(\Psi _{\hat{\alpha },m,m}^i>0\)  on  \(\Omega _{m,m}^i\)  implies $$\begin{aligned} \Psi _{\hat{\alpha },k,m}^i\ne 0\; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (5.31)
                  By the intertwining relation ( 5.17 ) and the eigenvalue Eq. ( 5.27 ), we obtain $$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} M_{\hat{\alpha },k} \Psi _{\hat{\alpha },k,m}^i&=M_{\hat{\alpha },k} \, \hat{\mathfrak {a}}_k \cdots \hat{\mathfrak {a}}_{m+1} \, \Psi _{\hat{\alpha },m,m}^i\\&= \hat{\mathfrak {a}}_k \cdots \hat{\mathfrak {a}}_{m+1} \, M_{\hat{\alpha },m} \, \Psi _{\hat{\alpha },m,m}^i = -\lambda _{m,m}^i(\hat{\alpha }) \, \Psi _{\hat{\alpha },k,m}^i \; \text {.}\end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$ 
                    (5.32)
                 The above identity and ( 5.31 ) ensure that  \(\Psi _{\hat{\alpha },k,m}^i\in \mathbb {R}^{\Omega _k}\)  is an eigenfunction for  \(-M_{\hat{\alpha },k}\)  associated to the eigenvalue  \(\lambda _{m,m}^i(\hat{\alpha })\) . Moreover, after recalling ( 5.29 ) and ( 5.16 ), we have $$\begin{aligned} \Psi _{\hat{\alpha },k,m}^i(\eta ) = \sum _{\xi \in \Omega _m:\, \xi \le \eta } b_{k,m}(\eta ,\xi )\,\Psi _{\hat{\alpha },m,m}^i(\xi ) =0\; \text {,}\;\;\quad \eta \in \Omega _{k,m'}^i,\ m'<m\; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ where the coefficients  \(b_{k,m}(\eta ,\xi )\)  are nonnegative and supported on $$\begin{aligned}\Omega _m(\eta ):=\{\xi \in \Omega _m: \xi _x\le \eta _x\ \text {for all}\ x\in V\}\; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ and the last identity used ( 5.26 ),  \(m'<m\) , and that $$\begin{aligned}{ \eta \in \Omega _{k,m'}\ \text {and}\ \xi \in \Omega _m(\eta ) \Longrightarrow \xi \in \Omega _{m,m''}\ \text {for some}\ m''\le m'\; \text {.}} \end{aligned}$$ Because of the block lower triangular structure of  \(M_{\hat{\alpha },k}\)  and the irreducible decomposition ( 5.24 ), this ensures that the restriction $$\begin{aligned} \Psi _{\hat{\alpha },k,m}^i\big |_{\Omega _{k,m}^{i,j}}\in \mathbb {R}^{\Omega _{k,m}^{i,j}} \end{aligned}$$ of  \(\Psi _{\hat{\alpha },k,m}^i\in \mathbb {R}^{\Omega _{k}}\)  to  \(\mathbb {R}^{\Omega _{k,m}^{i,j}}\)  is an eigenfunction for  \(-M_{\hat{\alpha },k,m}^{i,j}\)  with corresponding eigenvalue  \(\lambda _{m,m}^i(\hat{\alpha })\) . Finally, by the aforementioned positivity of  \(\psi _{\hat{\alpha },m,m}^i\in \mathbb {R}^{\Omega _{m,m}^i}\) , we have $$\begin{aligned} \Psi _{\hat{\alpha },k,m}^i (\eta ) > 0\; \text {,}\;\;\quad \text {for all} \ \eta \in \Omega _{k,m}^i \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ Indeed, for  \(\eta \in \Omega _{k,m}^i\) , the configuration  \(\eta ^*:=\sum _{x:\eta _x\ge 1}\delta _x\)  belongs to  \(\Omega _{m,m}^i\) , and ( 5.30 ) applies with  \(\xi =\eta -\eta ^*\)  (see Fig.  6  for a visual proof). Since  \(M_{\hat{\alpha },k,m}^{i,j}\)  admits  \(\psi _{\hat{\alpha },k,m}^{i,j}\in \mathbb {R}^{\Omega _{k,m}^i}\)  as the only positive eigenfunction (up to constant multiples), by the Perron-Frobenius theorem, we have $$\begin{aligned} \Psi _{\hat{\alpha },k,m}^i\big |_{\Omega _{k,m}^{i,j}} = c \, \psi _{\hat{\alpha },k,m}^{i,j} \quad \text {for some } c>0 \; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ and, thus,  \(\lambda _{m,m}^i(\hat{\alpha }) = \lambda _{k,m}^{i,j}(\hat{\alpha })\) . By ( 5.28 ), the desired claim in ( 5.15 ) follows.  \(\square \) 
                   Fig. 6 Full size image In this figure, we depict the eigenvalue equation in ( 5.32 ) (for simplicity, we dropped  \(\hat{\alpha }\)  from the notation). On the left-hand side, we show the  \(|\Omega _k| \times |\Omega _k|\)  matrix  \(M_{\hat{\alpha },k}\)  in its block lower triangular form. Stars ( \(\star \) ) indicate some possibly non-zero entries; triangles ( \(\blacktriangle \) , resp.  \(\blacktriangledown \) ) strictly positive (resp. negative) ones. The diagonal blocks in bold are the  \(|\Omega _{k,m}| \times |\Omega _{k,m}|\) -matrices  \(M_{\hat{\alpha },k,m}\) ,  \(m \in \llbracket 1,k \rrbracket \) , defined in ( 5.11 ). Furthermore, each  \(M_{\hat{\alpha },k,m}\) ,  \(m\ge 2\) , is further decomposed into block diagonal matrices  \(M_{\hat{\alpha },k,m}^{i,j}\) ,  \(i \in \llbracket 1,\mathfrak {n}_m \rrbracket \) ,  \(j \in \llbracket 1,\mathfrak N_i^k \rrbracket \) . Focusing on the red region  \(\Omega _{k,m}^{i,j}\) , we readily verify that  \(\Psi _{\hat{\alpha },k,m}^i \big |_{\Omega _{k,m}^{i,j}}\)  is a strictly positive eigenfunction of  \(M_{\hat{\alpha },k,m}^{i,j}\)  associated to  \(-\lambda _{m,m}^i(\hat{\alpha })\) 5.4  
                            \(\lambda _{k,k}(\hat{\alpha })\)  and  k  coalescing particles This subsection is devoted to the proof of a monotonicity in  k  of  \(\lambda _{k,k}(\hat{\alpha })\) , defined in ( 5.12 ) when  \(\Omega _{k,k}\ne \varnothing \) , and set equal to  \(+\infty \)  when  \(\Omega _{k,k}=\varnothing \)  (see Corollary  5.7 ). 
                   Proposition 5.14 
                   For all integers  \(k\ge 3\) , we have $$\begin{aligned} \lambda _{k,k}(\hat{\alpha }) \ge \lambda _{k-1,k-1}(\hat{\alpha })\; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (5.33)
                 Consequently, $$\begin{aligned} \lambda _{k,k}(\hat{\alpha }) \ge \lambda _{2,2}(\hat{\alpha })\; \text {,}\;\;\quad k \ge 2 \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (5.34)
                 
                
                   Proof 
                   Fix  \(k\ge 3\) . As  \(\Omega _{k-1,k-1}=\varnothing \)  implies  \(\Omega _{k,k}=\varnothing \) , we may assume that  \(\Omega _{k,k}\ne \varnothing \) . 
                   On  \(\Omega _{k,k}\) , the dynamics described by  \(M_{\hat{\alpha },k,k}\)  coincides with  k  independent copies of  \(\textrm{RW}(\hat{\alpha })=\textrm{RW}(G,\hat{\alpha })\)  killed upon exiting  \(\Omega _{k,k}\) . For  \(\xi \in \Omega _{k,k}\) , write  \(\mathsf P_\xi \)  for the law of this process started from  \(\xi \) , and define  \(\tau _k\ge 0\)  as its killing time. Since the killed chain is finite-state, $$\begin{aligned} \lambda _{k,k}(\hat{\alpha }) = -\lim _{t\rightarrow \infty } \frac{1}{t} \log \max _{\xi \in \Omega _{k,k}} \mathsf P_\xi [\tau _k>t] \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (5.35)
                 Fix  \(\xi \in \Omega _{k,k}\) , and choose  \(z\in V\)  such that  \(\xi _z=1\) . Set  \( \xi ^-:=\xi -\delta _z\in \Omega _{k-1,k-1}\) . We couple the  \((k-1)\) -particle process started from  \(\xi ^-\)  with the process obtained from the  k -particle dynamics started from  \(\xi \)  by ignoring the particle initially at  z . Under this coupling, $$\begin{aligned} \tau _k \le \tau _{k-1} \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ Indeed, any contact among the retained  \(k-1\)  particles kills both systems, while contacts involving the ignored particle kill only the  k -particle system. Hence, for all  \(t\ge 0\) , $$\begin{aligned} \mathsf P_\xi [\tau _k>t] \le \mathsf P_{\xi ^-}[\tau _{k-1}>t] \le \max _{\xi '\in \Omega _{k-1,k-1}} \mathsf P_{\xi '}[\tau _{k-1}>t] \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ By taking the maximum over  \(\xi \in \Omega _{k,k}\) , the above bound and ( 5.35 ) give ( 5.33 ).  \(\square \) 
                 5.5  Proofs of Theorems  2.3  and  2.4 
                         We are ready to prove Theorems  2.3  and  2.4 . 
                   Proof of Theorem 2.3 
                   By Corollary  5.2  and ( 5.13 ) (we omit  G  from the notation), $$\begin{aligned} \lim _{\varepsilon \rightarrow 0} \frac{\textrm{gap}_k(\varepsilon \hat{\alpha })}{\varepsilon }= \textrm{gap}_\textrm{RW} (\hat{\alpha }) \wedge \min _{m \in \llbracket 2,k \rrbracket } \lambda _{k,m}(\hat{\alpha }) \; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (5.36)
                 whereas by ( 5.15 ) and ( 5.34 ), $$\begin{aligned} \min _{m \in \llbracket 2,k \rrbracket } \lambda _{k,m}(\hat{\alpha }) = \lambda _{2,2}(\hat{\alpha }) \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ Hence, since the limit in ( 5.36 ) is strictly positive, Theorem  2.3  holds true.  \(\square \) 
                
                   Proof of Theorem 2.4 
                   Recall that, for all graphs  G  and site weights  \(\hat{\alpha }\) , we have  \({\textrm{gap}_1(G, \varepsilon \hat{\alpha })} =\varepsilon \, \textrm{gap}_\textrm{RW} (G,\hat{\alpha }) \) ,  \(\varepsilon >0\) ; moreover, by ( 5.36 ) for  \(k=2\) , we have $$\begin{aligned} \lim _{\varepsilon \rightarrow 0} \frac{\textrm{gap}_2(G, \varepsilon \hat{\alpha })}{\varepsilon } = \textrm{gap}_\textrm{RW} (G,\hat{\alpha }) \wedge \lambda _{2,2}(G,\hat{\alpha }) \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ Thus, it suffices to verify the existence of  G  and  \(\hat{\alpha }\)  such that $$\begin{aligned} \lambda _{2,2} (G,\hat{\alpha }) < \textrm{gap}_\textrm{RW} (G,\hat{\alpha }) \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (5.37)
                 In particular, let us recall from the discussion in Sect.  5.4 , that  \(\lambda _{2,2}\)  is the smallest eigenvalue of (the negative of) the generator describing two independent  \(\textrm{RW}(G,\hat{\alpha })\) , having  \(\hat{\alpha }=(\hat{\alpha }_x)_{x\in V}\)  as a (non-normalized) reversible measure, and which get instantaneously killed when attempting to get to a mutual graph-distance strictly smaller than two. 
                   In view of this, it is not surprising that the standard discrete torus  \(\mathbb {T}_N^d\) , with dimension  \(d\ge 2\) , size  \(N\in \mathbb {N}\) , and homogeneous site weights, satisfies ( 5.37 ), provided that  N  is large enough. From now on, we fix  \(\hat{\alpha }\equiv 1\) ,  \(d\ge 2\) ,  \(N\in \mathbb {N}\) , and $$\begin{aligned} G=\mathbb {T}_N^d\; \text {,}\;\;\text {with}\ c_{xy}\equiv 1\ \text {for all neighboring sites}\ x,y\in \mathbb {T}_N^d\; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ and drop them from the notation, simply writing, e.g.,  \(\lambda _{2,2}\)  and  \(\textrm{gap}_\textrm{RW}\)  instead of  \(\lambda _{2,2}(G,\hat{\alpha })\)  and  \(\textrm{RW}(G,\hat{\alpha })\) . Later on, we will take the limit  \(N\rightarrow \infty \) . 
                   Consider the positions  \((X_t,Y_t)_{t\ge 0}\)  of two independent particles on  \(\mathbb {T}_N^d\) , each one jumping with unit rate to any of its neighbors. Observe that  \((X_t, Y_t)_{t\ge 0}\)  is reversible with respect to the uniform measure  \(\kappa =\kappa _{N,d}\)  on  \(\mathbb {T}_N^d \times \mathbb {T}_N^d\) . In what follows, let  \(\mathsf P_{(x,y)}\)  and  \(\mathsf E_{(x,y)}\)  denote the law and corresponding expectation with respect to the law of  \((X_t,Y_t)_{t\ge 0}\)  when starting from  \((x,y)\in \mathbb {T}_N^d\times \mathbb {T}_N^d\) . It is well known (see, e.g., [ LP17 , Sections 12.3.1 and 12.4]) that, for all  \(N\in \mathbb {N}\)  large enough and all  \(d\ge 1\) , we have $$\begin{aligned} {\textrm{gap}_\textrm{RW} =2\left( 1-\cos \frac{2\pi }{N}\right) \ge \frac{\pi ^2}{N^2}\; \text {.}} \end{aligned}$$ 
                    (5.38)
                 Letting $$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {W}:=\{(x,y)\in \mathbb {T}^d_N: \textrm{dist}_{\mathbb {T}_N^d}(x,y)\ge 2\} \; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ by the second inequality in [ Her23 , Eq. (1.7)], we have $$\begin{aligned} \lambda _{2,2} \le \frac{1}{\max _{ \mathcal {U}\subseteq \mathcal {W}} \mathsf E_{\kappa ( \,\cdot \,| \mathcal {U})} [ \tau _{\mathcal {U}^\complement }] }\le \frac{1}{\mathsf E_{\kappa ( \,\cdot \,| \mathcal {W})} [ \tau _{\mathcal {W}^\complement }] } \; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (5.39)
                 where in the second step we substituted  \(\mathcal {U}=\mathcal {W}\) . The last denominator in ( 5.39 ) is almost the mean meeting time of two independent random walks, both being uniformly distributed on  \(\mathbb {T}_N^d\)  (and independently) at time  \(t=0\) . This latter quantity is known [ Cox89 , Theorem 4] to asymptotically diverge, as  \(N\rightarrow \infty \) , like  \(s_d(N)\)  given in ( 2.6 ). In the remainder of this proof, we show that the denominator in ( 5.39 ) follows the same asymptotic behavior. 
                   Since  \(\kappa (\mathcal {W}) = 1 - \frac{2d+1}{N^d}\) , we calculate $$\begin{aligned} \mathsf E_{\kappa ( \,\cdot \,\mid \Omega )} [ \tau _{\mathcal {W}^\complement }] = \left( 1-\frac{2d+1}{N^d}\right) ^{-1} \frac{1}{N^{2d}} \sum _{(x,y)\in \mathcal {W}} \mathsf E_{(x,y)} [\tau _{\mathcal {W}^\complement }] \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (5.40)
                 Moreover, since  \(Z_t:=X_t-Y_t\in \mathbb {T}^d_N\)  is a simple random walk (jumping to a nearest neighbor at rate 2), we have $$\begin{aligned} \mathsf E_{(x,y)} [\tau _{\mathcal {W}^\complement }] = \widehat{\mathsf E}_{x-y} [\hat{\tau }_1] \; \text {,}\;\;\quad (x,y)\in \mathcal {W}\; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (5.41)
                 where we use the hat-notation to refer to quantities related to  \(Z_t\) , while  \(\hat{\tau }_\ell \) ,  \(\ell \in \llbracket 0,\lfloor N/2\rfloor \rrbracket \) , denotes the first hitting time of the set $$\begin{aligned} \{z\in \mathbb {T}^d_N: \textrm{dist}_{\mathbb {T}_N^d}(z,0)=\ell \}\; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ By the strong Markov property and translation invariance of the dynamics, we have $$\begin{aligned} \widehat{\mathsf E}_{x-y} [\hat{\tau }_0] = \widehat{\mathsf E}_{x-y} [\hat{\tau }_1] + \widehat{\mathsf E}_{e_1} [\hat{\tau }_0] = \widehat{\mathsf E}_{x-y} [\hat{\tau }_1] + \widehat{\mathsf E}_0 [\hat{\tau }_0^+] - \widehat{\mathsf E}_0 [\hat{\tau }_1] \; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ where  \(\hat{\tau }_0^+\)  denotes the first return time to 0 for the walk  \(Z_t\)  started in 0, i.e., $$\begin{aligned} \hat{\tau }_0^+:=\inf \{t> \hat{\tau }_1: Z_t=0\}\; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ Clearly,  \(\widehat{\mathsf E}_0\, [\hat{\tau }_1] = \frac{1}{4d}\) , while, by Kac’s formula for return times (see, e.g., [ AF02 , p. 34, Lemma 2.25]), we obtain $$\begin{aligned} \widehat{\mathsf E}_0 [\hat{\tau }_0^+] = \frac{N^d}{4d}\; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ Hence, we get $$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \frac{1}{N^{2d}} \sum _{(x,y) \in \mathcal {W}} \widehat{\mathsf E}_{x-y} [\hat{\tau }_1]&= \frac{1}{N^{2d}} \sum _{(x,y) \in \mathcal {W}} \left( \widehat{\mathsf E}_{x-y} [\hat{\tau }_0] - \widehat{\mathsf E}_0 [\hat{\tau }_0^+] + \widehat{\mathsf E}_0 [\hat{\tau }_1] \right) \\&\ge \left\{ \frac{1}{N^{2d}}\sum _{(x,y) \in \mathcal {W}} \widehat{\mathsf E}_{x-y} [\hat{\tau }_0]\right\} - \frac{N^d}{4d} \; \text {.}\end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$ 
                    (5.42)
                 By [ Cox89 , Theorem 4] (see also [ Dur24 , Lemma 7.3.2] for a more recent textbook version), the expression between curly brackets is, for all  \(N\in \mathbb {N}\)  large enough, bounded below by  \(\mathfrak g_d\,s_d(N)\) , for some positive constant  \(\mathfrak g_d\)  (which, for  \(d\ge 3\) , is strictly larger than  \(\frac{1}{4d}\) , cf. [ Cox89 , Eq. (1.2)]). Hence, collecting ( 5.40 ), ( 5.41 ) and ( 5.42 ), we obtain, for some  \(C>0\)  (possibly depending only on  \(d\ge 2\) , but not on  \(N\in \mathbb {N}\) ) and for all  \(N\in \mathbb {N}\)  large enough, $$\begin{aligned} {\mathsf E}_{\kappa ( \,\cdot \,| \mathcal {W})} [ \tau _{\mathcal {W}^\complement }] \ge C s_d(N)\; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ which, along with ( 5.39 ), implies $$\begin{aligned} \lambda _{2,2} \le \frac{1}{Cs_d(N)} \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ Comparing this inequality with ( 5.38 ) (cf. ( 2.6 )) completes the proof of the theorem.  \(\square \) 
                 We conclude this section with a remark on the role of the directional limits in ( 2.8 ) in proving Theorem  2.3 . 
                   Remark 5.15 
                   (Directional  vs.  general limits) Theorem  2.3  is stated for directional limits  \(\alpha =\varepsilon \hat{\alpha }\) , with  \(\hat{\alpha }\in (0,\infty )^V\)  fixed and  \(\varepsilon \rightarrow 0\) ; see ( 2.8 ). Let us briefly explain what would be needed in order to pass from ( 2.8 ) to a completely general limit  \(\alpha \rightarrow 0\) . 
                   A natural target would be the lower bound (we omit  G  from the notation) $$\begin{aligned} \liminf _{\alpha \rightarrow 0} \frac{\textrm{gap}_k(\alpha )}{\textrm{gap}_2(\alpha )} \ge 1\; \text {,}\;\;\quad k\ge 3\; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (5.43)
                 Since, by ( 2.7 ), one has  \(\textrm{gap}_k(\alpha )\le \textrm{gap}_2(\alpha )\)  for all  \(k\ge 3\) , ( 5.43 ) would imply the full limit $$\begin{aligned} \lim _{\alpha \rightarrow 0} \frac{\textrm{gap}_k(\alpha )}{\textrm{gap}_2(\alpha )} =1\; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ By the sequential characterization of the liminf, it suffices to consider arbitrary sequences  \(\alpha _n\rightarrow 0\) , as  \(n\rightarrow \infty \) , in  \((0,\infty )^V\) . By setting $$\begin{aligned} \varepsilon _n:=(\alpha _n)_\textrm{max}=\Vert \alpha _n\Vert _\infty \; \text {,}\;\;\quad \beta _n:=\varepsilon _n^{-1}\alpha _n\; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ we have  \(\varepsilon _n\rightarrow 0\)  and, up to extracting a subsequence,  \(\beta _n\rightarrow \hat{\alpha }\) , for some  \(\hat{\alpha }\in [0,1]^V\)  with  \(\max _{x\in V}\hat{\alpha }_x=1\) . Along such a subsequence, the analogue of ( 5.1 ) reads as $$\begin{aligned} \varepsilon _n^{-1}L_{\alpha _n,k} = A_{\beta _n,k}+\varepsilon _n^{-1}B_k\; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ where  \(A_{\beta _n,k}\)  is the slow part and  \(B_k\)  is the fast part of the generator, see ( 5.2 ) and ( 5.3 ). Since  \(A_{\beta _n,k}\rightarrow A_{\hat{\alpha },k}\)  as finite matrices, the slow–fast reduction used in Proposition  5.1  applies, with minor notational changes, also to this  n -dependent situation. The limiting effective generator  \(\mathscr {G}_{\hat{\alpha },k}\)  is given by the same formula there. 
                   If  \(\hat{\alpha }\in (0,\infty )^V\) , then the rest of the proof of Theorem  2.3  goes through without changes: the limiting quantities  \(w_k(\hat{\alpha })\)  and  \(\lambda _{k,m}(\hat{\alpha })\)  appearing in ( 5.7 ) and ( 5.12 ) are exactly those analyzed in Sects.  5.2 – 5.4 . Thus, strictly positive subsequential directions do not create any additional difficulty. 
                   The only genuinely new case is when  \(\hat{\alpha }_x=0\)  for some  \(x\in V\) . Then, the limiting effective generator may acquire additional zero eigenvalues: sites with  \(\hat{\alpha }_x=0\)  may generate further transient states and, depending on the graph, may also disconnect irreducible components of the limiting effective dynamics. In particular, the effective gap at scale  \(\varepsilon _n=(\alpha _n)_\textrm{max}\)  may vanish, so that the relevant gaps may be of smaller order than  \(\varepsilon _n\) . In such a case, the first-order slow–fast limit does not determine the ratio  \(\textrm{gap}_k(\alpha _n)/\textrm{gap}_2(\alpha _n)\) ; one would have to analyze the next non-vanishing scales of the coordinates of  \(\alpha _n\) . Although one expects the same two-particle mechanism to arise also through this further multi-scale analysis, we do not pursue this extension here, and prefer to state Theorem  2.3  in the clean directional regime with  \(\hat{\alpha }\in (0,\infty )^V\)  fixed. 
                 6  Non-conservative SIP. Setting and Main Results In this section, we attach particle reservoirs to some sites of the graph. We describe this by introducing two sets of non-negative site parameters,  \(\omega =(\omega _x)_{x\in V}\)  and  \(\theta =(\theta _x)_{x\in V}\) . The former one represents the rates of interaction between a site and its reservoir. The latter one prescribes the reservoirs’ particle densities. Next to the inclusion particles’ motion considered so far, we now let particles be created and annihilated with rates resembling those of  \(\textrm{SIP}\) . 6.1  Model Let  \(G=(V,(c_{xy})_{x,y\in V})\)  and  \(\alpha =(\alpha _x)_{x\in V}\)  be as in Sect.  2 , and introduce some non-negative site weights  \(\omega =(\omega _x)_{x\in V}\)  and  \(\theta =(\theta _x)_{x\in V}\) . Then, we write  \(\textrm{SIP}(G,\alpha ,\omega ,\theta )\)  for the Markov process on Footnote  2 \(\Xi :=\cup _{k=0}^\infty \, \Xi _k\)  with infinitesimal generator  \(L_{G,\alpha ,\omega ,\theta }\)  given, for a bounded function  \(f\in \mathbb {R}^{\Xi }\)  and a configuration  \(\eta \in \Xi \) , by $$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned}&L_{G,\alpha ,\omega ,\theta }f(\eta )= \sum _{x,y\in V}c_{xy}\,\eta _x\left( \alpha _y+\eta _y\right) \left( f(\eta -\delta _x+\delta _y)-f(\eta )\right) \\&+ \sum _{x\in V}\omega _x\,\big \{ \eta _x\left( 1+\theta _x\right) \left( f(\eta -\delta _x)-f(\eta )\right) +\theta _x\left( \alpha _x+\eta _x\right) \left( f(\eta +\delta _x)-f(\eta )\right) \big \}\; \text {.}\end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$ 
                    (6.1)
                 Here, the first summation on the right-hand side corresponds with the conservative dynamics described by  \(L_{G,\alpha ,k}\)  in ( 2.1 ), and is referred to as bulk dynamics. The second summation concerns the particle creation-annihilation mechanism, and may be interpreted as a bulk-boundary dynamics. More in detail, particles at  \(x\in V\)  get killed independently, each at rate  \(\omega _x\left( 1+\theta _x\right) \) , while a new particle is created at  \(x\in V\)  with a configuration-dependent rate  \(\omega _x\,\theta _x\left( \alpha _x+\eta _x\right) \) . Furthermore, albeit the configuration space  \(\Xi \)  is countably infinite,  \(\textrm{SIP}(G,\alpha ,\omega ,\theta )\)  does not explode in finite time. Indeed, the total number of particles is stochastically dominated by a pure birth process with linearly growing rates (see, e.g., [ FGS22 , Proposition 2.1]). The long-time behavior of  \(\textrm{SIP}(G,\alpha ,\omega ,\theta )\)  clearly depends on the values of  \(\omega \)  and  \(\theta \) . To start with, we mention that  \(\omega _x=0\)  means that site  \(x\in V\)  does not directly exchange particles with any reservoirs, whereas  \(\theta _x=0\)  corresponds to having a purely absorbing reservoir at  \(x\in V\) . As a consequence, if  \(\omega \equiv 0\) , we recover the conservative case having, for any total number of particles  \(k\in \mathbb {N}\) ,  \(\mu _{\alpha ,k}\)  in ( 2.2 ) as a unique reversible measure. If  \(\theta \equiv 0\)  and  \(\omega \not \equiv 0\) , the system eventually empties out. In other words, if  \(\theta \)  vanishes on the support of  \(\omega \) , that is, $$\begin{aligned} \textrm{supp}(\omega ):=\{x\in V: \omega _x > 0\}\; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ then  \(\textrm{SIP}(G,\alpha ,\omega ,\theta )\)  has the empty configuration  \(\emptyset \in \Xi _0\)  as its unique stationary state, which, in turn, is absorbing. More generally, for  \(\omega \not \equiv 0\) , there exists a unique stationary measure for  \(\textrm{SIP}(G,\alpha ,\omega ,\theta )\) , referred to as  \(\mu _{G,\alpha ,\omega ,\theta }\) . In this situation, one typically distinguishes between two scenarios, commonly referred to as equilibrium and non-equilibrium, respectively. More in detail,  equilibrium  occurs if  \(\theta \)  is constant on  \(\textrm{supp}(\omega )\) . In this case,  \(\mu _{G,\alpha ,\omega ,\theta }\)  is reversible, in product form, and explicit (see, e.g., [ CGGR13 ,  FRS22 ]): assuming that  \(\theta \equiv \varrho > 0\) , then  \( \mu _{G,\alpha ,\omega ,\theta }= \nu _{\alpha ,\varrho }\) , where, for all  \(\eta \in \Xi \) , $$\begin{aligned} \nu _{\alpha ,\varrho }(\eta ):=\frac{1}{\mathcal {Z}_{\alpha ,\varrho }} \left( \frac{\varrho }{1+\varrho }\right) ^{|\eta |} \prod _{x\in V}\frac{\Gamma (\alpha _x+\eta _x)}{\Gamma (\alpha _x)\,\eta _x!}\; \text {,}\;\;\quad \text {with}\ \mathcal {Z}_{\alpha ,\varrho }:=(1+\varrho )^{|\alpha |}\; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (6.2)
                 Note that  \(\mu _{\alpha ,k}\)  in ( 2.2 ) is the canonical measure associated to the grand-canonical  \(\nu _{\alpha ,\varrho }\) . One speaks of  non-equilibrium  if  \(\theta \)  is non-constant on  \(\textrm{supp}(\omega )\) . Then,  \(\mu _{G,\alpha ,\omega ,\theta }\)  is, in general, neither reversible, nor in product form, nor explicit. We will not need to know more on the structure of this steady state; for further details, see, e.g., [ FRS22 ] and references therein. 6.2  Spectral gap identity when  \(\theta \equiv 0\) 
                         We start with the analysis of the purely absorbing  \(\textrm{SIP}\) . As already anticipated in Sect.  1.4 , we shall see that understanding the case  \(\theta \equiv 0\)  is one of the key ingredients for the spectral analysis of the setting with general  \(\theta \) . Observe that substituting  \(\theta \equiv 0\)  in ( 6.1 ) gives rise to a system in which all particles are never created, and get annihilated one at the time, until the system eventually gets empty. In particular,  \(L_{G,\alpha ,\omega ,0}\) , interpreted as an infinite matrix, is similar to a block lower triangular one. Moreover, the blocks on the diagonal — each for any total number of particles  \(k\in \mathbb {N}_0\)  in the bulk of the system — are of finite size and describe the following finite-state Markov chains. The case  \(k=1\)  corresponds to a process with just one particle in the bulk, which moves as  \(\textrm{RW}(G,\alpha )\)  defined in Sect.  2 , additionally killed at rate  \(\omega _x\ge 0\)  when sitting on  \(x\in V\) . We refer to this process as  \(\textrm{RW}(G,\alpha ,\omega )\) . The case  \(k\ge 2\)  is similar, with  k  interacting particles evolving in the bulk as  \(\textrm{SIP}_k(G,\alpha )\) , each independently killed with rate  \(\omega _x\ge 0\)  when sitting at  \(x\in V\) . Each of these Markov chains, say  \(\textrm{SIP}_k(G,\alpha ,\omega )\) , is sub-stochastic. Moreover, since the conservative  \(\textrm{SIP}_k(G,\alpha )\)  admits  \(\mu _{\alpha ,k}\)  in ( 2.2 ) as its unique reversible measure, all eigenvalues of (the negative of) the generator of  \(\textrm{SIP}_k(G,\alpha ,\omega )\)  (see ( 7.1 )) are real; as the conservative system is irreducible,  \(\textrm{supp}(\omega )\ne \varnothing \)  further ensures that such eigenvalues are strictly positive. Letting  \(\textrm{gap}_k(G,\alpha ,\omega )\) ,  \(k\ge 1\) , be the lowest of such eigenvalues, we write (cf. ( 2.4 )) $$\begin{aligned} \textrm{gap}_\textrm{SIP}(G,\alpha ,\omega )=\inf _{k\ge 1}\textrm{gap}_k(G,\alpha ,\omega )\; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (6.3)
                 Similarly, we introduce the spectral gap of  \(\textrm{RW}(G,\alpha ,\omega )\)  as $$\begin{aligned} \textrm{gap}_\textrm{RW}(G,\alpha ,\omega )= \textrm{gap}_1(G,\alpha ,\omega )\; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (6.4)
                 Our main result of this section establishes that, as soon as the killing rate  \(\omega \)  is not identically zero, the spectral gaps in ( 6.3 ) and ( 6.4 ) coincide, regardless of the underlying geometry and of the site weights — even when  \(\alpha _\textrm{min}\in (0,1)\) . This spectral gap identity stands in stark contrast to the behavior observed in the conservative setting, where restrictions are imposed on the value of  \(\alpha _\textrm{min}\) . 
                   Theorem 6.1 
                   ( \(\theta \equiv 0\) ) For all graphs  \(G=(V,(c_{xy})_{x,y\in V})\) , and site weights  \(\alpha =(\alpha _x)_{x\in V}\) ,  \(\omega =(\omega _x)_{x\in V}\)  with  \(\omega \ne 0\) , we have $$\begin{aligned} \textrm{gap}_\textrm{SIP}(G,\alpha ,\omega )= \textrm{gap}_\textrm{RW}(G,\alpha ,\omega )\; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                 Remarkably, the proof of the above identity (deferred to Sect.  7.1 ) is quite elementary. 6.3  General setting When dealing with the general case  \(\theta \ne 0\) , we must specify the infinite-dimensional function analytic setting that we work in. For this purpose, we define a class of functions on  \(\Xi \)  (Definition  6.2 ), written in terms of orthogonal  “Meixner” polynomial dualities introduced in [ FRS22 , Section 4], which take the following form: for all  \(\varrho \ge 0\) , $$\begin{aligned} D_{\alpha ,\varrho }(\xi ,\eta )=\prod _{x\in V}d_{\alpha _x,\varrho }(\xi _x,\eta _x)\; \text {,}\;\;\quad \eta \in \Xi \; \text {,}\;\;\xi \in \Xi _k\; \text {,}\;\;k\ge 0\; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (6.5)
                 where, for  \(\varrho =0\) , $$\begin{aligned} d_{\alpha _x,0}(\xi _x,\eta _x) = \frac{\eta _x!}{\left( \eta _x-\xi _x\right) !}\frac{\Gamma (\alpha _x)}{\Gamma (\alpha _x+\xi _x)}\mathbb {1}_{\left\{ \xi _x\le \eta _x\right\} }\; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ whereas, for  \(\varrho >0\) , $$\begin{aligned} d_{\alpha _x,\varrho }(\xi _x,\eta _x) =\sum _{\ell _x=0}^{\xi _x}\left( {\begin{array}{c}\xi _x\\ \ell _x\end{array}}\right) \,d_{\alpha _x,0}(\ell _x,\eta _x)\left( -\varrho \right) ^{\xi _x-\ell _x}= \left( -\varrho \right) ^{\xi _k}{{}_2F_1(-\xi _x,-\eta _x;\alpha _x;-\varrho ^{-1})} \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ This last identity is just a rewriting in terms of the ordinary hypergeometric function. Let us observe that  \(D_{\alpha ,\varrho }(\emptyset ,\,\cdot \,)\equiv 1\)  and that, for all  \(k\in \mathbb {N}\)  and  \(\xi \in \Xi _k\) ,  \(\eta \in \Xi \longmapsto D_{\alpha ,\varrho }(\xi ,\eta )\in \mathbb {R}\)  is a  k -th order polynomial in  \(\eta =(\eta _x)_{x\in V}\) . Moreover, as we shall show in Sect.  7.2  below, $$\begin{aligned} \{D_{\alpha ,\varrho }(\xi ,\,\cdot \,): |\xi |\le k\}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (6.6)
                 is a linearly independent set which spans the space of polynomials in  \(\eta \)  of order  \(\le k\) . This and the two other properties presented in Proposition  7.2  below turn the functions in ( 6.5 ) into a convenient basis of polynomials on  \(\Xi \) . 
                   Definition 6.2 
                   For all  \(\varrho > 0\) , integers  \(k\ge 1\) , and functions  \(\psi \in \mathbb {R}^{\Xi _k}\) , define $$\begin{aligned} F_{\alpha ,\varrho }^\psi (\eta ):=\sum _{\xi \in \Xi _k}\mu _{\alpha ,k}(\xi )\,\psi (\xi )\, D_{\alpha ,\varrho }(\xi ,\eta )\; \text {,}\;\;\quad \eta \in \Xi \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (6.7)
                 When  \(k=0\)  and, thus,  \(\psi \in \mathbb {R}\)  is a scalar, we conventionally set  \(F_{\alpha ,\varrho }^\psi \equiv \psi \in \mathbb {R}\) . 
                 Remark that, as long as  \(\psi \ne 0\) , the function in ( 6.7 ) is a non-vanishing  k -th order polynomial in the variables  \(\eta =(\eta _x)_{x\in V}\) . In particular, expressions like  \(L_{G,\alpha ,\omega ,\theta } F_{\alpha ,\varrho }^\psi \)  will always be well defined. Finally, introduce the operators  \(\mathfrak b_{\alpha ,\omega ,\varrho ,k-1}^\theta : \mathbb {R}^{\Xi _k}\rightarrow \mathbb {R}^{\Xi _{k-1}}\) ,  \(k\ge 1\) , acting on functions  \(\psi \in \mathbb {R}^{\Xi _k}\)  as $$\begin{aligned} \mathfrak b_{\alpha ,\omega ,\varrho ,k-1}^\theta \psi (\zeta ):=k\sum _{x\in V}\omega _x\left( \theta _x-\varrho \right) \frac{\alpha _x+\zeta _x}{|\alpha |+k-1}\,\psi (\zeta +\delta _x)\; \text {,}\;\;\quad \zeta \in \Xi _{k-1}\; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (6.8)
                 Clearly, we have $$\begin{aligned} \mathfrak b_{\alpha ,\omega ,\varrho ,k-1}^\theta = 0\; \text {,}\;\;\quad \text {if}\quad \theta \equiv \varrho \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (6.9)
                 The following theorem, whose proof is postponed to Sect.  7.2  below, is stated for any graph  G  and site weights  \(\alpha \) ,  \(\omega \) , and  \(\theta \) . 
                   Theorem 6.3 
                   Fix  \(\varrho >0\)  and recall Definition  6.2 .  
                     
                       (a) 
                      
                         Given  \(k\ge 0\) , any eigenpair  \(\lambda ,\psi \)  of the  k -particle purely-absorbing system solves  $$\begin{aligned} L_{G,\alpha ,\omega ,\theta }F_{\alpha ,\varrho }^\psi = -\lambda \, F_{\alpha ,\varrho }^{\psi } + F_{\alpha ,\varrho }^{\mathfrak b_{\alpha ,\omega ,\varrho ,k-1}^\theta \psi }\; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (6.10)
                 
                      
                     
                     
                       (b) 
                      
                         The functions  \(F_{\alpha ,\varrho }^\psi \)  as in Definition  6.2  may be chosen to form an orthogonal basis of  \(L^2(\nu _{\alpha ,\varrho })\) . 
                      
                     
                   
                 Let us comment on two powerful consequences of the above theorem in the reversible case  \(\theta \equiv \varrho \)  (we discuss the non-reversible case in Remark  6.5  below; see also Remark  6.6  for a comparison with the symmetric  exclusion  process). The first item tells us that any eigenvalue of the purely absorbing system with  \(k\ge 1\)  particles is one also for the general system and, moreover, it provides a constructive procedure to build eigenfunctions from those of the  k -particle absorbing system. Indeed, by ( 6.9 ), the identity in ( 6.10 ) becomes the eigenvalue equation $$\begin{aligned} L_{G,\alpha ,\omega ,\theta }F_{\alpha ,\varrho }^\psi = -\lambda \, F_{\alpha ,\varrho }^\psi \; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ because the second term on the right-hand side of ( 6.10 ) vanishes when  \(\theta \equiv \varrho \) . The second item guarantees the existence of a complete orthonormal system of eigenfunctions of  \(L_{G,\alpha ,\omega ,\theta }\) . This, combined with the fact that the generator is self-adjoint in the associated  \(L^2\) -space, ensures that  \(-L_{G,\alpha ,\omega ,\theta }\)  has a pure point spectrum therein, all contained in  \([0,\infty )\) , with a gap after the zero eigenvalue coinciding with that of the purely absorbing system. Letting $$\begin{aligned}\textrm{gap}_\textrm{SIP}(G,\alpha ,\omega ,\varrho )\end{aligned}$$ denote this spectral gap when  \(\theta \equiv \varrho >0\) , Theorem  6.1  readily implies the following result. 
                   Corollary 6.4 
                   ( \(\theta \equiv \varrho \) ) For all graphs  \(G=(V,(c_{xy})_{x,y\in V})\) , and site weights  \(\alpha =(\alpha _x)_{x\in V}\) ,  \(\omega =(\omega _x)_{x\in V}\)  with  \(\omega \ne 0\) , we have $$\begin{aligned} \textrm{gap}_\textrm{SIP}(G,\alpha ,\omega ,\varrho ) = \textrm{gap}_\textrm{RW}(G,\alpha ,\omega )\; \text {,}\;\;\quad \varrho > 0\; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                 The above result is stated in the reversible setting  \(\theta \equiv \varrho \) . The next two remarks clarify the corresponding non-reversible picture: for the infinite-state  \(\textrm{SIP}\)  one has to distinguish algebraic eigenfunction lifting from a full spectral statement on a prescribed function space (Remark  6.5 ), whereas in finite-state analogues such as the partial symmetric exclusion process this distinction disappears (Remark  6.6 ). 
                   Remark 6.5 
                   (Non-reversible setting) In view of Theorem  6.3 , it is plausible to expect that Corollary  6.4  should extend, in some sense, to the non-reversible setting as well. Indeed, since the two functions on the right-hand side of ( 6.10 ) are polynomials of order  k  and  \(\ell \le k-1\) , this guarantees that  \(\lambda \)  therein lies in the point spectrum of  \(L_{G,\alpha ,\omega ,\theta }\) , for all  \(\theta =(\theta _x)_{x\in V}\) . However, in absence of a natural Hilbert space ensuring that such eigenfunctions form a complete orthonormal system when  \(\theta \not \equiv \mathrm{const.}\)  on  \(\textrm{supp}(\omega )\) , the presence, e.g., of a continuous part of the spectrum (a property which also depends on the choice of the underlying reference function space) is what Theorem  6.3  does not exclude. 
                
                   Remark 6.6 
                   (Symmetric exclusion processes & Co.) The proof of Theorem  6.3 (a) — based on orthogonal-polynomial dualities, annihilation and creation operators — carries over to other non-equilibrium systems enjoying similar properties, e.g., [ RS18 ,  FRS22 ,  GRT25 ]. As an example, we mention the symmetric (partial) exclusion process ( \(\textrm{SEP}\) ) in contact with reservoirs (see, e.g., [ FRS22 ,  Sal23 ,  Sal24 ]), whose generator is readily obtained from  \(L_{G,\alpha ,\omega ,\theta }\)  in ( 6.1 ) by further imposing  \(\alpha =(\alpha _x)_{x\in V}\subset \mathbb {N}\)  and  \(\theta =(\theta _x)_{x\in V}\in [0,1]\) , and changing the plus signs in  \(\alpha _x+\eta _x\)  and  \(1+\theta _x\)  therein into minus signs. In this case, the configuration space is simply  \(\prod _{x\in V}\{0,1,\ldots ,\alpha _x\}\) , thus finite, and the technical issues explained in Remark  6.5  do not arise: the spectrum is just the union of finitely many eigenvalues. 
                   As our proof arguments used for  \(\textrm{SIP}\)  adapt to the open  \(\textrm{SEP}\) , one obtains analogues of Theorems  6.1  and  6.3 : on the one hand, the eigenvalues of the open  \(\textrm{SEP}\)  with arbitrary reservoir densities  \(\theta \in [0,1]^V\)  coincide with those of the purely absorbing case  \(\theta \equiv 0\) , and are therefore independent of  \(\theta \) ; on the other hand, importing the notation used above, one has  \(\textrm{gap}_\textrm{SEP}(G,\alpha ,\omega )=\textrm{gap}_\textrm{RW}(G,\alpha ,\omega )\) . These two results further ensure that $$\begin{aligned} \textrm{gap}_\textrm{SEP}(G,\alpha ,\omega ,\theta ) = \textrm{gap}_\textrm{RW}(G,\alpha ,\omega )\; \text {,}\;\;\quad \theta \in [0,1]^V \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ This, thus, recovers, in the reversible case, the spectral-gap identity of [ Sal23 , Corollary 1], and, for general reservoir densities, the corresponding result of [ Sal24 , Theorem 2]. 
                 7  Proofs of Theorems  6.1  and  6.3 
                      The following two sections may be essentially read independently. 7.1  Proof of Theorem  6.1 
                         For every  \(k\ge 1\) , the  k -particle purely absorbing system (i.e., when  \(\theta \equiv 0\) ) is described by the infinitesimal generator $$\begin{aligned} L_{G,\alpha ,\omega ,k}f(\eta ):=L_{G,\alpha ,k}f(\eta ) - f(\eta )\sum _{x\in V}\omega _x\, \eta _x \; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (7.1)
                 where  \(f\in \mathbb {R}^{\Xi _k}\) ,  \(\eta \in \Xi _k\) , and  \(L_{G,\alpha ,k}\)  given in ( 2.1 ). Equivalently, if  \(f\in \mathbb {R}^{\Xi _k}\)  is extended to  \(\Xi \)  by setting  \(f\equiv 0\)  on  \(\Xi \setminus \Xi _k\) , then the restriction to  \(\Xi _k\)  of the generator in ( 6.1 ) with  \(\theta \equiv 0\)  is precisely the operator in ( 7.1 ). Note that, when the process is in configuration  \(\eta \in \Xi _k\) , it gets killed with rate  \(\sum _{x\in V}\omega _x\,\eta _x\ge 0\) . Recall the definition of the probability measure  \(\mu _{\alpha ,k}\)  in ( 2.2 ). Since  \(L_{G,\alpha ,\omega ,k}\)  is the sum of a self-adjoint operator on  \(L^2(\mu _{\alpha ,k})\)  and a multiplicative one, it is self-adjoint on  \(L^2(\mu _{\alpha ,k})\)  and, thus, all the eigenvalues of  \(-L_{G,\alpha ,\omega ,k}\)  are real and non-negative. Additionally, since the conservative dynamics is irreducible and the multiplication operator is non-positive, all such eigenvalues are actually strictly positive. In particular, we obtain that  \(\textrm{gap}_k(G,\alpha ,\omega )\) , the lowest of such eigenvalues, is positive and, by the Perron-Frobenius theorem, characterized as the worst-case exponential killing rate for large times: letting  \(\tau _k\)  denote the first time at which one of the  k  particles gets killed, $$\begin{aligned} \textrm{gap}_k(G,\alpha ,\omega )= - \lim _{t\rightarrow \infty }\frac{1}{t}\log \max _{\eta \in \Xi _k} \mathbb {P}_\eta ^{G,\alpha ,\omega ,k}[\tau _k>t] \; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ Thanks to this asymptotic identity, the assertion of Theorem  6.1  boils down to show that, for all  \(k\ge 2\) , $$\begin{aligned} \max _{\eta \in \Xi _k}\mathbb {P}_\eta ^{G,\alpha ,\omega ,k}[\tau _k>t]\le \max _{x\in V} \mathbb {P}_{\delta _x}^{G,\alpha ,\omega ,1}[\tau _1>t]\; \text {,}\;\;\quad t\ge 0\; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (7.2)
                 For this purpose, we introduce a lookdown representation of the particle system with killing, extending the one known within the conservative setting [ KS24 , Appendix A]. We need to introduce a bit of notation. For every  \(k\ge 2\) , labeled configuration  \(\textbf{x}=(x_1,\ldots , x_k)\in V^k\) , and permutation  \(\varsigma \in S_k\) , we write  \(\varsigma \textbf{x}= (x_{\varsigma (1)},\ldots ,x_{\varsigma (k)})\in V^k\) . Further,  \(\mathcal {S}_k: \mathbb {R}^{V^k}\rightarrow \mathbb {R}^{V^k}\)  denotes the symmetrization operator, that is, $$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {S}_k \varphi (\textbf{x})= \frac{1}{k!}\sum _{\varsigma \in S_k}\varphi (\varsigma \textbf{x})\; \text {,}\;\;\quad \varphi \in \mathbb {R}^{V^k}\; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ whereas  \(\Phi _k(\textbf{x})=\sum _{i=1}^k \delta _{x_i}\in \Xi _k\)  is the operator which removes the particles’ labels. 
                   Proposition 7.1 
                   (Lookdown representation of  \(\textrm{SIP}\)  with killing) For all  \(k\ge 2\) , we have $$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {S}_k {\mathscr {L}}_{G,\alpha ,\omega ,k}(f\circ \Phi _k)= (L_{G,\alpha ,\omega ,k}f)\circ \Phi _k\; \text {,}\;\;\quad f \in \mathbb {R}^{\Xi _k}\; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (7.3)
                 where  \({\mathscr {L}}_{G,\alpha ,\omega ,k}\)  denotes the generator of the lookdown  \(\textrm{SIP}_k(G,\alpha ,\omega )\)  given, for all  \(\varphi \in \mathbb {R}^{V^k}\)  and  \(\textbf{x}\in V^k\) , by $$\begin{aligned} {\mathscr {L}}_{G,\alpha ,\omega ,k}\varphi (\textbf{x})= {\mathscr {L}}_{G,\alpha ,k}\varphi (\textbf{x}) - \varphi (\textbf{x})\sum _{i=1}^k \omega _{x_i}\; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (7.4)
                 Here,  \({\mathscr {L}}_{G,\alpha ,k}\varphi (\textbf{x})\)  reads as $$\begin{aligned} {\mathscr {L}}_{G,\alpha ,k}\varphi (\textbf{x}) = \sum _{i=1}^k \sum _{x,y\in V}c_{xy}\,\delta _{x,x_i}\left( \alpha _y+2\sum _{j=1}^{i-1}\delta _{y,x_j}\right) \left( \varphi (\textbf{x}_i^y)-\varphi (\textbf{x})\right) \; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ and stands for the conservative part of the lookdown dynamics, with  \(\textbf{x}_i^y\in V^k\)  denoting the configuration obtained from  \(\textbf{x}\in V^k\)  by setting the  i -th coordinate equal to  \(y\in V\) . 
                
                   Proof 
                   By [ KS24 , Proposition A.4], an identity which is analogous to that in ( 7.3 ) holds for the conservative dynamics, i.e., $$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {S}_k{\mathscr {L}}_{G,\alpha ,k}(f\circ \Phi _k)= (L_{G,\alpha ,k}f)\circ \Phi _k\; \text {,}\;\;\quad f \in \mathbb {R}^{\Xi _k}\; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ Hence, by linearity, it suffices to prove the identity only for the multiplication operators in ( 7.1 ) and ( 7.4 ) describing the killing part. Nevertheless, this identity holds trivially because particles are killed independently (or, equivalently, because  \(\textbf{x}=(x_1,\ldots , x_k)\mapsto (f\circ \Phi _k)(\textbf{x})\sum _{i=1}^k \omega _{x_i}\)  is invariant under permutations).  \(\square \) 
                 We now conclude the proof of Theorem  6.1 . 
                   Proof of Theorem 6.1 
                   Let  \(\mathcal {P}_t^{G,\alpha ,\omega ,k} = e^{t{\mathscr {L}}_{G,\alpha ,\omega ,k}}\) ,  \(t\ge 0\) , be the lookdown semigroup. In conclusion, taking  \(f=\mathbb {1}_{\Xi _k}\in \mathbb {R}^{\Xi _k}\)  in ( 7.3 ) and observing that  \(\mathbb {1}_{\Xi _k}\circ \Phi _k= (\otimes _{i=1}^k\,\mathbb {1}_V)\in \mathbb {R}^{V^k}\) , we obtain, for all  \(\textbf{x}\in V^k\)  and  \(t> 0\) , $$\begin{aligned} \max _{\eta \in \Xi _k}\mathbb {P}_\eta ^{G,\alpha ,\omega ,k}[\tau _k>t]&\le \max _{\textbf{x}\in V^k} \mathcal {P}_t^{G,\alpha ,\omega ,k}(\otimes _{i=1}^k\,\mathbb {1}_V)(\textbf{x})\\&\le \max _{x\in V}\mathcal {P}_t^{G,\alpha ,\omega ,1}\mathbb {1}_V(x)\; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ where the second inequality follows from neglecting all particles with labels  \(\ell =2,\ldots , k\)  (in other words, bounding  \(\otimes _{i=2}^k\,\mathbb {1}_V\le 1\) ), the first being independent from them. Since this last expression equals the right-hand side of ( 7.2 ), this proves the inequality therein and, thus, concludes the proof of the theorem.  \(\square \) 
                 7.2  Proof of Theorem  6.3 
                         Our main ingredient will be the duality property of  \(\textrm{SIP}\)  in contact with reservoirs (e.g., [ GKRV09 ,  CGGR13 ]), in particular, the orthogonal polynomial duality functions found in [ FRS22 ]. We shall not assume any prior knowledge on this topic, but rather exploit a few properties taken from [ KLS10 ,  RS18 ,  FRS22 ], which, for the reader’s convenience, we collect in the following proposition. For notational convenience, we simply write  \(L_\theta =L_{G,\alpha ,\omega ,\theta }\)  and  \(L_k=L_{G,\alpha ,\omega ,k}\)  (defined in ( 6.1 ) and ( 7.1 ), respectively). 
                   Proposition 7.2 
                   Fix  \(\varrho > 0\) .  
                     
                       (a) 
                      
                         Orthogonality and completeness.  Let  \(\left\langle \,\cdot \,\, \Bigg | \vert \, \,\cdot \, \right\rangle _{\alpha ,\varrho }\)  denote the inner product in  \(L^2(\nu _{\alpha ,\varrho })\)  (see ( 6.2 )). Then, for all integers  \(k,\ell \ge 1\)  and  \(\xi \in \Xi _k\) ,  \(\zeta \in \Xi _\ell \) ,  $$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \left\langle D_{\alpha ,\varrho }(\xi ,\,\cdot \,)\, \Bigg | \vert \, D_{\alpha ,\varrho }(\zeta ,\,\cdot \,) \right\rangle _{\alpha ,\varrho }&= {\mathbb {1}_{\{\xi =\zeta \}}}\,\frac{\varrho ^k\left( 1+\varrho \right) ^k}{Z_{\alpha ,k}}\,{\mu _{\alpha ,k}(\xi )^{-1}} \end{aligned}\; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (7.5)
                  Moreover,  \(\left\{ D_{\alpha ,\varrho }(\xi ,\,\cdot \,)\right\} _{k\ge 0,\,\xi \in \Xi _k}\)  is a complete system of  \(L^2(\nu _{\alpha ,\varrho })\) . 
                      
                     
                     
                       (b) 
                      
                         Duality.  For all  \(\theta =(\theta _x)_{x\in V}\)  and  \(\eta \in \Xi \) , we have  $$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} L_\theta D_{\alpha ,\varrho }(\xi ,\,\cdot \,)(\eta )&= L_k D_{\alpha ,\varrho }(\,\cdot \,,\eta )(\xi ) \\&+ \sum _{x\in V}\omega _x\,\xi _x\left( \theta _x-\varrho \right) D_{\alpha ,\varrho }(\xi -\delta _x,\eta ) \; \text {.}\end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$ 
                    (7.6)
                 
                      
                     
                   
                
                   Remark 7.3 
                   Since  \(\left\{ D_{\alpha ,\varrho }(\xi ,\,\cdot \,)\right\} _{\ell \le k,\,\xi \in \Xi _\ell }\)  spans all polynomials of order  \(\le k\) , by ( 7.6 ),  \(L_\theta =L_{G,\alpha ,\omega ,\theta }\)  leaves invariant such a subspace of polynomials. 
                
                   Proof of Theorem 6.3 
                   The rest of the proof of Theorem  6.3  is routine. Indeed, Theorem  6.3 (a) follows by linearity and the duality relation in ( 7.6 ): recalling  \(F_{\alpha ,\varrho }^\psi \)  from Definition  6.2 , first we obtain $$\begin{aligned} L_\theta F_{\alpha ,\varrho }^\psi (\eta )&= \sum _{\xi \in \Xi _k} \mu _{\alpha ,k}(\xi )\, \psi (\xi )\, L_\theta D_{\alpha ,\varrho }(\xi ,\,\cdot \,)(\eta )\\&= \sum _{\xi \in \Xi _k}\mu _{\alpha ,k}(\xi )\,\psi (\xi )\, L_k D_{\alpha ,\varrho }(\,\cdot \,,\eta )(\xi )\\&+\sum _{\xi \in \Xi _k}\mu _{\alpha ,k}(\xi )\,\psi (\xi ) \sum _{x\in V}\omega _x\,\xi _x\left( \theta _x-\varrho \right) D_{\alpha ,\varrho }(\xi -\delta _x,\eta )\; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ which, by ( 3.11 ) and the definition of  \(\mathfrak b_{\alpha ,\omega ,\varrho ,k-1}^\varrho \)  in ( 6.8 ), rearranges as $$\begin{aligned}&\sum _{\xi \in \Xi _k}\mu _{\alpha ,k}(\xi )\,\psi (\xi )\, L_k D_{\alpha ,\varrho }(\,\cdot \,,\eta )(\xi )+F_{\alpha ,\varrho }^{\mathfrak b_{\alpha ,\omega ,\theta ,k-1}^\varrho \psi }\; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ By the symmetry of  \(L_k\)  in  \(L^2(\mu _{\alpha ,k})\) , we finally obtain $$\begin{aligned} L_\theta F_{\alpha ,\varrho }^\psi (\eta )=\sum _{\xi \in \Xi _k}\mu _{\alpha ,k}(\xi )\, L_k\psi (\xi )\, D_{\alpha ,\varrho }(\xi ,\eta )+F_{\alpha ,\varrho }^{\mathfrak b_{\alpha ,\omega ,\theta ,k-1}^\varrho \psi }\; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ and the desired claim follows if  \(L_k\psi =-\lambda \,\psi \) . 
                   The second item in Theorem  6.3  is a direct consequence of the symmetry of  \(L_k\)  in  \(L^2(\mu _{\alpha ,k})\)  (thus, the orthogonality relation of eigenfunctions  \(\psi .\varphi \in \mathbb {R}^{\Xi _l}\)  associated to distinct eigenvalues) and the orthogonality relation in ( 7.5 ): for all integers  \(k,\ell \ge 0\) , all eigenfunctions  \(\psi \in \mathbb {R}^{\Xi _k}\)  and  \(\varphi \in \mathbb {R}^{\Xi _\ell }\)  of  \(L_k\)  and  \(L_\ell \) , respectively, we have $$\begin{aligned} \big \langle F_{\alpha ,\varrho }^\psi \big | F_{\alpha ,\varrho }^\varphi \big \rangle _{\alpha ,\varrho }= \mathbb {1}_{\left\{ k=\ell \right\} }\,\mathfrak c_{\alpha ,\varrho ,k} \left\langle \psi \, \Bigg | \vert \, \varphi \right\rangle _{\alpha ,k}\; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ for some constant  \(\mathfrak c_{\alpha ,\varrho ,k}>0\) . 
                   The completeness of the functions in ( 6.6 ) follows from a one-dimensional density result. Each marginal of  \(\nu _{\alpha ,\varrho }\)  is a negative-binomial measure, hence has exponential tails and a determinate Hamburger moment problem. By the classical theorem of M. Riesz [ Rie24 ] (see also [ Akh65 , Chapter 2, Section 3 and Corollary 2.3.3], [ BC81 ,  Ber96 ], and references therein), determinacy implies density of algebraic polynomials in the corresponding  \(L^2\) -space. Since  V  is finite and  \(\nu _{\alpha ,\varrho }\)  is a product measure on  \(\mathbb {N}_0^V\) , tensor products of these univariate polynomial bases are dense in  \(L^2(\nu _{\alpha ,\varrho })\) , yielding the desired completeness.  \(\square \) 
                 8  Extensions and Related Models In this last section, we extend our results to the Brownian energy process, and briefly discuss their connections with what is known for some closely related models. 8.1  Brownian energy process Given a graph  \(G=(V,(c_{xy})_{x,y\in V})\)  and site weights  \(\alpha =(\alpha _x)_{x\in V}\) , the corresponding Brownian energy process — referred to as  \(\textrm{BEP}(G,\alpha )\)  — is the diffusion on the probability simplex $$\begin{aligned} \varSigma :=\left\{ \zeta \in [0,1]^V: \sum _{x\in V}\zeta _x=1\right\} \; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (8.1)
                 with infinitesimal generator $$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {L}_{G,\alpha }= \frac{1}{2}\sum _{x,y\in V}c_{xy}\left\{ -\left( \alpha _y \zeta _x-\alpha _x\zeta _y\right) \left( \partial _{\zeta _x}-\partial _{\zeta _y}\right) +\zeta _x\zeta _y\left( \partial _{\zeta _x}-\partial _{\zeta _y}\right) ^2\right\} \; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ and the following Dirichlet distribution on  \(\varSigma \)  as its unique reversible measure $$\begin{aligned} \gamma _\alpha (\textrm{d}\zeta )= \left( \frac{1}{\textrm{B}(\alpha )}\prod _{x\in V} \zeta _x^{\alpha _x-1}\right) \textrm{d}\zeta \; \text {,}\;\;\quad \text {with}\ \textrm{B}(\alpha ):=\frac{\prod _{x\in V}\Gamma (\alpha _x)}{\Gamma (|\alpha |)}\; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                    (8.2)
                 Here,  \(\textrm{d}\zeta \)  stands for the uniform measure on  \(\varSigma \) . We refer to Sect.  1 , [ KS24 ] and references therein, for more information and history on this model. Here, we just mention that its mean field version (i.e., when  \(c_{xy}\equiv \mathrm{const.}\) ) coincides with the renowned multi-type Wright-Fisher diffusion with parent independent mutations (see, e.g., [ Shi77 ,  WZ19 ]). As shown in [ KS24 ],  \(\textrm{BEP}(G,\alpha )\)  inherits its eigenstructure from that of  \(\textrm{SIP}(G,\alpha )\) . More precisely, the Poisson intertwining relation [ KS24 , Eq. (4.1)] between the generators  \(\mathcal {L}_{G,\alpha }\)  and  \(L_{G,\alpha ,k}\)  allows to lift eigenfunctions of  \(\textrm{SIP}(G,\alpha )\)  to eigenfunctions of  \(\textrm{BEP}(G,\alpha )\) , both corresponding to the same eigenvalue. Using this intertwining relation (and, more specifically, that  \(\mathcal {L}_{G,\alpha }\)  maps the finite-dimensional subspace of polynomials of degree at most  k  into itself) and the fact  \(\mathcal {L}_{G,\alpha }\)  is a self-adjoint operator on  \(L^2(\gamma _\alpha )\) , one concludes that this lifting saturates all the spectral information (i.e., the spectrum of  \(\textrm{BEP}\)  is pure point and coincides with that of  \(\textrm{SIP}\) ; see [ KS24 , Sections 1.2 and 4] for further details). In particular, the corresponding spectral gaps coincide $$\begin{aligned} \textrm{gap}_\textrm{BEP}(G,\alpha )=\textrm{gap}_\textrm{SIP}(G,\alpha )\; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ Combining the findings in [ KS24 ] with our Theorems  2.2  and  2.4  on  \(\textrm{gap}_\textrm{SIP}(G,\alpha )\) , we readily deduce the following analogues of ( 1.3 ) and ( 1.6 ). 
                   Corollary 8.1 
                   For all graphs  \(G=(V,(c_{xy})_{x,y\in V})\)  and site weights  \(\alpha =(\alpha _x)_{x\in V}\) , we have $$\begin{aligned} \alpha _\textrm{min}\left\{ \frac{1}{21} \, \frac{ \alpha _\textrm{ratio}}{ 6^{\alpha _\textrm{min}/\alpha _\textrm{ratio}} } \, \frac{ c_\textrm{min}}{|V|^2\, \textrm{diam}(G) }\right\} \le \textrm{gap}_\textrm{BEP}(G,\alpha )\le \textrm{gap}_\textrm{RW}(G,\alpha )\; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ and, in some cases, we have $$\begin{aligned} \textrm{gap}_\textrm{BEP}(G,\alpha )< \textrm{gap}_\textrm{RW}(G,\alpha )\; \text {.}\end{aligned}$$ 
                 Interestingly, also our results on the spectrum of the non-conservative  \(\textrm{SIP}\) , via similar arguments, translate to the context of the  \(\textrm{BEP}\) . Indeed, the aforementioned Poisson intertwining is effective also when one adds the interaction with external reservoirs. As shown, e.g., in [ GRT25 , Section 4.1], using this operator, the generator  \(L_{G,\alpha ,\omega ,\theta }\)  in ( 6.1 ) of  \(\textrm{SIP}(G,\alpha ,\omega ,\theta )\)  satisfies, for all  \(\omega =(\omega _x)_{x\in V}\)  and  \(\theta =(\theta _x)_{x\in V}\) , an analogous intertwining relation with $$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {L}_{G,\alpha ,\omega ,\theta } = \mathcal {L}_{G,\alpha } + \sum _{x\in V}\omega _x\left\{ \left( \alpha _x\theta _x-\zeta _x\right) \partial _{\zeta _x} + \theta _x\zeta _x\,\partial _{\zeta _x}^2\right\} \; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ the generator of what we refer to as  \(\textrm{BEP}(G,\alpha ,\omega ,\theta )\) . When  \(\theta \equiv \varrho > 0\) , this non-conservative diffusion on  \([0,\infty )^V\)  is well known to have the following product measure  \(\gamma _{\alpha ,\varrho } = \otimes _{x\in V}\, \textrm{Gamma}(\alpha _x,\varrho )\)  as its unique reversible measure. Hence, arguing as above, we derive the corresponding spectral gap identity $$\begin{aligned} \textrm{gap}_\textrm{BEP}(G,\alpha ,\omega ,\varrho )=\textrm{gap}_\textrm{SIP}(G,\alpha ,\omega ,\varrho )\; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ and, as a consequence, the following analogue of Corollary  6.4 . 
                   Corollary 8.2 
                   For all graphs  \(G=(V,(c_{xy})_{x,y\in V})\) , and site weights  \(\alpha =(\alpha _x)_{x\in V}\) ,  \(\omega =(\omega _x)_{x\in V}\)  with  \(\omega \ne 0\) , we have $$\begin{aligned} \textrm{gap}_\textrm{BEP}(G,\alpha ,\omega ,\varrho ) = \textrm{gap}_\textrm{RW}(G,\alpha ,\omega )\; \text {,}\;\;\quad \varrho > 0\; \text {,}\;\;\end{aligned}$$ where  \(\textrm{gap}_\textrm{RW}(G,\alpha ,\omega )\)  is defined in ( 6.4 ). 
                 8.2  Other models As already anticipated in Sect.  1 ,  \(\textrm{SIP}\)  and  \(\textrm{BEP}\)  come with several closely related models, having a different dynamics, but exactly the same steady states. The models we have in mind fall into the class of mass redistribution models. Here, we shall focus only on those models with configuration-independent edge updates (see, e.g., [ Sas15 ] and references therein for configuration-dependent mass redistribution models with Dirichlet-type steady states): after an edge, say  xy , is selected with rate  \(c_{xy}\ge 0\) , particles/energies sitting on  x  and  y  get randomly redistributed therein. Among them, let us mention:  
                   
                     (a) 
                    
                       the Beta-Binomial splitting process and its continuous version, also known as Beta splitting process or (generalized) KMP model (see, e.g., [ PR26 ,  DMFG24 ,  GRT25 ]); 
                    
                   
                   
                     (b) 
                    
                       the discrete and continuous harmonic processes (see, e.g., [ FFG23 ,  GRT25 ]). 
                    
                   
                 All models in (a) and (b) have been studied in their conservative and non-conservative variants (see the aforementioned references for the precise definitions of the redistribution rules), and, just like  \(\textrm{SIP}\)  and  \(\textrm{BEP}\) , discrete and corresponding continuous models are isospectral (when reversible), enjoying completely analogous Poisson intertwining relations. To the best of our knowledge, the only sharp quantitative results on their convergence to equilibrium are confined to a handful of results, see [ CCL03 ,  Smi14 ,  CLL20 ,  PR26 ] and references therein. For what concerns the spectral gap, the only sharp results that we are aware of concern the Beta and Beta-Binomial splitting processes in two specific settings, both conservative:  
                   
                     (i) 
                    
                       the complete graph with  \(\alpha \equiv \mathrm{const.}\)  [ CCL03 ] (see also [ CLL20 , Remark 4]); 
                    
                   
                   
                     (ii) 
                    
                       the segment with  \(\alpha \equiv \mathrm{const.}\ge 1\)  [ CLL20 ]. 
                    
                   
                 In particular, in (i), the gap is attained by the system with  \(k=2\)  particles (which is strictly smaller than  \(\textrm{gap}_1\) ), while in (ii) it coincides with that of the corresponding single particle. In order to obtain spectral gap asymptotics for small diffusivity, the proof ideas of Theorems  2.3  and  2.4  apply also to these models (actually, with considerable simplifications due to the presence of edge updates only, and edges are a.s. never updated simultaneously). For instance, it is not difficult to show that, on graphs like that in Sect.  5.5  and for  \(\alpha \)  small enough, the spectral gap of the two particles must be strictly smaller than that of the single random walk. Similarly, the non-conservative (reversible) models satisfy a one-particle spectral gap identity, analogous to the one contained in Corollary  6.4 . Indeed, the key step in that proof (Sect.  7.1 ) carries over to these other models in contact with reservoirs, because of the analogous intertwining and consistency/duality relations available also for them, see, e.g., [ GRT25 ]. We leave the verification of these claims to the interested reader. What remains largely open is the problem of obtaining spectral gap’s sharp lower bounds—and, possibly, identities in terms of simpler processes—for the conservative variants of these mass redistribution models on general geometries.
